13The selected trajectory and observable normalization

13.1Choosing the nonlinear and stationary data together

The stationary construction, local expansion and scalar reset must use one set of paths and one scale schedule. We now describe the simultaneous choice and the way its identities are retained. The analytic inputs are the path-attachment estimates, their extended-domain convergence bounds, and the local nonlinear estimates proved in the preceding sections. This section concerns their common application; a numerical choice of large scales cannot replace an analytic estimate.

13.1.1The order of the choices

Prescribe the finite derivative orders, spatial powers and support exponents at every stage. They may tend to infinity. At a fixed stage \(j\), the adjacent target stage is either \(j\) or its immediate predecessor; thus every maximum below involves finitely many requirements. First choose the free-kernel bounds and block floors. The stationary construction gives a positive upper width \(\sigma_j\) and a lower path allowance \(A_j\). The startup expansion gives another lower path allowance. Choose the profile's path allowance above their maximum, and its width positive and at most \(\sigma_j\). Keep its direct coefficient below the purchased free-kernel bound. The profile construction retains the prescribed orders and support exponents. These choices precede the actual block lengths.

There are two levels of kernel bound: the free construction's bound and the bound required after replacing the terminal action by the Wilson action. Choose the latter from the former now, at the fixed profiles. Its validity is uniform over all later enlargements of the block floors. Consequently the local nonlinear estimates can request this final bound without choosing it from a horizon that has not yet been built.

Fix the startup and multiple-insertion requirements. Enlarge each block floor by the maximum of the startup floor, its scalar comparison floor and the multiple-insertion floor. Further enlarge it by the floors for the nonlinear geometry and the auxiliary local comparisons. The local targets have the same gauge chart, halo \(500\), earned exponent \(b_i + 3\), error exponent \(- 7 / 16\) and degree zero at target stage \(i\), where \(b_i\) is the support exponent of stage \(i\). These are the exponents requested of the output. The target's own support exponent is strictly larger than \(b_i + 3\) and its own rate is strictly below \(- 7 / 16\); the two margins are used in Subsection 13.4. The relevant comparison radius exceeds the halo by at least five. These are the targets used to prove the interval return, including the finite short-panel localization.

Choose any allowed block sequence above these floors. Only now select the wage bounds and local bound tables; their constants can depend on the actual block lengths. An arbitrary larger wage family is still allowed. Choose positive postal rates below any prescribed positive upper rates, together with their forecast bounds. The rate agreement needed by the scalar reset can be arranged explicitly. If \(\rho_{ji}\) is the selected postal rate, take the post-step forcing rate \(g_j\) no larger than

\(\displaystyle \min \{\rho_{jj} / 4, \rho_{j, j - 1} / 4\} .\)

At stage zero the repeated predecessor is harmless. Set the reset cost for the transition to \(i\) equal to four times the local credit constant at \(i\). Thus

\(\displaystyle g_j \leqslant \rho_{ji} / 4, \qquad c_i \leqslant q_{ji} / 4\) (13.1)

at every permitted transition. These are positive quantities. The post-step gap is supplied by the stationary estimate for this actual block sequence; take the auxiliary multiplier equal to one and the remaining post-step rate no larger than its stationary cap.

Next choose protection parameters below any prescribed positive upper bounds and below one. The local expansion records can then be selected with their smallness parameter at most \(1 / 4\). The order is significant: the interval return uses these records on every input curve, so their selection precedes all parameter intervals and finite periods.

Finally fix the insertion menus and auxiliary finite lists. Given any previous entry delays, enlarge them successively to pay the following already established estimates: the sector integrals and their active Gaussian prefactors; the marked and unmarked local bounds; the two forced absorptions; the small-field localization and parameter comparison; and the scalar reset and startup bounds. Impose both the fixed local reserve \(1 / 16\) and any requested positive smaller regular reserve \(\tau\), with forced reserve \(1 / 8\). The support and path conditions are identical in these two applications.

Here is a precise reason these successive enlargements are compatible. Each estimate supplies a stage-indexed lower threshold \(d^{(r)}_j\) and a conclusion valid whenever the entry coupling exceeds that threshold. Replace the current threshold at stage \(j\) by the finite maximum of it and all the \(d^{(r)}_j\) for that stage, and then by its running maximum up to \(j\). Shell lower bounds are treated the same way. The conclusions persist at every later delay. Requirements on a finite auxiliary list are imposed after that list is fixed, before the final delay is selected. This proves the quantifier order

\(\displaystyle \text{profiles} ; \text{block lengths} ; \text{rates and gaps} ; \text{protection} ; \text{menus and lists} ; \text{delays} ; \text{scale realization} .\)

The last realization is arbitrary among those paying these delays. In particular no threshold is selected from a later finite-horizon solution. Subsection 19.2 constructs a realization, with periods having arbitrarily late multiples of every integer. This is the period divisibility needed for the five auxiliary comparisons of the nonlinear return.

13.1.2Keeping the stationary and free data on that realization

Let \(a\) be one such realization. Build its free horizon \(M^0\) with the initial kernel bounds, and its retained horizon \(M^1\) with the next bounds. The retention identities keep their periodized covariance, row and tower data in the precise combinations used by the stationary construction. In particular the full-period lower bounds are supplied for \(M^1\), not for a separately selected quadratic form. Apply the stationary construction to \(M^1\) with the purchased gap and shell bounds. It supplies simultaneously, for every cutoff \(N\), paths \(R^1_{N, m}\), scalar corrections \(\alpha_{N, m}\) and stationary maps at every allowed period and forest. Its stronger version supplies the extended-domain estimates used in Subsection 14.21. The free convergence of Subsection 11.20 concerns these same retained towers.

Replace only the terminal normalization, using the revised horizon \(M\) and paths \(R_{N, m}\). The construction gives the exact identities

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} R_{N, N} & = R^{\text{Wilson}}_N, & R_{N, m} & = R^1_{N, m} \quad (m < N),\\ \alpha^{\text{revised}}_{N, m} & = \alpha_{N, m}, & \rho^{\text{revised}}_{N, m} & = \rho^1_{N, m} \quad (m + 1 < N) . \end{array}\)

The entire tower agrees with that of \(M^1\) when \(m + 1 < N\), and its transport matrix agrees already when \(m < N\). The new horizon satisfies the final nonlinear kernel bound chosen before the block lengths. At the terminal level its canonical direct coefficient is at most one, which gives the required Wilson positivity bound. At lower levels the retained all-period bounds apply.

For any fixed \(m\), all the displayed identities hold on the tail \(N > m + 1\). The limits and eventual larger-width bounds in Subsection 14.21 are therefore preserved: remove finitely many terms from each sequence and use equality of the remaining terms. This argument applies to each stationary derivative as well as each path and tower coefficient. It also preserves the extended-domain statement, because the one-step data coincide on this tail. Thus the final nonlinear construction carries both the stationary convergence data and the free-horizon convergence data. The terminal normalization introduces no independently selected background.

13.1.3The same local return on every input interval

On this final horizon the sector construction supplies its exact Gaussian expression, including the active determinant, Haar factors, sieve and coupling prefactor. The local bound tables selected above apply to this expression. The parameter comparison uses a single reference dictionary and the identity identification of its elementary labels, as in Subsection 12.9. The auxiliary locality estimates are retained when the delays are increased: they are stated beyond their entry thresholds, with all block lengths, rates and local dictionaries fixed.

Consequently, for every cutoff, every adjacent pair of levels and period, and every allowed continuous input curve on a compact interval, that section supplies the exact return in one dictionary, with early regular cost

\(\displaystyle H = c_i \delta_E (\hat{\beta}_m)\)

and the forecast forced cost. Both the \(1 / 16\) and the \(\tau\) reserve estimates refer to this same constructed horizon and local bound data. The selected tree and sector choice can depend on the interval and period, as permitted by the scalar reset, but are fixed throughout that interval.

We check the remaining recipe conditions rather than assuming that they follow from the density identity. Write

\(\displaystyle \delta = \delta_E (\hat{\beta}_m) / 32, \qquad \gamma = \exp \{-\mathfrak{k}(\rho_{ji}, \hat{\beta}_{m + 1}, \beta)\}\)

for the actual regular and forced bounds after reorganization, where \(\mathfrak{k}\) is the purchased postal decay exponent and \(\beta\) is the incoming coupling. We verify the comparison with the recipe bounds explicitly. Put \(x = \hat{\beta}_m\), \(y = x + c = \hat{\beta}_{m + 1}\), where \(c = s_G \log L \geqslant 0\), and write \(g (\beta) = \beta^{- 1 / 2}\) and \(t (x) = g (x) P (x)\). Let \(C \geqslant 1\) be the exchange constant and let \(H_{\text{post}} \geqslant 1\), \(D \in \mathbb{N}\), \(\rho > 0\) be the selected postal constants. With \(\ell_{\theta} (y) = P (y)^{\theta}\), define

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} M (x, y) & = H_{\text{post}} (1 + B (x) + P (y) + 2 \ell_{\theta} (y))^D,\\ R (x, y, \beta) & = M (x, y) [g (\beta)^3 + g (\beta)^{- D} (e^{- \rho B (x)} + e^{- \rho P (y)^2 / (1 + B (x))^D})],\\ T (x, y, \beta) & = C \left[ \left( \frac{C}{L} + \frac{1}{16 C} \right) \delta_E (y) + R (x, y, \beta) \right] . \end{array}\)

All terms in \(M\) and \(R\) are nonnegative. The recipe's portion and charges are precisely

\(\displaystyle \text{portion} = T, \qquad \text{charge}_E = T + M (x, y) t (x)^3, \qquad \text{charge}_K = H_{\text{post}} e^{-\mathfrak{k}}, \quad \mathfrak{k}= \rho \left( \frac{t (y)}{g (\beta)} \right)^2 .\)

In particular \(T \geqslant \delta_E (y) / 16\). Since \(c\) is fixed after \(L\) is chosen, the elementary shifted-gap comparison gives, for all sufficiently large \(x\), \(\delta_E (x) \leqslant 2 \delta_E (y)\). The reset constants of Subsection 12.10 have the form

\(\displaystyle c_i = \frac{1}{64 (1 + B_i^{\text{reset}})}, \qquad B_i^{\text{reset}} \geqslant 0, \qquad B_i^{\text{reset}} c_i \leqslant \frac{1}{64}, \qquad c_i \leqslant \frac{1}{64} .\)

Here \(B_i^{\text{reset}}\) is the coefficient of \(H\) in the regular reset bound; write \(v_i \geqslant 0\) for the coefficient of \(| \alpha | t (x)^3\) there. It follows that

\(\displaystyle H = c_i \delta_E (x) \leqslant \frac{\delta_E (y)}{32} \leqslant T, \qquad \delta = \frac{\delta_E (x)}{32} \leqslant \frac{\delta_E (y)}{16} \leqslant T \leqslant \text{charge}_E .\)

Moreover the uniform stage bound \(| \alpha | \leqslant A_j\) and the delayed cubic estimate \(64 v_i A_j t (x)^3 \leqslant \delta_E (x)\) give

\(\displaystyle \text{regularNeed} = B_i^{\text{reset}} H + v_i | \alpha | t (x)^3 \leqslant \frac{\delta_E (x)}{64} + \frac{\delta_E (x)}{64} = \delta .\)

Thus the actual local cost is paid, as well as the recipe's larger allowance. The soft reset bound is at most a stage constant times \((1 + B (x))^4 t (x)\); the late estimate for this expression in the reset section bounds it by the forecast's mild reserve. Its remaining hard reserve is exactly the purchased forecast comparison at this incoming band. These two reserves are retained in the forced absorption of the local return.

Since \(H_{\text{post}} \geqslant 1\), \(\gamma = e^{-\mathfrak{k}} \leqslant \text{charge}_K\). This proves

\(\displaystyle H \leqslant \text{portion}, \qquad \delta \leqslant \text{charge}_E, \qquad \gamma \leqslant \text{charge}_K .\) (13.2)

The incoming band lies in \([y / 4, 4 y]\), so \(g (y) / 2 \leqslant g (\beta) \leqslant 2 g (y)\). Hence \(\mathfrak{k} \geqslant \rho P (y)^2 / 4 \geqslant g_j P (y)^2\), using (13.1). Since \(P (x + c) / P (x) \to 1\) and \(P (x) \to \infty\), eventually \(g_j P (x + c)^2 \geqslant P (x)^{1 / 5}\). The coarse forced allowance is \(e^{- P (x)^{1 / 5}}\), so

\(\displaystyle \gamma \leqslant e^{- g_j P (y)^2} \leqslant e^{- P (x)^{1 / 5}} .\) (13.3)

The coarse regular allowance dominates \(\delta\) by its factor \(32\). Thus the output satisfies the costs of the next call as well as those of the recipe batch.

For clarity, the corresponding implication on the actual arrays is purely monotone. If \(u_i\) and \(v_i\) are their nonnegative regular and forced majorants at costs \(\delta, \gamma\), the same majorants work at the larger charges in (13.2); no supports, functions or labels change. Subsection 12.10 gives the exact normalized density identity for the same batch and \(t^2 | \Delta | \leqslant H \leqslant \text{portion}\). The permission predicate used in this construction imposes no additional batch restriction. Together these facts are exactly the batch relation of the chosen recipe. Appending this batch to the incoming finite history supplies the retained-history predicate at level \(m\) and the next-step predicate. This proves those predicates for the output that was actually constructed.

The terminal startup construction, treated separately below, must supply one continuous family on the full terminal band. Its regular and forced bounds, common summable majorants and invariant jets survive the fixed packing into the terminal dictionary. Its exact Wilson density starts the retained history. This is the required initial-family assertion even when \(N = 0\).

Once that startup assertion is proved, we can apply the actual scalar reset at every input interval and then Subsection 12.13. The result is a finite history at every cutoff and period, with the prescribed level-zero coupling, the exact transferred densities and the same selected paths \(R_{N, m}\). Its per-edge coupling error is the purchased reset width, and its forced estimate includes (13.3). No assertion of uniqueness of these shooting histories is required. Their subsequent convergence and the additional observable comparisons use the retained common data and the estimates of their respective sections.

13.2Attaching the funded service to the selected history

The finite-volume service is useful only if its data belong to the history selected for the public theorem. We verify that binding here. The public construction selects one run and applies the funded continuation to that run's own history, schedule, transitions, and finite-volume data. No cutoff, period, or extraction order is used to select a second history. The common schedule and its numerical bounds are organized in Subsection 13.1; here we check the remaining same-witness interface.

Let \(R\) denote the selected run. Write \(Y_R\) for its stored finite renormalization-group history and \(\mathcal{P}_R\) for the separately stored scale marked lifting. The distinction matters: the history used below is \(\text{trail} (Y_R)\), whereas the marked lifting supplies the scale data. Let \(t_R (d)\) be the reference level at grade \(d\), let \(S_R (d, N, p)\) be the transfer datum, and let \(D_R (N, p, m)\) be the selected transition record. We write

\(\displaystyle C_R = (\text{trail} (Y_R), t_R, S_R)\)

for the family with its fixed holonomy, label frames, and local sections. This notation names the family already stored in the run; it does not define a new history.

Proposition 13.1. (Same-history attachment) Suppose the selected run \(R\) carries its funded continuation certificate. Applying that certificate to the data stored in \(R\) gives a package on the same family \(C_R\) with the following properties:

  1. the reference-level condition

    \(\displaystyle d + 3 \leqslant J (t_R (d)) \qquad (d \geqslant 0) ;\)

  2. one nesting family, one scale schedule, one transition family, and one transfer family, such that the selected transition at \((N, p, m)\) is available for \(m < N\), and, for every \(d, N, p\) with \(t_R (d) \leqslant N\), the finite-volume transfer identity holds using those same data;

  3. the funded nonlinear-response and adjacent-scale estimates, together with the selected service on exactly \(C_R\);

  4. the centered service, including its height estimates and terminal forced-profile relation, as well as the finite-volume bounds and marked family of fixed marked sections conclusions recorded for \(C_R\).

The first three items and the finite-volume identities hold simultaneously for all cutoffs and periods in their stated domains. For each fixed grade and section in the purchased family of fixed marked sections, the terminal marked estimate is conditional on the reserved addresses and fee, the local forced-family attachment, and the paid selected-history record used in Propositions 13.17 and 13.19. Under these conditions on the same \(C_R\), it holds for all sufficiently large cutoffs, uniformly in the period; its threshold may depend on that grade and section. It is a separate local estimate, not a field included implicitly in the centered service.

Proof. We first identify the history-bearing data. The selected run stores \(Y_R\), the schedule \(t_R\), a transition family, the gradewise regularity data, and the finite-volume transfer witnesses. Its underlying family is

\(\displaystyle C_R = (\text{trail} (Y_R), t_R, S_R),\)

with the fixed holonomy, label frames, and local-section map. The funded continuation is a function of precisely these stored objects: the configuration and its nesting data, the schedule and its opening inequalities, the transfer family and transition records, and the regularity and finite-volume transfer witnesses. Its output therefore has the family formed from those same arguments. That family is \(C_R\) itself; there is no intervening identification map and no reselected label system.

Here is why the quantifiers in that application are uniform. The public selection fixes the history \(Y_R\) and a nesting family for it. Independently, it chooses the reference schedule on that selected run so that \(d + 3 \leqslant J (t_R (d))\) for each grade. On this fixed history it constructs the transition branches and their records once, and then defines one transfer family on all triples \((d, N, p)\). That family has the required regularity properties; for every \(d, N, p\) with \(t_R (d) \leqslant N\), its finite-volume witness is built from the already chosen nesting family and transition records. The witness at \((d, N, p)\) may depend on those indices, but the history, nesting family, schedule, transitions, and transfer family do not. Thus the simultaneous quantifiers are part of the selected run and are not obtained by diagonal extraction or by making new global choices after fixing \((N, p)\).

For the transfer witnesses, if \(t_R (d) > N\) the construction uses a default map. If \(t_R (d) \leqslant N\), it composes along the selected transitions and obtains the finite-volume identity and accounting estimate. This explains the cutoff in item 2 and shows that both cases use the original \(Y_R\).

The funded continuation supplies two quantitative inputs to the centered construction: bounds on the delayed nonlinear response and its tariff, and uniform estimates for each allowed adjacent-scale step. The latter are the scale inequalities proved in Subsection 4.6. That section applies them to the selected adjacent levels and block size, and supplies a common monotone threshold. It does not change the selected history, nor does it by itself establish the nonlinear-response bounds.

The centered service is assembled from these data as follows. The escape estimate uses the same nesting family, nonlinear-response bounds, and all selected transitions. Its room and bank estimates use the budget fields of the selected factor. The adjacent-scale inequalities give the required scale comparison at each selected step. For heights at grade \(d\) and reference level \(k\), the local residence estimate at \(k\) gives the later scale bounds, the one-cover footprint estimate, and a uniform camera bound. The five numerical implications used in this conversion are proved in Proposition 4.14; the eventual estimates and finite-prefix maximum are in Theorem 4.13. The maximum is over the finitely many grades below each target stage and its two adjacent stages, so it gives a common threshold for all later uses at that stage.

At the terminal endpoint, the finite-volume transfer witness gives the top transfer identity and its forced-profile relation. The centered service's terminal estimate is this same relation, using the domination and rise identities attached to the selected run. At an intermediate scale, the transfer witnesses and selected transitions feed the local transition estimate: specializing the tariff inequality to the zero-grade section gives the required relay bounds. The separate growth and jump estimates follow from positivity and the selected scale payment. The three-card coefficient, exponential-entry, and forced-profile identities are proved in Subsection 10.10; we use those identities here rather than constructing a second interpolation or repeating their bound proof.

The funded continuation returns the centered service together with its finite-volume bounds and family of fixed marked sections. Its local transition estimates use the same nonlinear-response bounds, adjacent-scale estimates, nesting family, transition records, finite-volume witnesses, and schedule just described. Both the core centered-service assembly and its stronger funded version use the supplied configuration, schedule, transfer data, and transition family. Neither creates a second history.

Finally, the terminal marked-field estimate is attached only after \(C_R\) has been fixed. The reserved addresses of the terminal representation are not constructed there: they are a property of the selected history, recorded for every cutoff and period in the nesting receipt stored in the run. This receipt is an input of the present section; it originates in the selection of the history from the exact startup family, and no result of this group of sections proves it. Given the reserved addresses and the lawful terminal representation, Proposition 13.15 constructs the terminal marked coefficients on this same family, with their density and forced-profile identities, provided its fee inequality holds; that inequality is the terminal fee purchased in Subsection 13.4. The quantitative local bounds are proved in Subsection 13.8, and their eventual uniformity for each fixed grade and section in Proposition 13.14. The local estimate requires soundness, reference-level eligibility, fit of the union of the stencils, nonnegative credit, a keeper, the regional card-times-allowance bound, cover at most \(81\), the square-root allowance-times-storm bound, and the stated positive terminal weight and nonnegative reserve. The local estimates supply the numerical bounds for all sufficiently large cutoffs for each fixed grade and section in the purchased family of fixed marked sections, uniformly in the period, subject to the stated schedule and matching inputs. In addition, the local forced-family attachment must be supplied for each relevant correction, on the terminal representation of this same \(C_R\), uniformly in the period. The root-test conclusion also uses the paid selected-history record. Given that record and the terminal attachment, paragraph 13.9.3 constructs the full served residual polynomial. Thus no separate residual-representation hypothesis is required. The marked density and forced-profile identity then give the terminal estimate on this same \(C_R\). Throughout, its history, schedule, transfer family, and terminal representation remain those of \(C_R\). No uniqueness assertion for a terminal field is needed.\(\Box\)

Remark 13.2. (Scope of the attachment) The proposition proves the common-trajectory attachment once the selected run's funded continuation certificate is available. The common funded-history construction is responsible for producing that certificate and its quantitative bounds; this section does not replace those purchases by the name of a record. It also does not use the separate grantor route as evidence for the public caller. The public construction applies its funded continuation to the data stored in the selected run.

Two points of bookkeeping are needed by later sections. First, the certificate is a universally quantified statement: it applies to every history of the same alloy with a nesting receipt, every schedule obeying the reference-level condition, and all transfer and transition families satisfying the making, climbing and transfer conditions. The run stores this certificate, and the public run stores in addition a stronger form with further conclusions; both are specialized to the same nine stored objects, namely the history, its nesting receipt, the schedule and its reference-level condition, the transfer family, the transition family, and the making, climbing and transfer receipts. Second, the certificate asserts the existence of the family of fixed marked sections, that is, of one local section and one marked family for each grade together with the gradewise coefficient maps and the presentation entering the terminal marked estimate; it does not name them. A later argument must fix one such witness once, before any cutoff, period or extraction order is chosen, and use it throughout. Reopening the existence statement at a later point may produce a different family of fixed marked sections and is not a same-object argument.

13.2.1Budget and domain of the stored service

Write \(W (j)\) for the worth fixed in the purchase, \(c_j\) for the column count of stage \(j\), and \(\ell_j\) for its chosen block side. At level \(i\) put \(C_i = c_{J (i)}\) and \(L_i = \ell_{J (i + 1)}\). Thus \(C_i\) is the column count denoted by \(\Gamma_i\) in Theorem 4.15; it is unrelated to the running-coupling increment. Notice that the level-\(i\) block is chosen at the entrance stage \(J (i + 1)\).

Proposition 13.3. (Room and bank on the purchased levels) The stage purchase can be made so that every allowed schedule has, at every level \(i \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle 64 W (J (i) + 1)^2 \leqslant C_i, \qquad 100000 W (J (i + 1) + 1)^6 (1 + C_{i + 1})^2 \leqslant L_i^{1 / 8} .\)

In particular the same inequalities hold for the selected run, independently of grade, cutoff and period.

Proof. After fixing worth and the orders, choose the stage column count as a running maximum of the room request \(64 W (j + 1)^2\), the factorial of the stage derivative order and the other pre-length column requests. An integer count can be used by taking ceilings. In particular the profile already pays the room request before any length is chosen. With these columns fixed, let

\(\displaystyle A_j = 100000 W (j + 1)^6 (1 + c_j)^2, \qquad n_j = \max \{1, \lceil A_j^8 \rceil\} .\)

Include \(n_j\) in the stage length floor before selecting \(\ell_j\). This is compatible with all other lower floors: take their maximum and, if nondecreasing floors are required, take the running maximum of that maximum. Any subsequent increase preserves the two purchases. Since \(\ell_j \geqslant n_j\) and the eighth root is increasing on the nonnegative reals, \(A_j \leqslant \ell_j^{1 / 8}\). Substitute \(j = J (i)\) in the column inequality and \(j = J (i + 1)\) in the length inequality. The same substitutions apply at \(i = 0\). The later entrance delays and the selection of finite-volume histories do not change the stage columns or these lengths.\(\Box\)

For the common C02 and C12 interface use the direct specialization of the funded continuation in Proposition 13.1, with family \(C_R\), reference map \(t_R\), transfer data \(S_R\) and interfaces \(D_R\). The stronger funded service is on precisely these objects. Its centered part restricts to each reference level \(k\) by retaining the transition receipts with \(k \leqslant i < N\) and the terminal receipt at \(N\). The stock clause for grade \(d\) is needed only for \(t_R (d) \leqslant k \leqslant N\), and the music clause for \(t_R (d) \leqslant N\) with interfaces \(t_R (d) \leqslant i < N\). The restriction makes sense even when \(k = N\), with no intervening interface. The centered birth estimate uses the stricter domain \(k < N\). All period and cutoff indices range over entries of this one run. The local analytic attachments needed for retention, music, the paid local transfer record and the uniform birth estimate are separate from this projection and the numerical budget proposition.

There is also a marked lifting construction with its own chosen finite-volume history, transfers and interfaces. In the stamped original marked lifting the history is \(Y_R\) and the transfers are \(S_R\), so forming its family gives \(C_R\) by substitution. The marked lifting service uses the recorded gate of its marked lifting step for \(i \geqslant 1\) and its \(k\)-restricted retention is asserted for \(k \geqslant 1\). At \(i = 0\) its interface is a default. The common interface above uses \(D_R\) directly, including at level zero, and needs no identification of marked lifting gates with these interfaces.

For this direct interface put \(x_i = \hat{\beta}_i\) and \(r_i = N_{J (i + 1)}\), where \(N_j\) is the stage occupancy bound. The marched weight multiplier is \(\phi_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 1}\) and the probe bound is \(\delta_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 2}\); both use the fine coupling. The grade height is \(\zeta_{d, i} = x_i^{- (\deg F_d + 4)}\) and the response transfer bound \(\psi_i\) is twice the title cost at level \(i + 1\) multiplied by the exponential of that level's support exponent. The terminal-bank reserve is \(u_i = x_i^{- 1 / 2} / 4\). In particular it is neither the marched multiplier nor the adjacent transfer record rate. Proposition 13.20 gives the separate terminal endowment from the paid delay at \(J (N + 1)\): for \(C_N^{\text{end}} = w_N^{\text{f}} + r_N \textsf{s} (B_N) u_N\), the bounds are \((2 B_N + 1)^4 C_N^{\text{end}} \leqslant 3\) and \(e^{C_N^{\text{end}}} - 1 \leqslant 1\), where \(w_N^{\text{f}}\) is the forced allowance. These numerical bounds require no interface beyond the cutoff.

13.3Binding the selected local course

The public construction returns a run together with a continuation on its selected history. Specializing that continuation to the run's own bolt, schedule, transfer family and interfaces yields a binding on the very family used by the run. This section proves that same-family assertion and records the monotonicity needed when comparing paid schedules. The global schedule and same-history identities are those of Subsection 13.1 and Proposition 13.1.

Write \(R\) for the run selected by the public construction and put

\(\displaystyle C_R = (\text{trail} (T_R), t_R, S_R), \qquad A_R =\mathcal{L} (C_R),\)

where \(T_R\) is its bolt, \(t_R\) its schedule, \(S_R\) its transfer family, and \(\mathcal{L} (C)\) denotes the directed level schedule of a course \(C\). The family \(C_R\) is exactly the family stored in \(R\), with its fixed holonomy, label frames and local sections.

A binding for a family \(C\) is the following comparison record on the level schedule \(\mathcal{L} (C)\) of that same family. It consists of a horizon \(M\) on \(\mathcal{L} (C)\), a bound map \(K\) and a share function \(\ell (N, i)\), with five properties. First, the shape-port identities hold. To state them, let \(V_{N, i}\) be the kernel history at level \(i\) obtained by descending from the constant top kernel history at the cutoff \(N\), and for \(i < N\) let \(D_{N, i}\) be the blocking step at level \(i\) whose input is \(V_{N, i + 1}\) and whose output is \(V_{N, i}\), as constructed in Subsection 3.9. For \(i \leqslant N\), the diagonal and dual-diagonal rows of the bed \(M_{N, i}\) are the pulse and dual pulse of \(V_{N, i}\) at the kernel penalty of the schedule, and its metric and inverse rows are the amended precision and amended inverse of \(V_{N, i}\) at that penalty with share \(\ell (N, i)\). For \(i < N\), the \(A_0\) and \(T\) rows of the tower at \((N, i)\) are the primal and two-form transports donated by \(D_{N, i}\) at that penalty. These identities fix four of the five rows of each bed; they do not determine its root row, which is constrained only by the second and fifth properties. Second, the horizon satisfies the horizon contract of Subsection 3.9 for the bound map \(K\). Third, for all \(i \leqslant N\) and every period \(p\), the quadratic form of the bed \(M_{N, i}\) on the torus of level \(i\) and period \(p\) is invariant under the signed coordinate changes. Fourth, every level step \((i, p)\) has the room required by the comparison: its patch satisfies the branch condition of the kernel, one hundred times its macroscopic radius is at most the side of the finer torus, and the coarser side exceeds one. Fifth, for all \(i \leqslant N\) and \(p\), the path of the representative of \(C\) at \((N, p, i)\) is attached to the bed \(M_{N, i}\) in the sense of Subsubsection 3.9.3. In particular a binding refers to the paths of \(C\) itself.

Proposition 13.4. (Binding on the selected family) The continuation stored in \(R\), specialized to its own bolt, schedule, transfers and interfaces, supplies a binding \(B_R\) for \(C_R\). Its horizon is the horizon of the directed alloy stored in \(R\), and its bound map is the selected run's bound map.

Proof. The continuation is universally applicable to a bolt, its nested factor, a schedule, transfer data and interfaces, subject to the making, climbing and freight conditions. The run stores these very objects and receipts: its tuning supplies nesting, its acceptance data supply the openings, its making data define the family, and its carrying and freight records supply the remaining steps. Specializing all arguments to those stored objects gives the family \(C_R\) by its definition. The resulting calibrated package contains a binding \(B_R\) on \(C_R\), with horizon equal to the alloy's horizon and bound map equal to the selected run's bound map. The binding is constructed from the shape-port, attached path and shell comparison for that same alloy and bolt, so no separately selected family is involved. The level schedule of the trail of a bolt is by construction the level schedule of its directed alloy, and the assembly of the public run records this identity for \(C_R\); therefore the schedule used by the binding is \(A_R\), and the statement that the two horizons agree is meaningful.\(\Box\)

We record the exact elementary monotonicity for the paid-schedule condition. For waits \(U, V\) over the same bar, write \(U \preceq V\) when their requests obey, pointwise in stage \(j\) and scale \(s\),

\(\displaystyle r_U (j) \leqslant r_V (j), \qquad w_U (j, s) \leqslant w_V (j, s), \qquad p_U \leqslant p_V .\)

Here \(r\) is the ready-time request, \(w\) the window request, and \(p\) the minimum-period request. A schedule \(A\) has stage map \(J_A\), scale values \(s_A (k)\) and period values \(P_A (i)\). Write \(\textsf{Pay} (U, A)\) when the fixed kernel, bar, stage, initial-stage, length and protection identities and the entry, scale and volume inequalities hold, together with the following two conditions:

  1. the separate period condition \(p_U \leqslant P_A (i)\) for every \(i\);

  2. for every \(j\) from the required initial stage onward, there is a \(k\) such that

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} r_U (j) \leqslant s_A (k), \qquad w_U (j, s_A (k)) \leqslant k,\\ J_A (i) = j \quad (k - w_U (j, s_A (k)) \leqslant i \leqslant k + w_U (j, s_A (k))) . \hspace{3cm} \text{(13.4)} \end{array}\)

The lower endpoint in this interval uses truncated natural subtraction. These are the schedule conditions used in Subsection 19.2.

Lemma 13.5. (Weakening the paid-schedule requests) If \(U \preceq V\) and \(\textsf{Pay} (V, A)\), then \(\textsf{Pay} (U, A)\).

Proof. The fixed identities and entry, scale and volume inequalities do not depend on the wait. The separate period condition for \(U\) follows for every \(i\) from \(p_U \leqslant p_V \leqslant P_A (i)\). Fix a stage \(j\) and take the same \(k\) supplied by the plateau condition for \(V\). Its ready-time inequality implies \(r_U (j) \leqslant r_V (j) \leqslant s_A (k)\). Set \(a = w_U (j, s_A (k))\) and \(b = w_V (j, s_A (k))\). Then \(a \leqslant b\), and truncated subtraction gives

\(\displaystyle \max \{0, k - b\} \leqslant \max \{0, k - a\}, \qquad k + a \leqslant k + b.\)

Hence the interval of indices on which \(J_A (i) = j\) for \(V\) contains the interval required for \(U\). Also \(a \leqslant b \leqslant k\), so the window bound for \(U\) holds. Thus the same index \(k\) verifies the entire plateau condition for \(U\); this proves \(\textsf{Pay} (U, A)\).\(\Box\)

These results identify the binding family and compare paid-schedule requirements. They do not themselves supply the subsequent local-service estimates.

13.4Observable estimates on a common RG trajectory

The nonlinear return and the observable estimates have to be available before a finite-volume history is selected. We now make the additional choices in that order. The resulting statements are uniform over every allowed representation on the purchased horizon. In particular, enlarging the finite volume does not require a new choice of block lengths.

Retain the profiles, horizons and targets of Subsection 13.1. At a transition from stage \(j\) to stage \(i\), write \(b_i\) for the outgoing support exponent, \(e_{ji}\) for the support exponent of the local target itself, and \(q_{ji}\) for the rate of its input allowance. These are not the earned exponent \(b_i + 3\) and the error exponent \(- 7 / 16\) requested of the output in Subsection 13.1: each target was purchased with two positive support margins above its earned exponent and with a rate strictly below its error exponent. The purchases therefore give

\(\displaystyle e_{ji} > b_i + 3, \qquad q_{ji} \leqslant - 7 / 16.\)

These are two different reserves: the first pays support enlargement, whereas the second gives decay with the coupling. Fix a nondecreasing integer occupancy schedule \(N_j \geqslant 1\). At each stage we need only the two possible target stages and the finitely many grades through \(j\).

13.4.1The broad parameter estimate and its localized bound

Put \(y = x + s_G \log L\), \(B = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil\) and \(r = y^{1 / 256}\). Apply Subsection 12.3 with outgoing exponent \(e = b_i + 3\), the actual common support dictionary, and the fixed derivative order and spatial power at stage \(i\). Its positive support reserve is \((e_{ji} - e) / 4\). The input allowance has upper exponent at most \(- 7 / 16\), so the integrated constituent bound has upper exponent \(- 2 / 5\) and the rooted derivative bound is eventually at most \(y^{- 3 / 8}\). For each finite real test \(\lambda\) on the parameter interval, its ordinary logarithmic family \(F\) therefore satisfies

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{N}_e \left( \int F (t) \hspace{0.17em} d \lambda (t) \right) \leqslant y^{- 3 / 8} M_{r / 2} (\lambda) .\)

Here \(\mathcal{N}_e\) is the rooted derivative budget at support exponent \(e\), and \(M_{r / 2}\) is the moment-test norm of Subsection 12.6. The forced family has one common absolute coefficient majorant at radius \(r / 2\), with the compulsory warning gain retained. The construction proves this for all compatible input representations and all real parameter lines; the majorant is not selected after the finite test.

The hypotheses on the real line deserve mention. Its coupling is the same quadratic line used in Subsection 12.2. Its activity coordinates are affine, hence of degree at most two. The path, cutoff, charts and input dictionaries are fixed on the line. The enlarged real interval remains in the purchased band, by the radius choice there. The compensated derivative and packet-action estimates are supplied by Subsection 9.10. Thus all the local hypotheses of the sector estimate hold on these actual lines, including their held fine fields and their cutoffs.

We check that the finite localization does not destroy the useful power. Let \(C_i\) be the signed-pullback derivative cost and define

\(\displaystyle w (x) = C_i \{y^{- 3 / 8} + 4 y^{- 7 / 16} \} .\)

This has upper exponent \(- 3 / 8\). The five auxiliary tori in Subsection 12.4 have sides bounded by a fixed multiple of \(1 + B\). Their numbers of sites, local ball counts, and weighted changes of anchor are fixed powers of \(1 + B\). The interpolation weights are fixed. For example, a contribution from auxiliary torus \(\Lambda_{\nu}\) has bound at most \(2 | \Lambda_{\nu} | w (x)\); its weighted change of anchor costs at most \(2^k (1 + cB)^{pk}\) at derivative order \(k\) and spatial power \(p\). The local allocation costs at most another fixed ball count and \(\exp ((b_i + 2) C_{\text{cover}})\), with \(C_{\text{cover}}\) fixed. Consequently both the localized raw derivative bound and its ordinary rooted bound have upper exponent \(- 3 / 8\). The latter is eventually at most \(y^{- 1 / 4}\), and the former at most \(y^{- 1 / 8}\). These comparisons use strict exponent margins and are uniform over the period and input representation.

The forced bound survives the same localization with its forecast normalization. The enlarged spatial factors have upper exponent zero. The compulsory Gaussian warning is \(\exp (- \mu P (y)^2 / c)\) with \(\mu > 0\) and fixed \(c > 0\); the permitted forecast spends a strictly smaller part of that exponent. The ratio of the spent bound to the forecast tends to zero faster than any fixed power of \(y\), as in Subsection 19.1. Thus the ordinary bound \(y^{- 1 / 8}\) and the soft correction can be paid simultaneously in the forced absorption inequalities. This uses the chosen positive forecast rate below its permitted fraction of \(\mu\), already selected before the delays. More precisely, the forecast hard rate is at most \(\mu / 16\). Its soft allowance is twice the zero-order compulsory activity bound, \(2 A_{\text{comp}} (x)\), and the resizing estimate is applied at fraction \(1 / 16\) before the final absorption. This keeps both the factor two and the stated rate reserve. No new rate is selected for an individual input.

13.4.2The fixed parameter radius and all derivatives

Choose a fixed \(q \geqslant 4\) and a desired normalized output allowance \(0 < s \leqslant 1 / 256\). The finite interpolation in Subsection 12.6, with degree \(300\), has a constant \(U = U (300, q)\). Choose the small response parameter \(t > 0\) so that \(6 Ut \leqslant s\). This choice precedes the nonlinear purchases; Subsection 13.1 permits any positive such \(t\). The pointwise localization estimate is

\(\displaystyle 3 tC_{\text{st}} x^{- 11 / 10} + R (x), \qquad R \text{ has upper exponent } - 23 / 20.\)

Combining it with the just-proved broad bound gives the fixed-radius bound \(sC_{\text{st}} x^{- 11 / 10}\). Indeed the discarded parameter tail is at most

\(\displaystyle (1 + U) (2 q)^{301} y^{- 365 / 256},\)

which is \(o (x^{- 11 / 10})\), as is \(UR (x)\). The linear reset and the common forced coefficient majorant give the two normalized output bounds at radius \(q / 2\) from that section. The scalar projection has bound \(sC_{\text{st}} x^{- 11 / 10} / \theta (x)^2\). Thus the finite-test hypotheses of Subsection 12.7 are available for the same return as its exact density identity.

The differentiated output also requires one common local collection of constants before an input is supplied. This follows by making the choices in Sections 8.28 and 9.10 at the largest requested order \(k\). For the chart derivatives use a coarse radius

\(\displaystyle q_k (x) = Z_k \hspace{0.17em} 2 \theta_f (x), \qquad v (x) = (k + 1) q_k (x),\)

where \(Z_k > 0\) is the fixed angular chart constant and \(\theta_f > 0\) is the fine fluctuation threshold. Then \(v (x) > 0\), \(kq_k (x) \leqslant v (x)\), and both radii have upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\). The comparison estimates allow any such radius. Fix the cutoff convention before this collection of constants is chosen. The first integrated polymer is the same unmasked local function used in Subsection 8.28. On a healthy support its mask is identically one in a neighborhood, so its ordered derivatives are the derivatives of that unmasked function. The angular-to-chart conversion at radius \(q_k\) uses \(kq_k \leqslant v\) and supplies all orders through \(k\). This identifies the pre-mask derivative estimate as well as the masked activity; the former is required by later differentiated insertions. The Gaussian normalization estimates apply to every read subset with the same row bounds, including the empty subset. The enforcement and warning derivatives have respectively the bounds proportional to the cover number and to one plus the warning-support cover number; these are exactly the cutoff terms retained in the differentiated activity construction.

Reserve a positive part of the target support exponent before summing the differentiated factors. At every order through \(k\), its exponential weight absorbs the polynomial cover and length factors. For completeness, choose a common positive support reserve \(\Delta = e_{ji} / 2\) at outgoing exponent zero. If \(A_k (x) \geqslant 0\) is the common integrated derivative constant of upper exponent zero and \(u_k (x) > 0\) the constituent allowance, its differentiated allowance is

\(\displaystyle s_k (x) = (1 + 2^k A_k (x)) u_k (x) .\)

It has the same negative upper exponent as \(u_k\). The connected-tree criterion \(4 (2 B + 1)^4 s_k / \Delta \leqslant 1 / 2\) therefore holds eventually. The ordinary and forced unweighted constituent allowances likewise become at most \(1 / 2\), and their tree criteria at reserve \(\Delta\) hold at the same threshold. Since \(0 + \Delta + \Delta = e_{ji}\), these are exactly the two reserves required in the renewed first and second expansions. The unstrengthened construction in Subsection 9.7 proves their majorants and all polynomial cover moments; we use it here at exponent zero, with the same \(\Delta\) in both expansions. Thus renewal does not introduce an unrelated set of constituent bounds.

The resulting connected activity majorants and nearby integrable domination are those of Sections 8.28, 8.26 and 9.9. Enlarge the finitely many constants to common nonnegative ones and intersect their large-coupling intervals. All those constants depend on the stage, block length, chart and purchased bounds, but not on the finite volume or input representation. The subsequent unweighted total sums may depend on the finite volume, as the continuity proof allows. This distinguishes the uniform local derivative estimates from the fixed-volume domination used to pass limits under sums.

13.4.3Insertion estimates and their common delays

The primary, regular and forced one-insertion estimates use the same local expansion. Their common heading and inverse bound were proved in Subsection 10.9. The primary calibration uses the extra derivative available when \(d + 3 \leqslant i\); the regular case uses its vanishing leading coefficient, and the forced case retains its square-root forced-allowance normalization. The auxiliary local integral and its comparison are those of Sections 9.3 and 9.4. The finite-support conditions are imposed by increasing the shell and entry bounds, after their fixed support radii have been selected.

For multiple insertions, the raised forced estimate and the ordinary estimate have distinct bounds. Use both, as in Subsection 3.16. The tariff has ordinary allowance \(y^{- 1}\), single-insertion allowance \(y^{- 2}\), and forced allowance \(2 e^{- \mu P (y)^2 / 16}\). The occupancy and projection inequalities of Subsection 9.2 apply with \(N = N_j\). In particular the local projection bound is at most \(e^{\sqrt{y}}\), while its two error bounds fit the fixed local splitting allowance. The required geometric floor is imposed before \(L\) is selected; the occupancy and error inequalities are imposed afterwards.

Here is the common choice explicitly. At stage \(j\) take the maximum of the pre-length floors for the finitely many allowed target stages, the primary comparisons, and the projected insertion geometry. Choose \(L_j\) above it. Having chosen wages, rates and protection, choose the primary records with smallness at most \(1 / 4\). For each allowed target stage, take the finitely many eventual thresholds for all grades through \(j\), the broad and fixed-radius parameter bounds, the differentiated output, the ordinary and raised menu bounds, and the projection inequalities. Include all previous thresholds. Replace their maximum at \(j\) by its running maximum up to \(j\). Enlarge the shell bound by the required fixed comparison radii and its growth lower bound by one.

Every conclusion used above is valid beyond its threshold, so all are preserved by any further enlargement. The quantifiers are therefore

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \text{profiles and block floors} ; \quad \text{block lengths} ;\\ \text{bounds and protection} ; \quad \text{delays} ;\\ \text{all compatible local inputs} . \end{array}\)

The scale realization and the selected finite-volume history come last. On that history each of the estimates applies to its actual held transition; the endpoint representation, its density identity, its singleton derivative and its marked profile all retain their original common dictionary. In particular the observable estimates do not choose another solution of the scalar shooting problem.

13.4.4Estimates for the terminal marked field

Three further requirements enter the same order of choices. They are consumed by Sections 13.7, 13.9 and 13.10, and they are recorded with the purchase, so that they hold on every schedule realized afterwards and in particular on the selected run.

The first is the terminal fee. For each grade \(d\) and each value of the support exponent there is a threshold with the following property: at every level \(m\) of every schedule whose profile at \(m\) has that support exponent and whose coupling \(\hat{\beta}_m\) exceeds the threshold, every torus side at level \(m\) is at least the minimal side of the local section of grade \(d\), and the fee of that section at its camera constant and at the forced allowance of level \(m\) is at most the square root of that allowance. This is the fee inequality of Proposition 13.15; the threshold is produced by the estimate at the end of Subsection 13.7. At stage \(j\) add the nonnegative parts of these thresholds over all grades and all profile indices through \(j\), and include the sum \(k_j\) in the entry delay of stage \(j\). Since the entry condition at level \(m\) is imposed at the stage \(J (m + 1) \geqslant J (m)\), every schedule paying the delays satisfies \(k_{J (m + 1)} \leqslant \hat{\beta}_m\), and therefore the fee inequality at every level \(m\) for every grade \(d \leqslant J (m)\). The fee concerns the local section of the family, with the family's label frames; it is fixed before the block lengths.

The second and third concern the family of fixed marked sections. Its sections and marked families are fixed first, before the profiles' bound maps, the block floors and all later choices. For this family of fixed marked sections, Subsection 13.6 produces a table of nonnegative credits and stage thresholds \(n_i\) on the block length, and an entrance delay. Include the thresholds \(n_i\) among the block floors of stage \(i\), together with the floors already listed; this is the comparison of thresholds with floor lengths which that section requires of its caller. Include its entrance delay among the entry delays, after the protection parameters and before the final delay is selected. By the argument given there, every schedule paying these delays then satisfies, at every transition from stage \(j\) to stage \(i\) and every coupling \(x\) beyond the entry delay of stage \(j\), the profile-cost bound, the credit bounds for all grades \(d \leqslant i\), and the tail inequality (13.5). The purchase records both facts together with the family of fixed marked sections: the credit table with thresholds not exceeding the selected block floors, and the delay condition for the selected delays. Neither requires a choice after the block lengths other than the enlargement of the entry delays.

These three requirements are finite maxima of thresholds at each stage and are stable under further enlargement, so they are compatible with the common choice described above.

This supplies the additional local purchases needed to apply the one-history transfer constructions. The passage from those local purchases to the complete centered service and the ultraviolet field family still requires their simultaneous global assembly.

13.5Producing and symmetrizing the marked lifting

This section supplies the marked lifting used by the fixed-stencil construction. It has two parts. First, the equation ideal is read by microscopic link observables whose Taylor errors lie strictly above the requested grade. Second, finite-dimensional linear algebra combines these reads into one marked lifting, and finite group averages impose the signed and real symmetries. The filtration throughout counts factors that are color coordinates of based curvature slots; it is not a count of raw link entries.

13.5.1The microscopic filtration and its Taylor data

Fix a gauge group, a cutoff degree \(d\), and a family of test holonomies: for every radial jet \(J\) and every mesh \(t\), link variables \(U_d (J ; t)\) on the cover lattice solving the link transport equation of the truncated radial connection of \(J\) at mesh \(t\), as in Subsection 7.10. Since the degree-\(\ell\) component \(A_{\ell}\) of \(J\) is a homogeneous one-form of degree \(\ell + 1\), these are the mesh-one holonomies \(U_d (J_t) = U_d (J_t ; 1)\) of the jet \(J_t\) whose component \(A_{\ell}\) is multiplied by \(t^{\ell + 2}\). The link-action derivatives of a field function \(f\) are the derivatives at \(t = 0\) of \(t \mapsto f (U_d (J ; t))\), for every \(J\). A slot \(S\) is one of the based, transported plaquette-curvature readouts on the cover lattice. For a real basis vector \(e_a\) of the color space, write

\(\displaystyle x_{S, a} (U) = e_a \cdot S (U) .\)

Let \(\mathcal{P}_{\geqslant r}\) be the complex span of finite products of at least \(r\) such coordinates, with the empty product allowed when \(r = 0\). Thus a single slot coordinate has filtration degree one even though, as a function of link matrices, it is a nonlinear polynomial or analytic expression.

For a label \(p\) of grade \(m = (r, s)\), a marked read consists of a field function \(f\) and a polynomial \(q\) with three properties. The function is gauge invariant, has at least \(s\) slot factors in the span sense above, and its link-action derivatives through order \(d\) agree with those of \(q\): for every jet \(J\) the function \(t \mapsto f (U_d (J ; t))\) is \(d\) times continuously differentiable at \(0\), and its derivatives of order at most \(d\) there equal those of the polynomial \(t \mapsto q (J_t)\). Here \(q\) has degree at most \(d\) in the first grade; it is then determined by \(f\). The correction \(q - p\) has strictly larger grade stamp

\(\displaystyle \text{st} (r, s) = (s, r)\)

in lexicographic order. When \(p\) lies in the Maxwell equation ideal, the read \(f\) is additionally required to be microscopic. A function \(f\) of the cover links is called microscopic if it is continuous and there are a radius \(\varrho\) and a side threshold \(M\) with the following property. For every torus of side at least \(M\), every site \(o\) of it, every real \(\beta \ne 0\), and every bounded measurable function \(S\) of the torus configuration which depends only on links both of whose endpoints have distance greater than \(\varrho\) from \(o\),

\(\displaystyle \int f (U^o) \hspace{0.17em} S (U) \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{\beta} (U) = 0,\)

where \(U^o\) is the torus configuration read on the cover from the anchor \(o\) and \(\mu_{\beta}\) is the Wilson measure. Neither gauge invariance nor continuity of the spectator \(S\) is required, and \(\varrho, M\) do not depend on \(\beta\), \(o\), \(S\) or the side. Microscopic functions with given \((\varrho, M)\) form a complex vector space, and \((\varrho, M)\) may be enlarged. These are precisely the properties needed for later stencil pricing and for the exact equation-ideal payment.

We name the two objects produced in this section. A marked lifting at cutoff \(d\) is a complex-linear map \(F : p \mapsto (q_p, f_p)\) from the label space into pairs consisting of a label and a function of the cover links, such that \(F (1) = (1, 1)\) and, for every grade \(m \in \{0, \ldots, d\}^2\) and every label \(p\) of pure grade \(m\), the pair \((f_p, q_p)\) is a marked read of \(p\) in the above sense, microscopic whenever \(p\) lies in the equation ideal. An equivariant marked lifting is a marked lifting which commutes with the signed coordinate changes and with the real structure; both actions are written out in the proof of Theorem 13.9.

The elementary analytic input is a first-order germ for each slot. Put \(\text{Snap}_d\) for the finite-dimensional space of radial link jets through degree \(d\). For every slot \(S\), the function

\(\displaystyle J \longmapsto S (U_d (J)), \qquad J \in \text{Snap}_d,\)

is real analytic near zero, transforms by the adjoint action under a constant gauge transformation at the base point, vanishes at zero, and has derivative equal to the linearized plaquette curvature. To see the analyticity directly, on one link the connection coefficient is a linear function \(A_J (t)\) of \(J\). On the Banach space \(C ([0, 1], \mathfrak{A})\) of continuous paths in the finite-dimensional matrix algebra \(\mathfrak{A}\) containing the group representation, the Volterra operator

\(\displaystyle (T_J u) (t) = \int_0^t u (s) A_J (s) \hspace{0.17em} ds\)

has norm at most \(\sup_s \|A_J (s)\|\). In a neighborhood of \(J = 0\) this is less than one, so the unique solution of the link equation is

\(\displaystyle u_J = (I - T_J)^{- 1} \boldsymbol{1} = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} T_J^n \boldsymbol{1} .\)

The series converges locally uniformly in the finite-dimensional parameter \(J\), and hence is analytic there. Uniqueness identifies it with the given link transport. A slot is obtained by a finite product of these transports and their inverses, followed by the plaquette curvature map; it is therefore analytic as well. Constant-gauge covariance follows by conjugating the ODE, and differentiating at \(J = 0\) gives the signed sum of the edge tangents around the plaquette, which is the linearized curvature. This proves the required slot germ without imposing any regularity on a configuration space of infinite link fields.

13.5.2Avoiding one edge while spanning Taylor tests

The next finite-dimensional fact is the source of the locality needed for products. Regard \(\text{Snap}_d\) itself as the real vector space \(\mathcal{T}_d\) of radial jets. For a slot \(S\), let

\(\displaystyle B_S = D_0 (J \mapsto S (U_d (J))) : \text{Snap}_d \to \mathfrak{g}.\)

If \(e\) is a prescribed cover edge, call a slot \(S\) safe for \(e\) if its spatial support avoids \(e\) along the translated path used to define \(S\).

Lemma 13.6. (Safe Taylor tests span) For each edge \(e\), the real linear span of the component functionals of \(B_S\), over slots \(S\) safe for \(e\), is all of \(\mathcal{T}_d^{\ast}\).

Proof. For \(d < 2\) the radial-jet space has no Taylor components and the claim is immediate. Assume \(d \geqslant 2\). Take a jet \(A \in \text{Snap}_d\) annihilated by every listed functional. For every pair of axes \(i < j\), the safe-slot geometry excludes only a finite set of plaquette base points. At each other base point it supplies a detour transport path avoiding \(e\). The hypothesis says the resulting slot derivative on \(A\) is zero. The slot-germ identity therefore gives the full sampled curl equation below, in which \(x_y \in \mathbb{R}^4\) denotes the position of the lattice point \(y\). Here \(C_{ij} (A_{\ell}) (x, t)\) denotes the axes-\((i, j)\) finite-mesh curl component of the degree-\(\ell\) Taylor field at position \(x\) and mesh \(t\). It is the function \(N_{\ell} (x, t)\) of Subsection 7.10 for the axes \(i, j\): it is jointly continuous, equals the curl component \(\partial_i A_{\ell, j} - \partial_j A_{\ell, i}\) at \(t = 0\), and is homogeneous of degree \(\ell\) under simultaneous scaling, \(C_{ij} (A_{\ell}) (tx, t) = t^{\ell} C_{ij} (A_{\ell}) (x, 1)\).

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\ell = 0}^{d - 2} C_{ij} (A_{\ell}) (x_y, 1) = 0\)

at every lattice point \(y\) outside the finite exceptional set. We now induct downwards: once shells above \(k\) have been shown to vanish, this same equation reduces to its sum over \(0 \leqslant \ell \leqslant k\). For \(t > 0\) define

\(\displaystyle F_k (x, t) = \sum_{\ell = 0}^k t^{k - \ell} C_{ij} (A_{\ell}) (x, t) .\)

Homogeneity of the degree-\(\ell\) curl component gives

\(\displaystyle F_k (tx_y, t) = t^k \sum_{\ell = 0}^k C_{ij} (A_{\ell}) (x_y, 1) = 0.\)

Choose lattice sites \(y_t\) with \(tx_{y_t} \to x\) as \(t \downarrow 0\). For \(x \ne 0\) the finite exceptional set is avoided for all sufficiently small \(t\). Continuity of \(F_k\) gives \(F_k (x, 0) = C_{ij} (A_k) (x, 0) = 0\), since every lower-shell term has a positive power of \(t\) at \(t = 0\). Continuity extends this identity to \(x = 0\). Thus the top coefficient has zero curl. For a homogeneous radial-gauge potential of degree \(k + 1\), this forces the potential to vanish directly. Differentiate its radial-gauge identity \(\sum_j x_j A_j (x) = 0\) in coordinate \(i\) and use curl-freeness and Euler's identity:

\(\displaystyle 0 = A_i + \sum_j x_j \partial_i A_j = A_i + \sum_j x_j \partial_j A_i = (k + 2) A_i .\)

Hence \(A_i = 0\) for every coordinate, so the top shell vanishes. Remove it from the sampled equation and repeat with the next lower index. Descending induction proves \(A = 0\). In a finite-dimensional space a family of functionals spans the dual exactly when its common kernel is zero, which proves the claim.\(\Box\)

The slot linearization in this proof is concrete. For a based plaquette readout it is the oriented sum of the four link tangents, transported to the base point; the ODE calculation above proves this formula for the actual link action. Only finitely many plaquette base points are excluded; at each remaining point the detour path is chosen separately. The radial argument therefore applies to every Taylor test, not just to tests supported on a generic set.

Lemma 13.7. (Equation-factor and spectator reads) Let \(a\) be a homogeneous polynomial in the equation ideal, of grade \(m = (r, s)\) with \(r \leqslant d\). Then \(a\) is a finite linear combination of terms

\(\displaystyle v = \lambda \hspace{0.17em} \prod_{i = 1}^k z_i,\)

each of grade \(m\), where \(\lambda\) is a linear functional on one Taylor layer annihilating the Maxwell solution-potential subspace of that layer and each \(z_i\) is a linear functional on one Taylor layer (a linear label atom). Their field reads are assembled from slot-coordinate observables. Each term \(v\) has a raw read \(f\) and a polynomial \(q\) of first grade at most \(d\) such that the link-action derivatives of \(f\) through order \(d\) agree with those of \(q\), the difference \(q - v\) has strictly larger grade stamp than \(m\), \(f \in \mathcal{P}_{\geqslant s}\), and \(f\) is microscopic. Individual terms need not be gauge invariant.

Proof. Write \(\mathcal{E}_j\) for a projection onto the space of radial potentials obtained from on-shell Maxwell curvatures at order \(j\), and let \(\mathcal{E}\) act coefficientwise. By definition, a label belongs to the equation ideal when it vanishes on every jet all of whose layers are such solution potentials. The equation ideal is therefore the kernel of the substitution \(b \mapsto \text{changed} (\mathcal{E}) b\), which replaces every literal atom \(u\) by \(\mathcal{E}u = u \circ \mathcal{E}\): the value of \(\text{changed} (\mathcal{E}) b\) at a jet \(J\) is \(b (\mathcal{E}J)\), the jets \(\mathcal{E}J\) are exactly the solution jets, and a polynomial vanishing at every jet is zero. Since \(a\) belongs to that ideal,

\(\displaystyle a = a - \text{changed} (\mathcal{E}) a.\)

Expand \(a\) into monomials before applying the substitution. For a literal slot atom \(u\), split \(u = (u -\mathcal{E}u) +\mathcal{E}u\). The first summand annihilates every Maxwell solution potential. For a product \(u_1 \cdots u_k\), use the telescoping identity

\(\displaystyle \prod_{j = 1}^k u_j - \prod_{j = 1}^k \mathcal{E}u_j = \sum_{i = 1}^k (u_i -\mathcal{E}u_i) \left( \prod_{j < i} \mathcal{E}u_j \right) \left( \prod_{j > i} u_j \right) .\)

The projection preserves the radial grade. Thus every summand has one solution-annihilating linear factor and a finite spectator product, with the original total grade. This proves the displayed factorization directly from the equation-ideal definition; no invariant-factor assumption is made.

Fix one generator \(v = \lambda \prod_i z_i\), and let \(j\) be the Taylor layer on which \(\lambda\) acts, so that \(\lambda\) has grade \((j + 2, 1)\). The functional \(\lambda\) annihilates the Maxwell solution-potential subspace of that layer. The required duality statement concerns only the top Taylor layer of the jets through cutoff \(j + 2\), that is, of \(A = (A_0, \ldots, A_j)\). A positive path force is the linearization at the zero jet of a color component of the force at a positive link \((y, \nu)\), transported to the anchor along a walk which ends at \(y\) and does not traverse that link; such a walk exists for every link. For a homogeneous potential \(A_{\ell}\), an axis pair \(p = (a, c)\) with \(a < c\) and an axis \(b\), define the transverse plaquette difference by

\(\displaystyle T_{\ell} (p, b ; x) = N_{\ell} (p ; x, 1) - N_{\ell} (p ; x - e_b, 1),\)

where \(N_{\ell} (p ; x, t) = C_{ac} (A_{\ell}) (x, t)\) is the sampled curl of the proof of Lemma 13.6 on the plaquette with axes \(p\) based at \(x\). The plaquette incidence in direction \(\nu\) selects \(T_{\ell} (p, c ; x)\) if \(a = \nu\), \(- T_{\ell} (p, a ; x)\) if \(c = \nu\), and zero otherwise; write \(\hat{T}_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x)\) for the result, the transverse force density. By the slot-germ identity, the linearized force at the link \((y, \nu)\) is \(\sum_{\ell = 0}^j \sum_p \hat{T}_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x_y)\) on the jet \(A\), whatever the walk. The corresponding line term \(L_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x, t)\) is the same incidence sum of the oriented arc; each arc is the difference of the two ordered triple averages of \(\partial_b \partial_a A_{\ell, c}\) and \(\partial_b \partial_c A_{\ell, a}\) over the mesh-\(t\) plaquette and the preceding edge in direction \(b\). It is jointly continuous in \((x, t)\). The fundamental theorem of calculus in the direction \(b\), the homogeneity of \(N_{\ell}\), and evaluation at \(t = 0\) give the scaling and zero-mesh identities

\(\displaystyle tL_{\ell} (\nu, p ; tx, t) = t^{\ell} \hat{T}_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x), \qquad \sum_p L_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x, 0) = - \sum_b \partial_b \text{curl} (A_{\ell})_{b \nu} (x) .\)

If every positive path-force test vanishes on \(A\), then at every lattice point \(y\) and for every axis \(\nu\) the finite force sum \(\sum_{\ell \leqslant j} \sum_p \hat{T}_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x_y)\) vanishes. Its line form is

\(\displaystyle F_j (x, t) = \sum_{\ell = 0}^j t^{j - \ell} \sum_p L_{\ell} (\nu, p ; x, t) .\)

The scaling identity shows that \(tF_j (tx_y, t)\) is \(t^j\) times the vanishing finite force sum. Since \(t > 0\), this implies \(F_j (tx_y, t) = 0\). For arbitrary real \(x\), choose lattice points \(y_t\) with \(tx_{y_t} \to x\); here, unlike in Lemma 13.6, no lattice point is excluded. Let \(t \downarrow 0\) and use continuity. Only the top coefficient remains, and the zero-mesh identity gives \(\sum_b \partial_b \text{curl} (A_j)_{b \nu} = 0\). The curl is also closed by the Bianchi identity, hence is an on-shell Maxwell curvature. Put \(Q_{ab} = \partial_a A_{j, b} - \partial_b A_{j, a}\). The radial potential obtained from an on-shell curvature \(Q\) of this degree is its radial contraction \((j + 2)^{- 1} \sum_a x_a Q_{ab}\). For the curl of \(A_j\) this contraction is \(A_j\) itself: the radial identity \(\sum_a x_a A_{j, a} = 0\), differentiated in \(x_b\), gives \(\sum_a x_a \partial_b A_{j, a} = - A_{j, b}\), and Euler's identity for the homogeneous degree \(j + 1\) gives \(\sum_a x_a \partial_a A_{j, b} = (j + 1) A_{j, b}\), so that

\(\displaystyle \sum_a x_a Q_{ab} = (j + 1) A_{j, b} + A_{j, b} = (j + 2) A_{j, b} .\)

Thus \(A_j\) is the radial potential of the on-shell curvature \(Q\), that is, it lies in the image of the Maxwell potential map.

Now \(\lambda\) is a linear functional on the top Taylor layer annihilating that solution-potential image. It vanishes on \(A_j\) whenever the positive force tests vanish on \(A\). Thus its composition with projection onto the top Taylor layer annihilates the common kernel of the force family. Finite-dimensional duality puts this composed functional in the span of the positive path forces. This is the force representation used to construct the equation-factor read; no statement about the full force-kernel in all Taylor layers is needed.

The equation-factor atom \(\lambda\) has grade \((j + 2, 1)\); write \(z = \prod_i z_i\) for the spectator word, whose grade is the sum of the grades of its factors, so that \(m\) is the sum of these two grades and \(s = k + 1\). At the intermediate cutoff \(j + 2\), the force representation expresses \(\lambda\), composed with the projection to the top layer, as a finite real combination \(\sum_{\gamma} c_{\gamma} \phi_{\gamma}\) of positive path forces. Each \(\phi_{\gamma}\) is given by a link, a walk avoiding it and a color index; these data define a transported force function \(g_{\gamma}\) of the cover links, and also the linearization \(\tilde{\phi}_{\gamma}\) of \(g_{\gamma}\) on jets through the requested cutoff \(d\), which restricts to \(\phi_{\gamma}\) on jets through \(j + 2\). Put \(\tilde{\lambda} = \sum_{\gamma} c_{\gamma} \tilde{\phi}_{\gamma}\). The force is a signed sum of slot coordinates, so replacing each linearized curvature by its actual slot read gives \(g_{\gamma}\) a Taylor polynomial through order \(d\) which differs from \(\tilde{\phi}_{\gamma}\) by terms of slot filtration at least two. The spectator word is read separately for each force term \(\gamma\), by induction on its length: for each factor \(z_i\), Lemma 13.6 supplies a combination of slot coordinates, all safe for the force link of \(\gamma\), whose linearization is \(z_i\), and the product \(h_{\gamma}\) of these combinations lies in \(\mathcal{P}_{\geqslant k}\). The product rule for Taylor polynomials then puts every error term of \(h_{\gamma}\) at least one slot above the spectator's own factor count \(k\). The raw read is \(f = \sum_{\gamma} c_{\gamma} g_{\gamma} h_{\gamma} \in \mathcal{P}_{\geqslant k + 1}\). Each summand is a transported force at a link times the continuous function \(h_{\gamma}\). Safety is a statement about cover edges; the one-link identity needs it after periodization, for every anchor. For each \(\gamma\), collect the finitely many cover edges traversed by the transport walk of the force and by the walks and plaquettes of all slots occurring in \(h_{\gamma}\). None of them is the force edge. Since the set is finite, there is a side threshold beyond which none of these edges is congruent to the force edge modulo the side; translation by an anchor preserves these inequalities. Hence, on every torus of at least that side and for every anchor, the periodized transport and the periodized \(h_{\gamma}\) are unchanged when the translated force link alone is varied. Each summand is therefore microscopic by the one-link identity proved after this lemma; taking the largest of the finitely many radii and thresholds, \(f\) is microscopic.

It remains to compare the Taylor polynomial \(w\) of \(f\) through order \(d\) with the generator \(v = \lambda z\). The functional \(\tilde{\lambda}\) extends the intermediate Taylor functional to the full jet through the requested cutoff \(d \geqslant j + 2\); it is multiplied by the unchanged spectator word. The extension has two distinct errors. First, the discrepancy between the constructed read and its own lifted Taylor polynomial has one more slot factor than the target's second grade. Second, the lifted linear functional agrees with the original equation atom on the intermediate jet; its remaining Taylor tail has strictly higher first grade while retaining the same slot count. Multiplying this tail by the spectator word preserves the strict first-grade increase. Thus the required comparison is the sum

\(\displaystyle w - v = \left( w - \text{Taylor}_{\leqslant d} (\tilde{\lambda} \hspace{0.17em} z) \right) + \left( \text{Taylor}_{\leqslant d} (\tilde{\lambda} \hspace{0.17em} z) - v \right),\)

where \(\text{Taylor}_{\leqslant d}\) discards the monomials of first grade above \(d\). The first term is strictly later in the slot-count coordinate of the grade stamp; the second is strictly later in the first-grade coordinate. Both are therefore strictly above the target stamp. This two-part comparison, together with the forced edge read, gives the required positioned read for the generator, with \(q = w\). Finite sums and scalar multiples extend it to the full homogeneous ideal element.

The reads in this lemma are raw reads; gauge averaging is done only after the terms for the invariant label have been assembled.\(\Box\)

The conditional moment calculation in this argument is the one-link Haar integration-by-parts identity. For a walk \(w\) from the anchor to the base point of a source edge \(e\), not traversing \(e\), the transported force is

\(\displaystyle \text{Ad}_{\text{travel} (U, 0, w)} \hspace{0.17em} \text{field} (U, e) .\)

Move the transport to the other slot using invariance of the color inner product, then expand in a real orthonormal color basis \((E_a)\). Every term has the form

\(\displaystyle \langle E_a, \text{field} (U, e) \rangle \hspace{0.17em} S_a (U),\)

where \(S_a\) is the spectator times a coefficient depending only on the walk transport. For the translated test at anchor \(o\), choose the Micro radius at least the distance of the base point of the source edge from the anchor, and choose the side threshold at least two, so large that this base point does not wrap and that the walk transport and the remaining factor of the read are independent of the translated source link in the sense just described. The test spectator is then local outside that radius and is unchanged when only the translated source link is varied. Haar integration by parts on that link gives

\(\displaystyle \int (\partial_{E_a} S_a - \beta \langle E_a, \text{field} (U, e) \rangle S_a) d \mu_{\beta} (U) = 0.\)

The derivative of the bare density is the displayed force term. The envelope bound supplies an integrable majorant for differentiating under the integral, so the identity also holds for the bounded measurable spectators permitted in the Micro definition. The derivative term vanishes, because neither the walk transport (the walk does not traverse \(e\)), nor the remaining factor of the read, nor the test spectator depends on the varied link. The identity therefore reads \(\beta \int \langle E_a, \text{field} (U, e) \rangle S_a \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{\beta} = 0\). Dividing by \(\beta\) and summing over \(a\) gives the force moment zero. The integration-by-parts identity is valid for every real \(\beta\); the condition \(\beta \ne 0\) in the definition of a microscopic function is used only in this division.

Scope of the factor lemma.
The force calculation used is only the top-component implication proved from the radial current limit: simultaneous vanishing of the positive path-force tests puts the highest radial coefficient in the image of the Maxwell potential map. The conditional one-link Haar identity is the moment input defining the equation leg. The argument above proves the monomial factorization, edge-avoiding spectator assembly, and two-part filtration comparison from these facts.

13.5.3The all-grade marked lifting

The previous lemmas give the exact pointwise producer needed for the finite-dimensional assembly.

Proposition 13.8. (Positioned equation-ideal read) For every pure-grade label \(p\) in the Maxwell equation ideal, of grade \(m = (r, s)\) with \(r \leqslant d\), there are a gauge-invariant function \(f\) and a label \(q\) such that \(f\) has slot degree at least \(s\), the action derivatives through order \(d\) of \(f\) and \(q\) agree, \(q - p\) has grade stamp strictly above that of \(m\), and \(f\) is microscopic. Thus \((f, q)\) is a microscopic marked read of \(p\).

Proof. Use the exact ideal factorization to write \(p\) as a finite sum of gap atoms times spectator monomials. Apply the factor-read lemma to those generators and add the resulting raw reads. Their polynomial targets sum to \(p\); their errors remain in the strictly higher-stamp subspace, and the slot-degree and microscopic conditions pass to finite sums. The resulting raw function need not yet be gauge invariant.

Now average the pair over the compact gauge group at the anchor. A constant gauge transformation rotates the color components of each of the finitely many fixed slot addresses among a finite basis. Thus the orbit of this particular finite sum lies in a finite-dimensional span, and its Haar integral is an ordinary finite-dimensional vector integral. The label \(p\) is gauge invariant, so the target of every rotated read is still \(p\); its Taylor correction remains in the same strictly higher stamp subspace, because the rotation acts on each Taylor layer and preserves both grades. Each rotated read is again microscopic with the same radius and threshold: the Wilson measure is invariant under the constant gauge transformation, and composing a spectator with it preserves its locality, measurability and bound. The integral of a continuous map into a finite-dimensional space against a probability measure lies in the linear span of its values. Applying this to the orbit of the pair \((1, f)\), whose first entry is constant, shows that the average is a finite linear combination of rotated reads with coefficients summing to one. Forms, slot filtration, the stamp condition relative to the fixed target \(p\), and the microscopic moment condition are preserved by such combinations.
