\(\displaystyle c_m (v) = t + g_m (- a) + g_m (a + v) \leqslant t + C_m \|Z.x\|_1 + C_m \|\mathcal{Z}_m (v) .x\|_1 \leqslant w + C_m (w +\|\mathcal{Z}_m (v) .x\|_1) .\)

This is the displayed terminal bound.

We now descend from \(m\) to \(l\). At a step \(i\), assume a keyed state at \(i + 1\) has been constructed. The same transfer sequence supplies its Heard and Responds data at \(i\) and \(i + 1\). Apply the paid one-step response with the state's format and remainder bound; its zero leading coordinate is exactly the transfer record hypothesis. The output is a keyed state at \(i\), its transfer sequence-index formula, the density push identity, the paid direction inequality, and the finite forced-profile witness. Replace only the state at \(i\) and keep all states at levels above \(i\) unchanged. Thus all already proved chair and transition clauses persist, and the new clauses hold at \(i\). Finite downward induction constructs the full sequence. This is a finite induction on \(m - l\); it does not pass to a subsequence or alter the selected history.

For the signed difference, let \(\varpi_l\) be the primary of the bottom state and evaluate every state at displacement \(- \varpi_l\). The bottom played coordinate is then zero. At each level, the format and remainder clauses follow from keyedness and the transfer sequence's earned bounds; transition and density clauses follow from the all-displacement response identities. For the numerical propagation fix this displacement \(v = - \varpi_l\) and abbreviate \(x_i = x_i (v)\), \(c_i = c_i (v)\), and \(t_i = t_i (v)\); thus the map used at step \(i\) is the possibly displacement-dependent \(B_{i, v}\), whose norm bounds are uniform. Put \(C_i = \text{columns} (i)\), \(s_i = L_i^{1 / 8}\), \(e_i = s_i^{- 3}\), and \(A_i = 32 (1 + C_i)^2\). Room cost says \(64 W (J_i + 1)^2 \leqslant C_i\); since \(J_{i + 1} \leqslant J_i + 1\) and \(W\) is nondecreasing, this yields \(64 K_i^2 \leqslant C_i\). The bank inequality at step \(i\), together with \(W (J_{i + 1}) \leqslant W (J_{i + 1} + 1)\), yields \(100000 K_i^6 (1 + C_{i + 1})^2 \leqslant s_i\). Also \(K_i \geqslant 1\) and \(C_i \geqslant 1\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle 4 K_i e_i \leqslant 1, \qquad 4 K_i A_{i + 1} e_i \leqslant 1, \qquad (4 K_i^2 + 1) (s_i e_i) \leqslant 1.\)

Indeed, writing \(C = C_{i + 1}\) and \(K = K_i\), the bank bound implies \(s_i \geqslant 128 K^2 (1 + C)^2\), since \(100000 K^6 \geqslant 128 K^2\) for \(K \geqslant 1\). Thus \(4 KA_{i + 1} = 128 K (1 + C)^2 \leqslant s_i\) and \(4 K \leqslant s_i\). Also \((4 K^2 + 1) s_i e_i = (4 K^2 + 1) s_i^{- 2} \leqslant 1\) because \(s_i \geqslant 128 K^2\). These are the three displayed numerical inequalities. The direction inequalities for the translated states imply, with \(u = \|x_i \|_1\), \(u_+ = \|x_{i + 1} \|_1\), and \(y = \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L_i} (x_{i + 1})\|_1\),

\(\displaystyle y \leqslant K_i (u + e_i (y + c_{i + 1})), \qquad c_i \leqslant K_i y + e_i c_{i + 1}, \qquad u_+ \leqslant y.\)

If \(A_i u \leqslant c_i\), combining these inequalities with the three numerical bounds gives \(A_{i + 1} u_+ \leqslant c_{i + 1}\) and \(s_i c_i \leqslant c_{i + 1}\). Indeed, substituting \(u \leqslant (K_i y + e_i c_{i + 1}) / A_i\) into the first direction inequality gives

\(\displaystyle y \leqslant \left( \frac{K_i^2}{A_i} + K_i e_i \right) y + \left( \frac{K_i e_i}{A_i} + K_i e_i \right) c_{i + 1} .\)

Since \(A_i = 32 (1 + C_i)^2\), we have \(K_i^2 / A_i \leqslant 1 / 32\); \(K_i e_i \leqslant 1 / 4\); and \(A_i \geqslant 1\). Thus the coefficient of \(y\) is at most \(9 / 32 < 1 / 2\), and the coefficient of \(c_{i + 1}\) is at most \(2 K_i e_i\). It follows that \(y \leqslant 4 K_i e_i c_{i + 1}\). Now \(u_+ \leqslant y\) and \(4 K_i A_{i + 1} e_i \leqslant 1\) give \(A_{i + 1} u_+ \leqslant c_{i + 1}\). In the same way \(s_i u_+ \leqslant 4 K_i (s_i e_i) c_{i + 1} \leqslant c_{i + 1}\), because \(4 K_i \leqslant 4 K_i^2 + 1\) and \((4 K_i^2 + 1) (s_i e_i) \leqslant 1\); this is the second inequality of the last item of the lemma. Also \(c_i \leqslant K_i y + e_i c_{i + 1}\) gives \(s_i c_i \leqslant (4 K_i^2 + 1) s_i e_i c_{i + 1} \leqslant c_{i + 1}\) by the third numerical bound. At the bottom, keyedness gives \(x_l = 0\) and nonnegative cost. Thus induction gives \(A_i \|x_i \|_1 \leqslant c_i\) at every level, and iterating \(s_i c_i \leqslant c_{i + 1}\) gives \(\prod_{j = i}^{m - 1} s_j c_i \leqslant c_m\). The scale identity is \(\prod_{j = i}^{m - 1} s_j = \exp ((\sigma_m - \sigma_i) / 8)\).

At the top the terminal bound and \(A_m \|x_m \|_1 \leqslant c_m\) imply \(2 C_m \|x_m \|_1 \leqslant c_m\) and hence \(c_m \leqslant 2 (1 + C_m) w\). This completes the last item of the lemma. Since \(A_i \geqslant 1\), also \(\|x_i \|_1 \leqslant c_i\). Therefore \(\exp ((\sigma_m - \sigma_i) / 8) (c_i +\|x_i \|_1) \leqslant 2 c_m \leqslant 4 (1 + C_m) w\). This proves the cone clause directly from the room and bank inequalities, on the same finite interval and history.\(\Box\)

Same-history inputs to the lemma.
For each step, the response is supplied by a same-history coefficient transfer map, a paid transfer record for every keyed silent upper coefficient, and the matching Transfer witness. The transfer record fixes the input exponential entry, forced profile, distinguished leading value, and density; its output coefficient has the specified exact density identity and paid leading/remainder bounds. The transfer witness includes the upper and lower forced profiles and exponential entries at every displacement, together with common finite support and the charge comparison used above. These are exact inputs, not continuity limits.

The local three-card algebra which produces the paid transfer record from a joint coefficient and remainder estimate is proved in Subsection 10.10. It uses the common-history identities, transfer bound and forced-estimate there, together with the three selected card estimates and the finite threshold maximum. This finite descent consumes the resulting transfer record and transfer witness records; it does not reprove the card algebra. The common-history attachment of these records is supplied by Propositions 13.34 and 13.35, under the common selected-history purchases. The zero-coordinate Transfer record used in this descent is supplied by Proposition 13.36, with its insertion, soundness and merger hypotheses verified as described in Subsection 13.10.

13.9.3Application to the terminal insertion

We now state the calibration used by the selected field argument. Fix one family \(C\) which makes the action, its selected history, a period \(p\), a terminal volume \(N\), and a degree \(d\). Write \(a_i\) for the mesh at level \(i\), \(L_i = a_i / a_{i + 1}\) for the block side, \(\sigma_i\) for the running scale, \(C_i = \text{columns} (i)\), \(\alpha_i\) for the support exponent and \(B_i\) for the support radius of the level-\(i\) budget, \(\theta_i\) for its threshold, \(J_i\) for the clock index, and \(T\) for the local section of the family at degree \(d\), with frame \(F\) and maximal factor grade \(q\). The following notation is used for the root tests:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} \lambda_i & = L_i^{1 / 8}, & \text{gain} (N, i) & = e^{(\sigma_N - \sigma_i) / 8},\\ \| \text{invDil} (H, i, d)\| & = a_i^{- d}, & \text{redBud} (N, k, E) & = E / \text{gain} (N, k),\\ \text{volFac} (m, k) & = \exp \left( (a_m / a_k)^4 \hspace{0.17em} \text{vacuumBound}_k + 5 \right), & \text{scaleDecay}_{- \gamma} (N, p) & = e^{- \gamma \sigma_N}, \end{array}\)

with \(\gamma = 1 / 8\) and \(\text{vacuumBound}_k\) the vacuum bound per site at level \(k\), and

\(\displaystyle \text{rootFac} (d, m, k) = \text{volFac} (m, k) \hspace{0.17em} \frac{4 a_k^{- d}}{\zeta_{d, k}} \hspace{0.17em} e^{\gamma \sigma_k} .\)

For \(m \leqslant N\), \(\text{rootTest}_{m ; N, p} (f)\) is the centered root test of Subsection 4.1 for the centered tiling of depth \(m\) based at the origin and the finite-volume law \(\mu_{N, p}\): the \(K\)th root of the modulus of the expectation of the product of the \(K\) prescribed copies of \(f\), where \(K = | \text{Site} (m, p) |\) is the number of cells. We say that \(m\) is opened at \(k\), and write \(\mathcal{O} (m, k)\), if \(m \leqslant k\) and \(B_k^2 \leqslant a_m / a_k\).

The paid selected-history record.
The calibration consumes the following record on the fixed family and history. The family, the history, the numerical sequences and the indexed field of interfaces are fixed globally: they do not depend on \(N\), \(p\) or \(d\), and the items are required for all of them. The record is assumed in this section. Its simultaneous production on the selected public run, including the reserved-address and fee conditions of Subsection 13.7, is supplied under the common selected-history purchases in Subsection 13.10; its paragraph on the paid selected-history record identifies each of (R1)–(R7) with its supplier. It consists of a nondecreasing function \(W \geqslant 1\) on stages, real sequences \(u_i, \psi_i, \phi_i, \delta_i\), heights \(\zeta_{d, i}\), a sequence of natural numbers \(r_i\), and one gauge interface \(D_{N, p, i}\) between the representatives at levels \(i + 1\) and \(i\) for all \(i < N\) and all \(p\), with these properties.

    (R1)

    Room and bank. For every \(i\), \(64 W (J_i + 1)^2 \leqslant C_i\) and \(100000 \hspace{0.17em} W (J_{i + 1} + 1)^6 (1 + C_{i + 1})^2 \leqslant L_i^{1 / 8}\).

    (R2)

    Scale bounds. For every \(i\): \(\phi_i > 0\), \(\delta_i > 0\), \(1 \leqslant \delta_i \phi_i e^{B_i / 128}\), \(B_{i + 1}^2 \leqslant L_i B_i^2\) and \(8 \leqslant B_i\).

    (R3)

    Occupancy. The sequence \(r_i\) is nondecreasing and \(r_i \geqslant 1\).

    (R4)

    Transition service. Put \(\textsf{s} (B) = e \hspace{0.17em} (2 (16 B + 9))^4\), and let \(w^{\text{f}}_i\) be the forced allowance at level \(i\). For all \(N\), \(p\) and \(i < N\) there are reals \(y, w\) such that:

    • the interface \(D_{N, p, i}\) satisfies the multi-marker offer input with occupancy \(r_i\), offer threshold \(\alpha_{i + 1} - 4\) and offer allowance \(y\), ordinary exponent \(\alpha_i + \frac{1}{2}\) with site budget \(1\), and flagged exponent \(\alpha_i + 1\) with site budget \(w\). Its full conditions remain an input; they are not derived here. They include the pushed representation of every finite census of offers of occupancy at most \(r_i\), soundness and tuning of the interface, and a contact condition on the upper representation;

    • \(0 < \delta_i \leqslant 1\), \(\alpha_i \geqslant 100\), \(\alpha_{i + 1} \geqslant 100\);

    • with \(T = r_i \textsf{s} (B_{i + 1}) \delta_i + 4 \delta_i\), one has \(T + e^T T \leqslant y\);

    • with \(c = w + r_i \textsf{s} (B_i) \delta_i\), one has \((2 B_i + 1)^4 c \leqslant \frac{1}{2}\) and \(e^c - 1 \leqslant 1\).

    Moreover, at every level \(i\): \(u_i \geqslant 0\), \(\alpha_i \geqslant 3\), and with \(C = w^{\text{f}}_i + r_i \textsf{s} (B_i) u_i\) one has \((2 B_i + 1)^4 C \leqslant 3\) and \(e^C - 1 \leqslant 1\).

    (R5)

    Heights. For every \(i \geqslant \text{ref} (d)\): \(\zeta_{d, i} > 0\), \(\phi_i \leqslant 1\), \(\psi_i \geqslant 0\),

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} \phi_i L_i^{d + 1} \zeta_{d, i} \leqslant \zeta_{d, i + 1}, \qquad \phi_i L_i^d C_{i + 1} \zeta_{d, i} \leqslant \zeta_{d, i + 1},\\ \zeta_{d, i} C_i \leqslant 1, \qquad 2\mathcal{O}_d (\psi_i + 2 C_{i + 1}) \zeta_{d, i + 1} \leqslant 1, \end{array}\)

    where \(\mathcal{O}_d\) is the orbit cost of the frame. For every terminal level \(N \geqslant \text{ref} (d)\) and period \(p\) there is \(X \geqslant 1\) with \(|T (e_j) (0, U) | \leqslant X\) for all axes and configurations, the footprint of \(T\) at the origin has cover number at most one, and \(e^{\alpha_N + 2} X \theta_N^{- q} \zeta_{d, N} \leqslant u_N\).

    (R6)

    The transfer sequence. For every \(N \geqslant \text{ref} (d)\) and \(p\) there is a transfer sequence \(M\) on this history at \((N, p)\) whose played coefficients are the entries of the family's transfer data, which is admissible at every level from \(\text{ref} (d)\) to \(N\) and responds across every intervening interface. Its terminal coefficient family has the properties of Proposition 13.15: lead \(v\) at every label \(v\), so that the terminal index is the identity, zero regular part, charge at most \(\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\), the density identity (13.10) of Subsection 13.7, and forced profile equal to the hook of the singleton response \(f_v\). It has a transfer witness witness with bound \(\psi_i\) at every interface \(D_{N, p, i}\), \(\text{ref} (d) \leqslant i < N\): a finite set of insertion supports and, for every label \(v\), a response decomposition across the interface relating the exponential entry and forced profile of the upper transported coefficient to the forced profile of the lower one, with upper bound \(\mathcal{O}_d \| \iota_{i + 1} (v)\|_1 (\psi_i + 2 C_{i + 1})\) and lower bound \(g_i (v)\). The full conditions on the insertion support and response decompositiones remain inputs here. The transfer sequence may depend on \((d, N, p)\).

    (R7)

    The paid one-step input. At every interface \(D_{N, p, i}\), \(\text{ref} (d) \leqslant i < N\), there is a number \(\eta_i \geqslant 0\) with \((1 + 2 C_i) \eta_i \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (L_i)\), the interface is sound, \(\psi_i \geqslant 0\), and there is a finite set of insertion supports such that every upper coefficient with zero leading coordinate, the terminal format and remainder bound \(q\) has a transfer record across the interface with budgets \((\eta_i q, \eta_i q, 2 q)\), as described before Lemma 13.18. The full conditions on the transfer record beyond that description remain an input here.

The multi-marker offer input in (R4) has the following interface. Fix one of its interfaces, its upper and lower representations, and the associated parent map from upper to lower sites. Take any finite marker set \(V\) and any downward-closed family of subsets containing the empty set and all singletons; its marker algebra has commuting square-zero generators, with only the allowed subsets as modes. Choose sites \(o_v\) on the upper lattice, strictly positive weights \(t_v\), and any finite family of offers. Each offer consists of an upper activity leaf with support \(P\), a nonempty allowed marker mode \(A\), and a complex coefficient \(c\); its marker sites \(\{o_v : v \in A\}\) lie in \(P\). Its fare is the leaf weight times \(|c| \prod_{v \in A} t_v\). The offer is priced if, for every upper site \(x\), the sum of fares over supports containing \(x\), each multiplied by \(e^{(\alpha_{i + 1} - 4) \text{coverNumber} (B_{i + 1}, P)}\), is at most \(y\). Assume also that at most \(r_i\) markers have any one parent site.

For every such census, weights, sites and priced offer, the input supplies lower-lattice polynomial families \(F_P\) indexed by supports and \(K_{P, I}\) indexed by support–inventory pairs. Both are gauge invariant, measurable and local to their supports. On the lower lattice the marker positions are the parent sites \(b_v = \text{parent} (o_v)\), and each coefficient of a mode \(A\) in these families vanishes unless \(\{b_v : v \in A\}\) is contained in its support. An \(F_P\) term vanishes unless \(P\) is healthy and has zero empty-mode coefficient. A \(K_{P, I}\) term vanishes unless \(I\) is nonempty, \(I \subseteq P\), and the flags in \(P\) are exactly \(I\). Each family has nonnegative summable support bounds. For each support or support–inventory pair, there is one nonnegative majorant for each mode that bounds the absolute value of that mode's coefficient for every configuration. The weighted sum of these configuration-independent majorants is at most the corresponding support bound; the weight of a mode \(A\) is \(\prod_{v \in A} t_v\). At each lower site the support bounds, multiplied by the exponential of the support cover number, sum to at most \(1\) for \(F\) with exponent \(\alpha_i + 1 / 2\), and at most \(w\) for \(K\) with exponent \(\alpha_i + 1\).

Their assembled lower representation equals exactly the transition push of the offered upper polynomial. For every lower support–inventory pair and configuration, the empty-mode coefficient of \(K\) is the ordinary lower activity profile; its singleton coefficient for marker \(v\) is the sum, over the offered leaves whose mode is \(\{v\}\), of their coefficients times their interface-transported profiles. These identities hold pointwise, not merely after integration. The same interface is also required to have the soundness and tuning interfaces: a uniform forced-cost bound and motion equivariance for its leaf transports, and compatibility of those transports with every countable grouped leaf sum. Finally, the upper representation must have the contact addresses: every nonempty support has a zero-activity regular address, and every nonempty inventory contained in a support has an address eligible whenever the flags realize that inventory. These are hypotheses of the present calibration statement. On the retained public history they are supplied by Theorem 13.30, with the common purchases and local analytic hypotheses stated there.

The items (R1)–(R3), (R5) and the inequalities of (R4) are numerical. The remaining parts of (R4), (R6) and (R7) are the common-trajectory records; they must hold for the one family and the one indexed field of interfaces, for all \(d\), \(N \geqslant \text{ref} (d)\) and \(p\). For each \((d, N, p)\) one transfer sequence realizing the family's transfer entries is chosen, and that choice is retained throughout all subsequent scale, census and motion tests at those indices. Choosing a different history or interface field for each period or terminal volume is insufficient.

The terminal insertion.
A terminal insertion at \((N, p, d)\) with field \(b\), charge \(Q\), bound \(t\) and budget \(E\) is a coefficient \(Z\) at level \(N\) with the fields of Proposition 13.17: the terminal format; the remainder bound with weight \(\sqrt{w_N}\) and bound \(t\); \(\|Z.x\|_1 + t \leqslant Q\); \(0 < Q\); \(4 (1 + C_N) Q \leqslant E\); an attainment with reserved mass \(B_{\text{term}}\) such that \(B_{\text{term}} \zeta_{d, N} \leqslant u_N E\), together with the forced-profile hook identity; the density equation

\(\displaystyle Z. \text{density} (U) = a_N^d R_N . \text{eval} (U) b (U) ;\)

and continuity of \(b\). Note that \(E > 0\).

Proposition 13.19. (Same-history field calibration) Assume \(C. \text{ref} (d) < N\), that the history has the opening schedule of Subsection 4.4, and that the paid selected-history record (R1)–(R7) holds. Let \(Z\) be a terminal insertion at \((N, p, d)\) with field \(b\), charge \(Q\) and budget \(E\). Then there is one label \(v\) such that

\(\displaystyle L_{N - 1}^{1 / 8} \|Z.x - X_N^{- 1} v\|_1 \leqslant 2 (1 + C_N) Q, \qquad C_N = \text{columns} (N),\)

and, for every \(k\) with \(\text{ref} (d) \leqslant k < N\), every \(m\) opened at \(k\), and every motion \(s\),

\(\displaystyle \text{rootTest}_{m ; N, p} (s \cdot (b - C.O (v))) \leqslant \text{rootFac} (d, m, k) \hspace{0.17em} E \hspace{0.17em} \text{scaleDecay}_{- \gamma} (N, p) .\)

Here \(X_N\) is the terminal coefficient calibration isomorphism on the selected family, and \(C.O (v) = a_N^{- d} T (\theta_N^{- 1} \cdot_F X_N^{- 1} v) (0, \cdot)\) is its local observable at the origin. The label \(v\) and all bounds use the same \(C\), history, and transfer sequence.

Proof. The record (R6) supplies the transfer sequence \(M\) and its Heard and Responds fields from the bottom reference \(r = \text{ref} (d)\). Its transfer witnesses, the transfer records (R7), the budgets (R1), and the terminal insertion bounds are the inputs of Lemma 13.18 with \(l = r\), \(m = N\) and terminal cone parameter \(w = Q\), as specified by the terminal insertion charge bound. The larger budget \(E\) is reserved for the root-test error estimate. This produces one backward sequence of keyed states \((\varpi_i, Z_i, b_i)\), \(r \leqslant i \leqslant N\), on the original history. Let \(a = \varpi_r\) be the primary of its state at \(r\), and set \(v = X_N (a)\). By invertibility, \(X_N^{- 1} v = a\). In the notation of the lemma put \(x_i =\mathcal{Z}_i (- a) .x\) and \(c_i = c_i (- a)\).

The terminal coordinate identity of the lemma and \(\iota_N = \text{id}\) give \(x_N = Z.x + \iota_N (- a) = Z.x - X_N^{- 1} v\). The last item of the lemma gives \(c_N \leqslant 2 (1 + C_N) Q\) and, at the step \(i = N - 1\),

\(\displaystyle L_{N - 1}^{1 / 8} \|x_N \|_1 \leqslant c_N .\)

Combining these inequalities proves the first displayed estimate. The step \(N - 1\) lies in the range of the lemma because \(\text{ref} (d) \leqslant N - 1\), by the explicit assumption \(\text{ref} (d) < N\).

For the root-test estimate fix \(k\) and \(m\) as in the statement, abbreviate \(\zeta_i = \zeta_{d, i}\), and choose

\(\displaystyle w = \frac{\zeta_k / 4}{\| \text{invDil} (H, k, d)\| \hspace{0.17em} \text{redBud} (N, k, E)} .\)

Every factor in the denominator is positive. The height record gives \(\zeta_k > 0\), the physical scale gives \(\| \text{invDil} (H, k, d)\| = a_k^{- d} > 0\), and \(E > 0\) gives positive escrow. Marched weights are \(w_i = w \prod_{q = k}^{i - 1} \phi_q\). The exact recurrence for the physical scale and the gain is

\(\displaystyle \| \text{invDil} (H, i + 1, d)\| = L_i^d \| \text{invDil} (H, i, d)\|, \qquad \text{gain} (N, i + 1) = \text{gain} (N, i) / \lambda_i .\)

Since \(1 \leqslant \lambda_i \leqslant L_i\), the adjacent paid bound implies inductively

\(\displaystyle w_i \| \text{invDil} (H, i, d)\| \frac{E}{\text{gain} (N, i)} \leqslant \frac{\zeta_i}{4} \qquad (i \geqslant k) .\)

The base case is equality at \(k\) by the definition of \(w\). For the step, the left side is multiplied by at most \(\phi_i L_i^{d + 1}\); the first inequality of (R5) pays this by \(\zeta_{i + 1} / \zeta_i\). Thus the exact march/height inequality required for the finite root estimate holds at every later scale.

For the finite-copy construction, at level \(i\) the residual coefficient \(Z_i\) of the state is keyed; the coefficient used in the construction is the translated coefficient family \(\mathcal{Z}_i (- a) = Z_i + \Pi_i (\varpi_i - a)\) with its bound \(c_i = c_i (- a)\). Keyedness of \(Z_i\) and the earned bounds of the transfer sequence transfer format and the remainder bound, with bound \(c_i\), to this translated coefficient family, which need not have zero leading coordinate. The third item of Lemma 13.18 with \(w = Q\), and the terminal insertion inequality \(4 (1 + C_N) Q \leqslant E\), give \(\text{gain} (N, i) (c_i +\|x_i \|_1) \leqslant E\). Combined with the per-level bound inequality for

\(\displaystyle \frac{w_i \| \text{invDil} (H, i, d)\| E}{\text{gain} (N, i)},\)

this gives the finite-copy rate bound \(w_i \| \text{invDil} (H, i, d)\| (c_i +\|x_i \|_1) \leqslant \zeta_i\) by multiplication. The Transfer record's density debt is exactly the density transition in the finite-copy transition identity; its transfer witness supplies the accompanying finite response decomposition support and the forced-profile/exponential-entry comparison. At the terminal level let \(c = (M. \text{tone} \hspace{0.17em} N) . \text{index} (- a)\). The top clause of ‘Sung‘ gives \((M. \text{tone} \hspace{0.17em} N) . \text{index} = \text{id}\), so \(c = - a\); the terminal spread bound gives \(\|c\|_1 \leqslant 2 E\). Put \(j = \| \text{invDil} (H, N, d)\|\) and let \(w_N\) be the terminal bound. The height estimate \(w_N jE \leqslant \zeta_N / 4\) implies

\(\displaystyle 2 w_N j \|c\|_1 \leqslant \zeta_N .\)

The terminal ‘erected‘ estimate, applied at this response cost, supplies an added bound \(A_{\text{ins}}\) with \(w_N jA_{\text{ins}} \leqslant u_N / 2\). If \(B_{\text{term}}\) is the terminal insertion's original attainment bound, the terminal budget inequality

\(\displaystyle B_{\text{term}} \zeta_N \leqslant u_N E,\)

together with \(w_N jE \leqslant \zeta_N / 4\), gives \(w_N jB_{\text{term}} \leqslant u_N / 4 \leqslant u_N / 2\). Hence the combined bound \(A_{\text{ins}} + B_{\text{term}}\) fits the finite-copy allowance \(u_N\). The forced profile is preserved by the terminal insertion hook identity and linearity of the transported forced-profile map. Finally, the finite-copy density identity is the terminal insertion density equation after the terminal ‘originate' identity: the two recentering terms have density equal to the transfer sequence readout, leaving precisely the terminal marked density. These are all same-history records; no new terminal volume or history is selected in assembling these data.

The state residuals \(Z_i\) are keyed. Their translated coefficient families are the finite-copy coefficients; the translated coefficient family at the bottom reference has zero leading coordinate, by the choice of the negative bottom primary. The Accord identities give the Wilson push and all-displacement profile updates; the terminal insertion gives the terminal forced hook and density identity; and the terminal budget and footprint cover pay the forced correction. The output is the exact finite-copy identity for the terminal field \(b + \text{initial} (- a)\), the sum of \(b\) and the original-history insertion with primary \(- a\). Since the terminal calibration is linear,

\(\displaystyle \text{initial} (- a) = - C.O (X_N a) = - C.O (v),\)

so that field is \(b - C.O (v)\). To spell out the finite root estimate, fix \(m\) opened at \(k\) and write \(K = | \text{Site} (m, p) |\) for the number of slots of its census. The finite family estimate needs a site assignment for these slots at every level from \(k\) to \(N\). It is the opened-copy assignment of Lemma 4.9: the slots are embedded by the canonical aligned embeddings, they migrate along the scale embeddings, they are injective, hence of occupancy one, which is at most \(r_k\) by (R3), and distinct slots have separation at least \(B_j^2\) at every level \(j\) between \(k\) and \(N\); at the terminal level these sites are the centers of the copies in the root test. The remaining inputs of the finite family estimate are the monotone occupancy schedule (R3), the scale bounds (R2), the transition service (R4), the height inequalities (R5), and continuity of the field \(b - C.O (v)\), which holds because \(b\) is continuous by the terminal insertion and \(C.O (v)\) is a polynomial cylinder.

Serving the residual polynomial.
We now construct the representation needed for this census. This step uses the full transition and profile records in (R4), (R6), (R7), not only their numerical inequalities. Let \(I\) be its finite set of slots, and use the algebra with commuting generators \(z_{\nu}\), \(\nu \in I\), satisfying \(z_{\nu}^2 = 0\). Write \(F_{\nu}\) for the prescribed oriented copy of \(b - C.O (v)\). All subsets of \(I\) are retained. Define the terminal polynomial

\(\displaystyle A_N (U) = R_N . \text{eval} (U) \prod_{\nu \in I} (1 + z_{\nu} F_{\nu} (U)) . \) (13.13)

In particular its coefficient at any subset \(J \subseteq I\) is \(R_N . \text{eval} \prod_{\nu \in J} F_{\nu}\). We construct local arrays for this polynomial; identifying its singleton coefficients alone would not suffice.

At level \(i\) orient the translated coefficient family \(\mathcal{Z}_i (- a)\) at the site of slot \(\nu\) and multiply it by \(a_i^{- d}\). Let \(g_{i, \nu}\) be its ordinary exponential entry and \(q_{i, \nu}\) its forced profile. Denote its full density by \(\chi_{i, \nu}\). The density decomposition (7.45) expresses \(\chi_{i, \nu}\) as \(R_i . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} g_{i, \nu}\) plus the gas with exactly one insertion of \(q_{i, \nu}\). The format and remainder bounds give summable majorants for these profiles. Linearity, the adjacent density identity, and \(a_{i + 1}^{- d} = L_i^d a_i^{- d}\) give

\(\displaystyle \chi_{i, \nu} = \textsf{P}_i \chi_{i + 1, \nu}, \qquad \chi_{N, \nu} = R_N . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} F_{\nu} .\)

The site migration and the soundness of the same interface justify these identities after orientation as well. A conjugating orientation conjugates all coefficients and responses and preserves their absolute bounds.

The sum of the terminal insertion attainment and the negative played attainment constructed above, rescaled and oriented at slot \(\nu\), is a response \(\Psi_{\nu} (X, U)\) with hook \(q_{N, \nu}\). It is local, gauge invariant, measurable, anchored at that slot, and zero when \(X\) is healthy. Its weighted reserved mass is at most \(u_N\). Set \(h_{\nu} (U) = \sum_X \Psi_{\nu} (X, U)\). The hook identity, or equivalently the singleton coefficient of (7.52), says that the gas with one insertion of \(q_{N, \nu}\) is \(R_N . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} h_{\nu}\). Consequently

\(\displaystyle R_N . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} F_{\nu} = R_N . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} (g_{N, \nu} + h_{\nu}) .\)

Telescoping a finite product proves this identity for every product of slots with the same factor \(R_N . \text{eval}\). Thus it holds without dividing by that factor, even at its zeros. Since the generators have square zero, (13.13) is exactly

\(\displaystyle e^{E_N} \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \sum_{\nu} z_{\nu} g_{N, \nu} \right) \mathcal{G} (k_N^0) \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( \sum_{\nu, X} z_{\nu} \Psi_{\nu} (X, \cdot)), \right.\) (13.14)

where \(R_N . \text{eval} = e^{E_N} \mathcal{G} (k_N^0)\) denotes the actual background gas, with its background ordinary contribution included in \(E_N\).

Apply the connected-component construction of (7.52) to (13.14). Its vertices are the selected background forced factors and the response factors \((\nu, X)\); edges mean intersecting supports. Every nonzero response factor meets a flag, which is covered by a background forced factor, so every component has such a background vertex. Replace each component by the product of its factors, on their union support with their union inventory, and sum components with that output pair and marker mode. Conversely, components with disjoint union supports recover uniquely the original selected factors. This bijection preserves products and all marker modes. The empty-mode array is \(k_N^0\), and the singleton array is the hook \(q_{N, \nu}\): a component with one response consists of that response and the background factors meeting its support. Remove these prescribed modes to define the higher flagged array. The ordinary higher array is zero. In particular we have constructed the higher coefficients rather than replaced them by products of singleton densities.

For convergence and payment, the anchored estimate (7.46) converts the per-slot weighted response mass \(u_N\) into site bound \(r_N \textsf{s} (B_N) u_N\) at exponent \(\alpha_N\): the reserved mass is measured at exponent \(\alpha_N + 2\), so it pays the one extra exponent in that anchored estimate. The background forced bound \(w_N^{\text{f}}\) is measured at \(\alpha_N\). With \(D_N = w_N^{\text{f}} + r_N \textsf{s} (B_N) u_N\), (R4) gives

\(\displaystyle (2 B_N + 1)^4 D_N \leqslant 3, \qquad e^{D_N} - 1 \leqslant 1.\)

The connected-support bound (7.35), with spare exponent three, therefore bounds the flagged array at exponent \(\alpha_N - 3\) by one. Removing modes cannot increase a bound. The same majorants justify the component regrouping for countable background addresses by absolute convergence. This proves terminal servedness with reserve one at pitch \(\pi_N = \alpha_N - 3\).

Here are the adjacent profile inputs to the descent, explicitly in the notation of Subsection 7.8. For each slot at an interface there is a constant \(\ell_{\nu}\), an anchored family of elementary insertions \(M_{\nu j}\) using finitely many slots, and a lower response \(\Psi'_{\nu}\), such that

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rlll} g_{i + 1, \nu} - \ell_{\nu} & = \sum_j z_{\nu j} \text{ord} (M_{\nu j}), & q_{i + 1, \nu} & = \sum_j z_{\nu j} \text{flag} (M_{\nu j}),\\ q_{i, \nu} & = \sum_j z_{\nu j} \mathcal{Q}_i (M_{\nu j}) +\mathcal{H}_{R_i} (\Psi'_{\nu}) . & & \end{array}\)

Here \(\mathcal{Q}_i\) is the same interface's quoted profile map and \(\mathcal{H}_{R_i}\) its lower hook. These are the finite response decomposition identities in (R6)–(R7). To obtain them for the translated coefficient family, take the union of the transfer record insertion support for the keyed upper residual and the transfer witness insertion support for the played displacement \(\varpi_{i + 1} - a\), and add the two expansions. In the lower state the recentering terms cancel:

\(\displaystyle Z_i + \Pi_i (\varpi_i - a) = V + \Pi_i (- h) + \Pi_i (\varpi_{i + 1} + h - a) = V + \Pi_i (\varpi_{i + 1} - a),\)

where \(V, h\) are the one-step correction and primary defined before Lemma 13.18. The cancellation holds for the ordinary entry, density, and forced profile separately. Thus the lower profile in the displayed response decomposition is exactly the required one. Taking a union of insertion supports does not change their interface, supports or parent map; the absolute bounds add. Before physical rescaling their upper bound is at most

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{O}_d \|x_{i + 1} \|_1 (\psi_i + 2 C_{i + 1}) + 2 c_{i + 1} \leqslant \mathcal{O}_d (\psi_i + 2 C_{i + 1}) (c_{i + 1} +\|x_{i + 1} \|_1),\)

since \(\mathcal{O}_d, C_{i + 1} \geqslant 1\) and \(\psi_i \geqslant 0\). More precisely the Transfer record term costs \(2 b_{i + 1} \leqslant 2 c_{i + 1}\). The lower response bound at exponent \(\alpha_i + 2\) is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \eta_i b_{i + 1} + g_i (\varpi_{i + 1} - a) \leqslant \eta_i b_{i + 1} + g_i (- h) + g_i (\varpi_{i + 1} + h - a) = c_i,\)

by subadditivity of the played charge and the defining formula for \(b_i\). After rescaling, call these bounds \(s_{i, \nu}\) and \(t_{i, \nu}\). The required two payments are

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} 2 w_{i + 1} s_{i, \nu} & \leqslant 2 w_{i + 1} a_{i + 1}^{- d} \mathcal{O}_d (\psi_i + 2 C_{i + 1}) (c_{i + 1} +\|x_{i + 1} \|_1) \leqslant 1,\\ w_{i + 1} t_{i, \nu} & \leqslant w_{i + 1} a_i^{- d} c_i \leqslant \zeta_i C_i \leqslant 1. \end{aligned}\)

These follow from the rate bounds, (R5), \(w_{i + 1} \leqslant w_i\), and \(C_i \geqslant 1\). Both profiles and the upper exponential entry are therefore paid; a bound on only one profile would not establish this transition.

Now define \(A_i = \textsf{P}_i A_{i + 1}\), acting coefficientwise, and \(\pi_i = \alpha_i\) for \(i < N\). Suppose \(A_{i + 1}\) has the constructed representation at pitch \(\pi_{i + 1}\) and reserve one. In particular \(\pi_{i + 1} \geqslant \alpha_{i + 1} - 4\), including the terminal step. Apply Subsection 7.8 with upper weights \(w_{i + 1}\) and shrinkage \(\delta_i\). The response decomposition just constructed gives singleton cost \(\delta_i\) after real/imaginary compression, while the higher arrays cost at most \(4 \delta_i\). Occupancy gives total prepared site cost \(T = r_i \textsf{s} (B_{i + 1}) \delta_i + 4 \delta_i\). The compatible-product expansion costs at most \(T + e^T T \leqslant y\). Hence (R4) applies to this entire prepared polynomial, including every higher mode. Its ordinary and flagged output bounds and \((2 B_i + 1)^4 c \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}\), \(e^c - 1 \leqslant 1\) permit the lower response closure at exponent \(\alpha_i + \tfrac{1}{2}\). The exact pushed singleton density and the lower hook identify the prescribed \(g_{i, \nu}\) and \(q_{i, \nu}\) by (7.45), using the nonzero selected Wilson density at this scale, whose strict positivity is proved in Proposition 13.34.

Finally, the separation estimate in Subsection 4.2 gives, for each supported mode \(J\) with \(|J| \geqslant 2\),

\(\displaystyle \prod_{\nu \in J} w_i \leqslant \prod_{\nu \in J} (\delta_i w_{i + 1}) e^{\text{coverNumber} (B_i, X) / 2} .\)

Indeed \(w_i \leqslant \delta_i w_{i + 1} e^{B_i / 128}\) by (R2), and the tour bound is \(B_i |J| \leqslant 64 \text{coverNumber} (B_i, X)\). This pays the change back to \(w_i\) and leaves exponent \(\alpha_i\) and reserve one. It completes the induction down to \(k\). Higher modes throughout are those of \(A_i\), never reset to products of the single-insertion pushes.

The terminal coefficients are integrable, since the fields are continuous on the finite-volume compact configuration space. The bounded measurable transition kernels preserve integrability; Fubini and their mass identity preserve every coefficient integral and the common partition normalization. Therefore, for every \(J \subseteq I\),

\(\displaystyle \frac{\int [z_J] A_k}{\int R_k . \text{eval}} = \int \prod_{\nu \in J} F_{\nu} \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{N, p} .\)

All integrals on the left use the respective normalized priors. In particular the full mode is the original residual census moment. We have proved the required served representation at \(k < N\) with exponent \(\alpha_k\) and reserve one. With constant positive slot weight \(w\), the finite family estimate (obtained by applying the marked estimate to the product over all \(K\) slots) is

\(\displaystyle \| \Omega \| w^K \leqslant \exp \hspace{-0.17em} \left( | \text{Site} (k, p) | \hspace{0.17em} \text{vacuumBound}_k + 5 K \right), \qquad \Omega = \int \prod_{j \in \text{Site} (m, p)} U_j \hspace{0.17em} d \mu_{N, p} .\)

Here \(U_j\) is the \(j\)th translated/reflected census copy, with the chosen motion applied, of the residual field \(b - C.O (v)\). Thus \(\Omega\) is exactly the moment in the definition of the displayed root test. For each slot, \(x_k\) is the leading coefficient of its translated coefficient family \(\mathcal{Z}_k (- a)\) and \(c_k\) its bound, as above, and the marched weight has \(w_k = w\). The rate clause at the opened scale \(k\) is \(w_k \| \text{invDil} (H, k, d)\| (c_k +\|x_k \|_1) \leqslant \zeta_{d, k}\), and \(\zeta_{d, k} C_k \leqslant 1\) by (R5). Hence

\(\displaystyle w_k \| \text{invDil} (H, k, d)\| C_k (c_k +\|x_k \|_1) \leqslant 1, \qquad C_k = \text{columns} (k) \geqslant 1.\)

The local hull estimate bounds the magnitude of its exponential entry by \((1 + C_k) \|x_k \|_1 + c_k\): the unit coordinate costs at most \(\|x_k \|_1\), the masked core section at most \(C_k \|x_k \|_1\) by the selected-family bound clause, and the masked regular part at most the bound \(c_k\). Since \((1 + C_k) \|x_k \|_1 + c_k \leqslant 2 C_k (c_k +\|x_k \|_1)\), the weighted entry bound is at most \(2\). The forced profiles have total modulus at most \(c_k\), so the weighted total forced bound is at most \(1\). The action allowance is \(f = 1\). Thus the marked estimate (4.10) of Subsection 4.2 charges \(x + y + 2 f = 5\) per slot (the more general choice \(x = 3, y = 1\) gives the \(6\)-unit bound). The constant \(5 K\) is exactly the sum of the per-slot tolls, and the extensive term is the cost of all scale-\(k\) sites. The aligned side-count identity is

\(\displaystyle | \text{Site} (k, p) | = K (a_m / a_k)^4 .\)

Consequently, with \(g = (a_m / a_k)^4 \text{vacuumBound}_k + 5\), we have \(\| \Omega \| w^K \leqslant e^{Kg}\). If \(\Omega = 0\) the desired inequality is immediate; otherwise the same inequality still gives \(\| \Omega \|^{1 / K} \leqslant e^g / w\). By definition the normalized census root test is \(\| \Omega \|^{1 / K}\), hence

\(\displaystyle \text{rootTest}_{m ; N, p} (s \cdot (b - C.O (v))) \leqslant \frac{\text{volFac} (m, k)}{w} .\)

Finally, the definitions of the reduced budget, the scale decay and the volume factor convert this ratio exactly:

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \frac{\text{volFac} (m, k)}{w} & = \text{volFac} (m, k) \frac{4 \| \text{invDil} (H, k, d)\|}{\zeta_k} \text{redBud} (N, k, E)\\ & = \text{rootFac} (d, m, k) E \text{scaleDecay}_{- \gamma} (N, p) . \end{aligned}\)

The bound holds for every \(k\), \(m\) and motion in the stated ranges, because the record (R1)–(R7) and the terminal insertion do not depend on them. No subsequence or replacement history enters the construction.\(\Box\)

Boundary of this producer.
The proof above expands the terminal recentering, finite backward induction, cost conversion, marched-weight calculation, and the final use of the finite-copy estimate. It does not derive the selected history's coefficient map and transfer record producer, its all-volume height ledger, or the selected-history binding of the terminal insertion. Those exact suppliers are listed separately in the evidence record; none follows merely from the existence of a family of fixed marked sections. The precise remaining certificate is the record (R1)–(R7); on one fixed family and one fixed field of adjacent gauge interfaces, its same-history part (R6)–(R7) reads as follows. For each degree \(d\), terminal level \(N \geqslant \text{ref} (d)\), and period \(p\), it must provide a transfer sequence whose terminal coefficient family is the chosen factory representative, whose played fields realize the factory entries, which is admissible at every level from \(\text{ref} (d)\) to \(N\), and which responds across each intervening interface. The same transfer sequence must have transfer witnesses at all those interfaces and the terminal forced-profile hook. For every \(i \geqslant \text{ref} (d)\) it must also provide the paid local transfer record and its transfer witness for every displacement comparison, with the uniform height, room, bank, Brine and monotone occupancy bounds used above. These are same-family, same-history quantifiers: the transfer sequence is chosen for each \((d, N, p)\) and then retained through all tests at those indices, whereas choosing a different history or interface field for each period or terminal volume is insufficient.

The nested field and adjacent comparisons are supplied by Propositions 13.31, 13.34 and 13.35; the zero-coordinate response is Proposition 13.36. Subsection 13.10 identifies the height, room, bank and occupancy bounds in (R1)–(R7). The terminal insertion attachment uses Proposition 13.15, including its reserved-address, lawfulness and fee hypotheses. All these applications retain the same original funded history as in Proposition 13.1; none permits a new history to be chosen for each cutoff or period.

13.10The common history and its centered service

We now apply the local constructions to a single family. The input is the simultaneous purchase of Sections 13.1 and 13.4, including the stationary and free convergence estimates on their common horizon. We retain the prescribed positive blocking penalty throughout.

Choose nondecreasing profile orders and spatial powers, both at least the stage index, and nondecreasing support exponents at least \(100\). The particular choices \(6 + j\), \(2 + j\) and \(100 + j\) suffice for the elementary construction. The selected public run uses the first stage \(4\), the orders \(6 + j\), the powers \(2 + j\), and the constant support exponent \(b_j = 100\) at every stage; the identity \(b_j = 100\) is retained with that run and is required by later consumers. Throughout this section the public run \(R\) is retained, including its selected shooting parameters, its reference schedule \(t_R\), its transition records and its transfer family. The following paragraphs unpack the construction and the estimates of these retained witnesses; where they speak of choosing an object, the object meant is the one stored in \(R\), and no second choice is made. Choose the fixed parameter radius and the small response reserve as in Subsection 13.4. Make the block-length choices before their numerical costs. Apply Subsection 19.2 to all the resulting requests, with a nondecreasing occupancy schedule. This gives one sequence of levels, couplings and periods, with \(J (m) \to \infty\).

13.10.1Choosing the history before its observable comparisons

For every cutoff \(N\) and period \(p\), the terminal family is the exact Wilson family of Subsection 12.15. The interval return and scalar reset are applied with the strengthened local conditions of Subsection 13.4. Thus every member of the resulting continuous interval family carries those conditions. To prove that its bottom couplings cover the required interval, forget only these additional conditions and apply the barrier and covering argument of Subsection 12.13. This leaves the interval, its endpoints and every replayed history unchanged. Choose a parameter in that very interval whose bottom coupling is the prescribed common value, and retain its original strengthened certificate. Retain that member, its actual representations \(R_{N, p, m}\) and its adjacent transitions. All of them use the already purchased paths, kernels and clipping data. Choosing the members for the countably many pairs \((N, p)\) introduces no new scale request. On the run \(R\) these are stored data: for every \((N, p)\) the run stores the interval family, a parameter in its interval, and the identity of its history at \((N, p)\) with the replay of that family at that parameter. Its nesting receipt records that every adjacent pair of this history is a protected and accurate transition of the purchased step and that the terminal representative carries the reserved addresses.

The original certificate at the chosen parameter supplies the local parameter estimates on these histories. Thus the comparisons and fixed-level limits in Sections 12.8, 14.21 and 10.13 apply to this family. The uniform local derivative bank is that of Subsection 13.4. In particular, the fixed-level convergence is available for the actual selected activity arrays, not just for their stationary paths.

This application carries one input that the certificate of the selected member does not by itself provide. Subsection 12.8 compares the selected history with auxiliary truncated histories, which have a prescribed coupling script and zero terminal activity, and Subsection 14.21 draws its limits under the assumption that the transition hypotheses of Subsection 9.10 hold for the actual input of each such auxiliary history at every step, on the same path and dictionaries. The certificate at the chosen parameter concerns the selected history only. The fixed-level limits are therefore used here subject to that same assumption on the auxiliary histories; it is not proved in this section.

Put \(t_d = t_R (d)\), the reference level stored in the run for the grade \(d\). The public construction supplies

\(\displaystyle 1 \leqslant t_d, \qquad d + 3 \leqslant J (t_d) .\)

Such levels exist by divergence of \(J\); no monotonicity of \(d \mapsto t_d\) is recorded or used. At each adjacent step choose the insertion support containing the origin principal modes and the keeper keys for all relevant support/inventory shapes. These keys form a finite set: the torus is finite, so its support powerset and the pairs \(I \subseteq X\) with \(I \ne \varnothing\) are finite. The insertion support uses these shapes, not the countably many reserved serial addresses. Hence it covers every forced coefficient while remaining finite. Take the finite union required for the admitted grades. Subsection 10.9 supplies one heading and one local transfer map for that entire insertion support. Consequently the same map works for all those grades. Enlarging the insertion support preserves its finite interpolation proof and the exact endpoint representation.

Apply the finite backward construction of Subsection 4.3, starting at the terminal marked section constructed in Subsection 13.7. This gives the finite transfer data at every grade, cutoff and period. The three exact identities in (10.45) identify the coefficient, exponential entry and forced profile throughout the construction. In particular, its terminal density is \(a_N^d R_{N, p, N} (U) \hspace{0.17em} \text{initial}_{N, p} (v) (U)\); the physical observable \(\text{initial}_{N, p}\) already contains the factor \(a_N^{- d}\). The local maps used in this construction are the adjacent maps already chosen above; the downward induction does not select another background history. The unit, gauge invariance and signed-motion identities are preserved by the exact local section and by the equivariant finite assembly. Thus these transfer data form one family on the retained history. On the run \(R\) this family is the stored transfer family \(S_R\), and the adjacent maps are its stored transition records; for \(t_d > N\) the stored transfer datum is a default map and carries no assertion, as explained in the proof of Proposition 13.1.

13.10.2Retention estimates on the fixed interfaces

Write \(x_i = \hat{\beta}_i\), \(\alpha_i\) for the support exponent, \(B_i = B (x_i)\), and \(r_i = N_{J (i + 1)}\). Set

\(\displaystyle y_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 1}, \qquad \delta_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 2}, \qquad u_i = \tfrac{1}{4} x_i^{- 1 / 2} .\)

Use \(w_i^{\text{f}}\) for the history's forced allowance and \(w_i\) for the distinct flagged response bound of the tariff at step \(i\).

Proposition 13.20. (Retention and terminal endowment on the selected history) Keep the selected replay, nesting and interfaces above, and the fixed profile and purchased entrance delays. At each stored interval point assume the arrival, ordinary and raised-response data of Subsection 3.16 are available on the retained representations and interface. Assume also the following preservation property of the retained return: for each nonterminal selected entry, its output dictionary has zero regular addresses for nonempty supports and eligible forced addresses for every nonempty inventory contained in its support. Eligibility here is required whenever the actual flags realize the inventory. Assume the delays include the activity thresholds of Proposition 3.39 for these targets and the thresholds of Theorem 4.13. Then for every \(N, p\) and \(0 \leqslant i < N\) the fixed interface has the following paid response. Its input is any finite priced offer of nonempty modes with positive marker weights, fine sites in the offered supports, and parent-site occupancy at most \(r_i\). The site-rooted input bound is at most \(y_i\) at exponent \(\alpha_{i + 1} - 4\). Its ordinary and flagged output families have the support and flag conditions and per-mode majorants in the multi-marker offer of Subsection 13.9. Their rooted weighted bounds have bounds \(1\) and \(w_i\) at exponents \(\alpha_i + 1 / 2\) and \(\alpha_i + 1\), respectively. The assembled density is the exact push of the offered polynomial. In each flagged box the quiet coefficient is the lower ordinary activity profile and the singleton coefficient is the sum of the corresponding offered singleton profiles transported by this interface. The interface is sound and tuned, and the upper contact addresses are retained. For the same \(y_i, w_i\),

\(\displaystyle 0 < \delta_i \leqslant 1, \qquad \alpha_i, \alpha_{i + 1} \geqslant 100, \qquad T_i + e^{T_i} T_i \leqslant y_i, \qquad (2 B_i + 1)^4 c_i \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}, \qquad e^{c_i} - 1 \leqslant 1,\)

where \(T_i = r_i \textsf{s} (B_{i + 1}) \delta_i + 4 \delta_i\), \(c_i = w_i + r_i \textsf{s} (B_i) \delta_i\) and \(\textsf{s} (B) = e \{2 (16 B + 9)\}^4\).

At every terminal level \(N \geqslant 0\) the same schedule gives

\(\displaystyle u_N \geqslant 0, \qquad \alpha_N \geqslant 3, \qquad (2 B_N + 1)^4 C_N^{\text{end}} \leqslant 3, \qquad e^{C_N^{\text{end}}} - 1 \leqslant 1, \qquad C_N^{\text{end}} = w_N^{\text{f}} + r_N \textsf{s} (B_N) u_N .\)

Proof. For fixed \(N, p, i\), take the interval witness of the stored step. Its upper representation is the interval input at the retained point and its lower representation the return at that same point. The interface is the one-step transport at that point, with those two endpoint identities substituted. Thus replacing the local representations by the selected entries in the response formulas changes neither the operator on activities nor the dictionary. At the top, Proposition 12.6 supplies the regular and forced addresses on the selected Wilson representative. At any lower entry the preservation property applies to the return of the selected step above it. Hence the upper entry of every interface has these addresses, including when that entry is terminal. For a nonempty support take its zero regular address. For each nonempty inventory take an eligible forced address at that support, using eligibility on the configurations whose flags realize the inventory.

At this step the entrance stage is \(J (i + 1)\), the target stage is \(J (i)\), and the coupling is \(x_i\). The selected clock gives \(x_{i + 1} = x_i + c_G \log L_i\) and the purchased entrance bound at \(x_i\). The tariff therefore applies at the retained occupancy \(r_i\). Its shipment inequality converts the site-rooted offer at the upper exponent into the rooted birth-region bound, using the parent map and the occupancy bound, as in Theorem 3.33. The ordinary response uses the ordinary component budget and the raised response uses the separately paid flagged budget. The latter has bound \(w_i\) from the same tariff. Applying the finite-menu handoff to the retained response data gives the two grouped ledgers. For a fixed mode, sum the configuration-independent absolute majorants over entries of its output support or box. The triangle inequality bounds its grouped coefficient by that sum. Since the mode weights are nonnegative and unchanged by grouping, the weighted sum of these majorants is bounded by the corresponding grouped support bound. The support restrictions, quiet and singleton identities, and exact push are the grouping identities of Theorem 3.33. The finite-menu handoff gives soundness and compatibility with grouped sums for this same one-leaf transport. This uses the purchased response data for every priced offer; it does not select a new interface when the offer changes. The probe, purchase, lodging and credit inequalities of the tariff are precisely the displayed inequalities for \(\delta_i, T_i, c_i\) after the fine-coupling substitution. In particular the allowance used to buy \(T_i + e^{T_i} T_i\) is the input bound of the very same response.

For the terminal assertion, apply (4.17) at \(x = x_N\) with stages \(J (N)\) and \(J (N + 1)\). This is a numerical assertion about the global schedule, so it uses the purchased next entrance inequality even when the finite history itself stops at \(N\). The selected allowance is \(w_N^{\text{f}} = \text{forcedReserve} (x_N)\) and the other term in that bound is \(N_{J (N + 1)} \textsf{s} (B_N) s (x_N)\), which equals \(r_N \textsf{s} (B_N) u_N\). The resulting two inequalities give the claimed endowment. Positivity of \(x_N\) and \(\alpha_N \geqslant 100\) give the remaining two assertions. This argument precedes the choice of a terminal label and needs no terminal coefficient family, including at \(N = 0\).\(\Box\)

The proposition concerns the fixed interfaces of the public run, including its step at level zero when \(N > 0\). The per-marked lifting service projection uses the marked lifting's positive-level interfaces. Its retention assertion is restricted to \(1 \leqslant k \leqslant i < N\); no response at its default zero-level interface is required. In either application the terminal endowment depends only on the schedule. The preservation of the required slots by the retained return is proved in Lemma 13.21 below. The arrival and raised-response receipts on the selected replay are supplied in Theorem 13.30, from the local estimates which the common purchases pay on the stored steps.

Lemma 13.21. (Reserved slots on every selected entry) Keep the selected history. For every \(N, p\) and every \(0 \leqslant m \leqslant N\), the stored representation \(R_{N, p, m}\) is written in the dictionary given by the fixed construction of Proposition 12.6, applied on the level-\(m\) torus at the level-\(m\) budget point. Its coefficients on the reserved labels of that dictionary vanish. Precisely:

  1. for every regular address, that is, a site together with a nonempty support containing it, and every natural number, the regular coefficient of \(R_{N, p, m}\) on the corresponding reserved regular label is zero;

  2. for every support \(X\), every nonempty inventory \(I \subseteq X\) and every natural number \(n\), the forced coefficient of \(R_{N, p, m}\) on the corresponding reserved forced label is zero. That label has support \(X\) and inventory \(I\). Its additional predicate holds at every configuration \(U\) with \(X \cap \text{flags} (U) = I\), where the flags are taken at the threshold of level \(m\).

In particular, the preservation property assumed in Proposition 13.20 holds for every nonterminal selected entry. Every stored representation, terminal or not, has a Keeper in the sense of the terminal Keeper of Subsection 13.8. That is an injective assignment, to every triple \((X, I, n)\) with \(\varnothing \ne I \subseteq X\) and \(n \in \mathbb{N}\), of a forced address with zero coefficient, support \(X\) and inventory \(I\), eligible whenever \(X \cap \text{flags} (U) = I\).

Proof. For \(m = N\), the stored top representation is a member \(F_{\beta}\) of the exact startup family, by the choice of the history at the beginning of this section. Proposition 12.6 gives (1) and (2) for \(F_{\beta}\).

Let \(m < N\). By the stored identities recalled in the proof of Proposition 13.20, \(R_{N, p, m}\) is the output of the interval return of step \(m\) at the retained interval point. That output lives on the level-\(m\) torus. It is obtained from the return's batch by the fixed packing and absorption (Subsection 12.11; the batch is that of Subsection 12.10). This is the same packing and grouping as in the startup, now applied on the level-\(m\) torus at the level-\(m\) budget point.

The proof of Proposition 12.6 shows that the vanishing on reserved labels is a property of this packing and grouping. It holds for every torus, budget point and coefficient family to which they are applied:

  • The packing injection takes values among the unreserved labels, and every entry outside its image is zero.

  • A reserved forced label is the group formed by one reserved hard label alone. Its activity is therefore that packed hard coefficient, times indicators, and this coefficient is zero.

  • In the return, grouped labels may also contain logarithmic labels, which are absent at the startup. This does not affect the reserved singletons, which contain no other label.

  • Every other grouped entry outside the image of the packed family is zero.

Applying this to the renewed arrays of the return gives (1) and (2) for \(R_{N, p, m}\).

The support, inventory and additional predicate of a reserved label are data of the dictionary, not of the coefficients. They are given by the same construction as for the startup family, on the level-\(m\) torus. The predicate is the flag condition at the level-\(m\) threshold, by the construction recalled in the first subsection of Subsection 13.7.

For the preservation property, take any regular address, that is, a site in a nonempty support. Its reserved regular label is a regular address of \(R_{N, p, m}\) with that site and support and zero coefficient. For a nonempty inventory \(I \subseteq X\) and every \(n \in \mathbb{N}\), the reserved forced label of \((X, I, n)\) is a forced address with support \(X\), inventory \(I\) and zero coefficient. That address is eligible whenever the actual flags in \(X\) realize \(I\). Distinct triples have distinct reserved labels, so \((X, I, n)\) is mapped injectively. This is the asserted Keeper. Restricting to \(n = 0\) gives the assignment on pairs used in Proposition 13.36.\(\Box\)

13.10.3The menu on the actual component alphabet

The shipment bound must pay the whole finite menu, including several entries assigned to the same elementary activity. We record this step before applying either response estimate. Fix a fixed interface and its underlying mixed alphabet from Subsection 8.2. An optional label \(a\) has nonnegative bound \(v_a\), birth support \(X_a\) and nonnegative length charge \(d_a\). The mandatory packets and their bounds are kept fixed. Let \(\mathcal{I}\) be the finite menu, let \(\sigma (\lambda)\) be the optional label modified by entry \(\lambda\). Write \(r_{\lambda} \geqslant 0\) for its menu fee and \(\ell_{\lambda} \geqslant 0\) for its leaf weight: the normalized local insertion \(h_{\lambda}\) satisfies the pointwise bound \(|h_{\lambda} | \leqslant \ell_{\lambda}\). Its wage is \(w_{\lambda} = r_{\lambda} \ell_{\lambda}\). For this label the length charge is the fine crest cover number, and its birth support is the coarse birth region used in (3.78). Define

\(\displaystyle v_a^{\mathcal{I}} = v_a + \sum_{\lambda : \hspace{0.17em} \sigma (\lambda) = a} w_{\lambda} .\) (13.15)

The assignment \(\sigma\) need not be injective.

Lemma 13.22. (Finite menus preserve the mixed component estimate) Suppose the unmarked packet and optional bounds satisfy the hypotheses of the mixed component estimate of Subsection 8.2, at its chosen head exponent \(q\) and positive length reserve \(\varepsilon\). For its application to the switched integrals, retain the common mixed-body estimate as well: after any admitted set of optional labels is removed, the integral of the absolute residual body is bounded by the product of the remaining optional bounds and the unchanged packet bounds, against the same Gaussian and Haar measures. Suppose also that the shipment conversion for the menu gives

\(\displaystyle \sup_z \sum_{\lambda : \hspace{0.17em} z \in X_{\sigma (\lambda)}} w_{\lambda} e^{2 \varepsilon d_{\sigma (\lambda)}} e^{qC_B (X_{\sigma (\lambda)})} \leqslant \mathcal{R}.\) (13.16)

If the unmarked optional site bound is \(\Gamma_o\), the augmented alphabet has optional site bound at most \(\Gamma_o +\mathcal{R}\). Its packet bounds are unchanged. Thus the mixed component estimate applies with total vertex bound \(\gamma' = \Gamma_p + \Gamma_o +\mathcal{R}\) whenever its tree smallness condition holds at \(\gamma'\).

For every finite component \(A\) of the optional alphabet, its augmented product is exactly

\(\displaystyle \prod_{a \in A} v_a^{\mathcal{I}} = \sum_{\text{\scriptsize{$\begin{array}{c} T \subseteq \mathcal{I}: \sigma |_T \text{injective}\\ \sigma (T) \subseteq A \end{array}$}}} \left( \prod_{\lambda \in T} w_{\lambda} \right) \left( \prod_{a \in A \setminus \sigma (T)} v_a \right) .\) (13.17)

Consequently the component bound also pays the sum over all admitted menu replacements, with no factor depending on the cardinality of \(\mathcal{I}\). In the selected shipment application, \(q = Q. \text{relative}\), \(\varepsilon = \varepsilon_Q\) and \(\mathcal{R}= \text{activityBound} (Q, L, x)\).

Proof. Insert (13.15) into the weighted optional site sum. The new part at a site \(z\) is

\(\displaystyle \sum_{a : z \in X_a} \sum_{\lambda : \sigma (\lambda) = a} w_{\lambda} e^{2 \varepsilon d_a} e^{qC_B (X_a)} = \sum_{\lambda : z \in X_{\sigma (\lambda)}} w_{\lambda} e^{2 \varepsilon d_{\sigma (\lambda)}} e^{qC_B (X_{\sigma (\lambda)})} .\)

The menu is finite and all terms are nonnegative, so this regrouping is valid even for a countable optional alphabet. The asserted site bound follows by addition. The packet population and its analytic weight estimate have not changed. Apply the combination of vertex populations and the reserved-tail estimate (8.10) with this new optional bound and the same supports, lengths, seed marks and positive reserves.

To prove (13.17), expand each factor of the finite product. At label \(a\) one chooses either \(v_a\) or one of the wages with \(\sigma (\lambda) = a\). The chosen menu entries therefore form a subset \(T\) on which \(\sigma\) is injective. Conversely, such a \(T\) specifies exactly one choice at every occupied label, and the unmarked choice at every remaining label. This is a bijection, including when several menu entries have the same image. Multiplying the corresponding factors proves the identity. Any restriction to admitted components or replacements decreases its nonnegative majorant. For the actual switched component, let \(\mathcal{B}\) denote its remaining body after the labels in \(\sigma (T)\) have been replaced. Before integration, the pointwise leaf bounds give

\(\displaystyle \left| \prod_{\lambda \in T} r_{\lambda} h_{\lambda} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{B} \right| \leqslant \left( \prod_{\lambda \in T} w_{\lambda} \right) |\mathcal{B}| .\)

Integrate against the same positive Gaussian and Haar measures and use the unmarked mixed-body estimate for the remaining labels, with its unchanged mandatory packets. The forest and tag averages are probability averages, so they preserve this bound. Thus each replacement has precisely the product majorant in (13.17). This argument uses pointwise insertion bounds and a body estimate before summing components; it does not multiply bounds for separate integrals. All replacement choices are summed before the rooted estimate is applied.

Finally, the shipment-conversion part of the proof of Theorem 3.33 already proves (13.16): its \(f_{\lambda}\) is \(w_{\lambda} e^{2 \varepsilon_Q n_{\lambda}^{\text{crest}}}\) and \(d_{\sigma (\lambda)} = n_{\lambda}^{\text{crest}}\). In particular the length reserve is included in the shipment, not added afterwards. That part of the proof uses only the offer, its parent occupancy, the birth geometry and the tariff; it does not use the paid output terms assumed for the subsequent response assembly. It can therefore be used here before those terms have been constructed. The named origin condition follows from nonempty menu modes and the inclusion of their fine sites in their crests. This proves the stated application on the same elementary alphabet as the fixed interface.\(\Box\)

13.10.4Uniform payment of the two response rows

We separate the numerical part of the arrival hypothesis from the construction of the component majorants. This distinction is useful because the ordinary and raised rows have different soft bounds, even though they have the same final allowance.

Fix an allowed pair of stages \((j, h)\), its block side \(L = L_j\), and its positive strength \(\mu = \mu_{j, h}\). For a coarse coupling \(x\) put

\(\displaystyle y = x + s_G \log L, \quad B = \lceil (\log x)^2 \rceil, \quad P = \exp ((\log \log y)^2), \quad H_1 = 2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 8}, \quad H_2 = 2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 16} .\)

Let \(C_{\text{cov}} = e^{\chi_0} \geqslant 1\) be the fixed cover enlargement factor in Subsubsection 3.13.2. The two final soft demands \(f_0 (x, \alpha)\) and \(f_+ (x, \alpha)\) are, respectively, (3.82) and (3.83), evaluated with the profile at stage \(h\), radius \(B\), threshold \(\theta (x) = x^{- 1 / 2} \exp ((\log \log x)^2)\), and regular allowance \(c_h x^{- 11 / 10}\), where \(0 < c_h \leqslant 1 / 64\). Both are evaluated at the base budget point; the second pays the receipt at the raised point, whose radius is still \(B\). Write \(A_j\) for the fixed bound on the absolute value of the reset parameter after \(L_j\) is chosen.

Lemma 13.23. (Simultaneous numerical response payment) There is a threshold \(d_{j, h}\), independent of the period, cutoff, input representation and finite menu, with the following properties for every \(x \geqslant d_{j, h}\). For any \(a, b \geqslant 0\) with

\(\displaystyle a \leqslant e^{- \mu P^2 / 4}, \qquad b \leqslant C_{\text{cov}} y^{- 1 / 8},\)

set \(\kappa_1 = \mu P^2 / 8\) and \(I_1 = e^{\kappa_1} a + 2 b\). Then

\(\displaystyle b \leqslant 1, \qquad (2 B + 1)^4 I_1 \leqslant 1, \qquad e^{- \kappa_1} (e^{I_1} - 1) \leqslant H_1 .\) (13.18)

For every \(| \alpha | \leqslant A_j\) and \(\epsilon \in \{0, +\}\), put \(\kappa_2 = \mu P^2 / 16\) and \(I_{2, \epsilon} = e^{\kappa_2} H_1 + 2 f_{\epsilon} (x, \alpha)\). Then

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} 0 \leqslant f_{\epsilon} (x, \alpha) \leqslant y^{- 1 / 4} \leqslant 1, \qquad (2 B + 1)^4 I_{2, \epsilon} \leqslant 1,\\ e^{- \kappa_2} (e^{I_{2, \epsilon}} - 1) \leqslant H_2 . \hspace{3cm} \text{(13.19)} \end{array}\)

These statements can be included in the common delays before the history is selected. They then hold at every stored step \(i < N\), with \((j, h) = (J (i + 1), J (i))\) and \(x = \hat{\beta}_i\), including the direct stored step at \(i = 0\).

Proof. The reset and path bounds defining both soft demands are nonnegative. The footprint calculation (19.2), applied separately to the two reserves, gives constants \(C_0, C_+ \geqslant 0\) such that, for \(| \alpha | \leqslant A_j\),

\(\displaystyle 0 \leqslant f_{\epsilon} (x, \alpha) \leqslant C_{\epsilon} (1 + B)^4 \theta (x) .\)

The hypotheses used there hold eventually: \(\theta \leqslant 1\) and \(c_h x^{- 11 / 10} \leqslant x^{- 1} \leqslant \theta^2\). The base and raised demands have different constants, but the same bound on \(\alpha\) and the same \(\theta, B\). Since \(y / x \to 1\), the logarithm of \((1 + B)^4 \theta (x) y^{1 / 4}\) tends to minus infinity. Indeed its nonconstant terms are \(4 \log (1 + B) + (\log \log x)^2 - \frac{1}{4} \log x + o (1)\). Taking the maximum of the two thresholds proves the claimed \(y^{- 1 / 4}\) bound simultaneously for both demands.

Write \(b_B = (2 B + 1)^4\). We have

\(\displaystyle I_1 \leqslant e^{- \mu P^2 / 8} + 2 C_{\text{cov}} y^{- 1 / 8}, \qquad I_{2, \epsilon} \leqslant 2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 16} + 2 y^{- 1 / 4} .\)

Each right side, multiplied by \(b_B\), tends to zero. For the exponential terms this follows from \(P^2 / \log y \to \infty\); for the power terms it follows from \(B = O ((\log y)^2)\). Increase one threshold until both products are at most one and \(C_{\text{cov}} y^{- 1 / 8} \leqslant 1\). Every corresponding \(I\) lies in \([0, 1]\). On this interval \(e^I - 1 \leqslant (e - 1) I \leqslant 2\). Multiplying by \(e^{- \kappa_1}\) or \(e^{- \kappa_2}\) proves the two output estimates. In particular, the constants \(\kappa_1\) and \(\kappa_2\) work for every admitted precursor and every reset parameter, rather than being selected after a menu is tested.

At stage \(j\) there are only finitely many permitted targets \(h\). Take the maximum of their thresholds and the previously purchased delay, then its running maximum in \(j\). All the inequalities hold on tails, so this operation preserves them and all previous purchases. This is an instance of (19.4) in the choice order of Subsection 13.4. It is made before the scale sequence and shooting parameter are selected, not by altering an already selected history. The stored clock pays this delay at \(\hat{\beta}_i\) for the permitted pair \((J (i + 1), J (i))\). Its fine coupling is exactly \(\hat{\beta}_{i + 1} = \hat{\beta}_i + s_G \log L_i\). The stored reset parameter obeys the same stage bound \(A_{J (i + 1)}\) by the interval-return construction of Subsection 13.1. Substitution proves the last assertion for every admitted cutoff and period. No positive lower bound on \(i\) was used.\(\Box\)

For the precursor in Subsubsection 3.13.2, \(a = 2 V_Q e^{- E_0}\), with \(V_Q \leqslant 1 / 2\) and \(E_0 = \mu P^2 / 4\). Its ordinary soft bound is at most \(y^{- 1 / 8}\), whereas its raised soft bound is at most \(C_{\text{cov}} y^{- 1 / 8}\). Thus the first payment covers both paths. After their respective widening and absorption, the second payment applies to \(H_1\) and their respective final demands. It gives exactly \(H_2\), the flagged response allowance \(w_i\) of Proposition 13.20. The argument does not substitute \(f_0\) for \(f_+\) or change the budget at which \(f_+\) is evaluated.

The remaining analytic attachment.
The two lemmas above supply the finite-menu augmentation and the four numerical absorption bounds. The common mixed-body estimate retained in Lemma 13.22 is not an independent input: it is derived in Lemma 13.24 below from the simultaneous product estimate of Subsection 8.3, and so reduces to the local hypotheses of that estimate on the held tree sectors. The lemmas do not supply those local hypotheses, or the actual local component and differentiated entrance estimates used in Subsubsection 3.13.1. In particular that section requires the rooted thin-component majorant at its full exponent and the corresponding tree smallness bound before applying its cluster estimate. The precursor and vapor estimates in Subsubsection 3.13.2 must concern the same residual bodies, local return and profile, with the two distinct soft demands. The packet estimates include all analytic variants together. These are analytic inputs on the held local step, not additional consequences of increasing the delays. The following subsections match them to their uniform local producers on the stored steps, and Theorem 13.30 then removes the arrival and raised-response hypothesis of Proposition 13.20.

13.10.5The residual body on the actual alphabet

We show that the body hypothesis of Lemma 13.22 follows from the estimate that already controls the integrated activities of a sector. Fix a tree sector of the held step. As in Subsection 8.3, its integration variables are a Haar variable \(Y\) and the single Gaussian vector \(Z\), with the product probability measure \(\nu (dY) \gamma_A (dZ)\). The vertices are of four types: connected packets, which are mandatory, and the three optional types, namely forced activities, exponential logarithmic corrections and warnings. Warning vertices are indexed by the warning addresses of that section, so distinct warning vertices have distinct addresses. Different warning types at the same site are different addresses. Every factor is evaluated at the same \((Y, Z)\).

Lemma 13.24. (Residual bodies on one tree sector) Assume that the vertex factors \(f_i\) of this sector are measurable and satisfy the uniform pointwise bounds \(|f_i | \leqslant c_i\) of the table preceding (8.22): \(c_i = C_0 e^{(s - g) n_i}\) for a packet with \(n_i\) seeds, \(c_i = v_i\) for a forced activity, \(c_i = w_i\) for the factor \(e^{\ell_i} - 1\) of a logarithmic correction, and \(c_i = T\) for a warning. Assume that each nonzero warning forces its coordinate excursion within distance \(2 B\) of its address, and that (8.19) holds. Put \(\tilde{c}_i = E (x) T\) for a warning and \(\tilde{c}_i = c_i\) otherwise. Then for every finite set \(S\) of distinct vertices and every set \(O \subseteq S\) of optional vertices,

\(\displaystyle \int \left| \prod_{i \in S \setminus O} f_i (Y, Z) \right| \hspace{0.17em} d \nu \hspace{0.17em} d \gamma_A \leqslant \prod_{i \in S \setminus O} \tilde{c}_i .\) (13.20)

The same bound holds after the probability averages over forests and tags. Consequently, the common mixed-body estimate retained in Lemma 13.22 holds on the held step with the optional bounds \(\tilde{c}_i\) and the packet bounds \(C_0 e^{(s - g) n_i}\). These bounds do not depend on the removed set.

Proof. The set \(S' = S \setminus O\) is again a finite set of distinct vertices of the four types. The pointwise bounds are assumptions on individual vertices, so they hold for every member of \(S'\). The excursion implication is also a property of each warning separately. If \(\prod_{i \in S'} f_i (Y, Z) \ne 0\), then every warning factor in \(S'\) is nonzero. Hence \(Z\) lies in the excursion event of each of these warnings, that is, in \(\mathcal{C} (X (S') ; t, 2 B)\). These are exactly the hypotheses of (8.22) for \(S'\).

For completeness we recall its proof in this case. Pointwise,

\(\displaystyle \left| \prod_{i \in S'} f_i (Y, Z) \right| \leqslant \boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{C} (X (S') ; t, 2 B)} (Z) \prod_{i \in S'} c_i .\)

The left side is measurable and bounded on a probability space, so it is integrable. By Fubini's theorem, integrate first in \(Z\) for fixed \(Y\). The Gaussian law \(\gamma_A\) does not depend on \(Y\), so (8.19) bounds the inner integral by \(E (x)^{|X (S') |} \prod_{i \in S'} c_i\). Integrating this constant against the probability measure \(\nu\) leaves it unchanged. Since distinct warnings of \(S'\) have distinct addresses, \(|X (S') |\) is the number of warnings in \(S'\), and the bound equals \(\prod_{i \in S'} \tilde{c}_i\). Only the warnings that remain in \(S'\) enter the event, so a removed warning takes its probability bound \(E (x)\) with it. No product of separate warning probabilities is used.

The right side of (13.20) does not depend on the forest, the tags or the configuration. Averaging the left side with probability weights therefore preserves the bound. The admitted removals in Lemma 13.22 replace only optional labels, those in \(\sigma (T)\). So the residual body is the product over \(S \setminus \sigma (T)\), the packet bounds are those of the retained packets, and (13.20) with \(O = \sigma (T)\) is the stated common estimate. In that lemma, the inserted factors are bounded pointwise by their wages before this single integration. The switched integral is therefore at most \(\prod_{\lambda \in T} w_{\lambda} \prod_{i \in S \setminus \sigma (T)} \tilde{c}_i\), without multiplying bounds for separate integrals.\(\Box\)

The lemma removes no hypothesis of Subsection 8.3. It uses the pointwise vertex bounds, the plateau and mean inputs behind the excursion implication, and the uniform covariance bound behind (8.19). On the held step these remain to be supplied by their local producers: Subsection 8.25 for packets, the inherited, remote and itinerary bounds for the logarithmic corrections, and Subsection 8.15 for the warning plateau. That attachment is part of the remaining analytic work listed above. The bounds \(\tilde{c}_i\) are the ones whose rooted site sums enter the mixed component estimate. In particular, a warning carries \(E (x) T\), not \(T\).

13.10.6Adjacent quadratic data and the warning inputs

The stationary cancellation used in Subsection 8.15 requires identities between the two adjacent quadratic forms and their paths. We specify these identities on the retained history before using that cancellation. This also gives a precise meaning to the compatibility of the tube with its quadratic data.

Fix \(N, p\) and \(i < N\). The tube of this step consists of the stored tower at \((N, i)\), the incoming path \(R_{N, i + 1}\) with its clip, and the outgoing path \(R_{N, i}\) with its clip. Here \(R_{N, m}\) denotes the path underlying the representation \(R_{N, p, m}\), not its activity array. Its two beds are \(H_f = M.H (N, i + 1)\) and \(H_c = M.H (N, i)\), periodized on the respective tori. Use the notation \(D_f, D_c, J, J', A_0, T, \bar{T}, M_F, M_N, M_O\) of Subsection 3.9 for these very data. By adjacent quadratic compatibility we mean the following:

  1. The diagonal two-scale identity holds for these beds and this installed response, including the quadratic identity (8.109) and the transport identities (3.50).

  2. The six block identities (3.54)–(3.59) hold for this tower and these clips.

  3. Both full quadratic bilinear forms are invariant under signed coordinate changes. The two paths are attached to their respective beds, and those beds have the floor identities and bounds of the retained horizon at the indicated periods and clips. In particular, the full curvature factors obey \(J^{\ast} J = W\) and \({J'}^{\ast} J' = W'\) for the two metric matrices, with the same factors as in the block identities.

The floors here include the metric and diagonal inverse identities, normalization of the metric zero mode, equality of the metric and diagonal actions on exact curvatures, symmetry, and the stated lower and upper counting-norm bounds. They are the floor data of Subsection 3.9; positivity of an unrelated quadratic form would not replace them.

Lemma 13.25.
