(Compatibility of the stored adjacent tube) Retain the common horizon and stationary plan used to construct the selected history, with their quadratic and path receipts as in Sections 13.1 and 11.21.8. Every stored tube at \(i < N\) has adjacent quadratic compatibility. This includes \(i = 0\) when \(N > 0\), and holds on every admitted period for the same paths and tower.

Proof. Take the two witnesses to be \(M.H (N, i + 1)\) and \(M.H (N, i)\), without changing the horizon. Its adjacent crossing identity gives item (1). Its retained block completion gives item (2), by Proposition 3.32. The horizon construction retains these block identities after its bound enlargement; this is part of the construction stated at the end of Subsection 3.9. The signed invariance of the beds is the invariance of their full quadratic forms proved in Subsection 3.11. The first clause of the stationary plan, Definition 11.36, attaches \(R_{N, i + 1}\) and \(R_{N, i}\) to these beds. The floor receipts belong to those same beds and clips by the retained-horizon input of that plan. This proves item (3).

All clauses apply at \(i + 1 \leqslant N\) and \(i \leqslant N\). These inequalities follow from \(i < N\), and do not require \(i > 0\). The argument therefore applies to the actual stored step at zero; it invokes no default comparison interface. Likewise, it applies when \(i + 1 = N\): we use the final retained horizon's receipts, not equality with a different horizon only valid away from the terminal level. Choosing the shooting parameter changes neither these paths nor the tower. The periodized identities and attachments thus remain the ones used in the replayed history.\(\Box\)

We next record what this supplies to the warning argument. Keep the funded path, shell, guide and local precision bounds used in Sections 8.5, 8.12, 8.13 and 8.14, on the stored tube. For a sector, compatibility of its store means that its coarse-flag component equals the flags of its coarse field at the coarse threshold; its other seed components need not vanish. Let \(x = \hat{\beta}_i\), \(h = \hat{\beta}_{i + 1}\) and retain the scales of (8.17). In particular the fine representation has flag threshold \(\tau\) and actual coupling \(\beta \in [h / 2, 2 h]\).

Lemma 13.26. (Warnings on the stored sectors) Under these funded local bounds and the common purchases of this section, the mean, plateau and covariance inputs of Subsection 8.3 hold simultaneously on every compatible sector of every stored step. More precisely, for each fixed allowed stage pair there are constants \(C_{\mu}, M, a, \xi > 0\) and a common lower threshold for \(x\), independent of \(N, p\), the forest, tags and coarse field, such that

\(\displaystyle \| \mu_e \| \leqslant C_{\mu} \tau, \qquad 0 \leqslant C_{\text{loc}} \leqslant M 1, \qquad C_{\mu} + 1 \leqslant a, \qquad q \xi \leqslant 1.\)

Here \(C_{\text{loc}}\) is the covariance of the single Gaussian vector used in that sector. Outside the \(30 B\) seed neighbourhood the event \(\mathcal{W}_B (j)\) gives site and bond likelihoods equal to one, fine plaquette chords less than \(\tau / 4\), and predictor chords less than \(W \tau\). Consequently every nonzero warning forces its excursion within distance \(2 B\), and its joint probability bound can be taken to be

\(\displaystyle E (x) = 2 b_{2 B} p_{\text{coord}} \exp (- \xi \sqrt{P} / 4) .\)

The same assertions hold with the reduced warning margin \(\xi / 2\), and the corresponding bound, when the zero-germ statement of Subsection 8.28 is required.

Proof. The compatibility lemma supplies the diagonal identity, signed invariance, full curvature factors and stack identities used in (8.111). Thus the stationary cancellation and local mean comparison of Subsection 8.14 concern this tube, with its original paths and tags. Apply Subsection 8.15 to it. Its explicit choice (8.119) gives \(C_{\mu}\); (8.118) fixes the fine and predictor margins, and (8.124) gives \(\xi\). The three errors in that proof are bounded using the same stationary cancellation, transported rows and local inverse. Its final choice \(a \geqslant C_{\mu} + 1\) gives the broad cutoff plateau. No new stationary field is selected for this application.

To verify the covariance input, use the completed sector precision \(A\) of Subsection 8.12. Its gap and weighted row bounds supply (8.99). Subsection 8.13 constructs \(C_{\text{loc}} = (P_0 + P_0^{\ast}) / 2\) from the local window inverse \(P_0\), and proves \(C_{\text{loc}} \geqslant 0\) and \(\|C_{\text{loc}} \|_k \leqslant S\) for fixed \(S\). The weighted row-and-column norm dominates the Euclidean operator norm, so \(M = \max \{S, 1\}\) gives the claimed bound. Restriction to active coordinates preserves it by extension of a test vector by zero. This is the covariance of the sector Gaussian, not an identification with the full inverse \(A^{- 1}\) or with the different derivative-row construction.

The sector's flag identity makes a site farther than \(30 B\) from its seeds clean on the hulls required in the safety proof. That proof gives the claimed plateau conclusion on \(\mathcal{W}_B (j)\). For the five mask, likelihood and contraction types, a warning outside its compulsory region has its address outside the \(300 B\) seed neighbourhood. Since \(300 B \geqslant 30 B + B\), the hypotheses of Lemma 8.2 apply, including the fine-site contraction and the enlargement to \(2 B\). For a fine-bond cutoff the mean bound and \(q \xi \leqslant 1\) give the broad-cutoff plateau directly; its additional hot-bond and \(304 B\) exclusions only set more factors to zero. Finally apply (8.19) with the just established \(M\) and \(\xi\). It bounds distinct addresses, including different types at one site. The strict margins in the safety proof persist at \(\xi / 2\), giving the zero-germ assertion and the same Gaussian calculation at that margin.

For each fixed stage pair all constants above precede the final lower threshold for \(x\). There are finitely many permitted lower stages for each upper stage. Take the maximum of these thresholds, and then its running maximum in the upper stage, in the common purchases before selecting the history. All estimates hold on upper tails, so this preserves the earlier purchases. The stored clock pays that threshold at \(x = \hat{\beta}_i\), also at \(i = 0\). The asserted uniformity follows from the uniformity over periods, sectors and tags in each cited local estimate. The already selected histories are not altered afterwards.\(\Box\)

This supplies the warning part of Lemma 13.24 from the funded local construction. Its packet, forced and logarithmic pointwise bounds, and the component and differentiated entrance estimates, still require their own attachment. In particular no health condition for a packet's enlarged support follows from the sector's coarse-flag identity alone.

13.10.7The value bounds on the selected input

We now attach the scalar bounds to the actual incoming representation. Fix an allowed pair of stages \(j = J (i + 1)\) and \(k = J (i)\), and put \(x = \hat{\beta}_i\), \(h = \hat{\beta}_{i + 1} = x + s_G \log L\) and \(P = \exp ((\log \log h)^2)\). The radius used for the component expansion is \(B = B (x)\); the incoming representation has radius \(B (h)\). These two radii are not identified.

Lemma 13.27. (The stored representation is an admissible input) For every \(i < N\), every admitted period and every retained member of the shooting interval, its representation at \(i + 1\) has the incoming path and high clip of the stored tube. Its threshold is \(P / \sqrt{h}\), its radius is \(B (h)\), and its support exponent is that of stage \(j\). Its coupling belongs to \([h / 2, 2 h]\). Its regular and forced activity bounds are, respectively, no larger than

\(\displaystyle h^{- 11 / 10}, \qquad e^{- P^{1 / 5}},\)

and its path bounds may be used with loop allowance \(2 P\). The tube retains its funded step and the coarse period bound \(c \geqslant 100 (1 + B (x))\). These statements also hold at \(i = 0\).

Proof. The interval construction in Subsection 13.1 appends the actual reset batch to the incoming history. The batch relation retains its paths, clips, budget points and label bounds; it does not only identify the resulting density. At the top these data are supplied by the Wilson startup, and at every lower level they are part of the retained-history assertion of that construction. The path and clip at \(i + 1\) are therefore precisely the upward path and high clip of the stored tube at \(i\). The budget point there is the one assigned by the common clock. Its threshold and radius are \(P / \sqrt{h}\) and \(B (h)\), and its profile is the stage-\(j\) profile. The regular allowance is at most \(h^{- 11 / 10}\) and the forced allowance is \(e^{- P^{1 / 5}}\), by the calendar allowances and the final absorption in that construction. The chosen member stays in the level band; substitution of the clock identity \(h = x + s_G \log L\) makes this exactly \([h / 2, 2 h]\).

The purchased protection exponent obeys \(0 < \theta_j \leqslant 1\) and \(P \geqslant 1\). Hence the retained loop capacity \(2 P^{\theta_j}\) is at most \(2 P\). Increasing this numerical allowance preserves the same path coefficients and all their bounds. The funded step and the size inequality are those of the common schedule, at coarse level \(i\); no limiting argument in the period is used. Each argument requires only \(i < N\), which supplies the existing fine level \(i + 1 \leqslant N\). Thus it also covers zero. Finally, selecting a member of the interval retains all these assertions and introduces no new path, budget point or label family.\(\Box\)

Choose a halo multiplier \(\ell \geqslant 500\) containing all read radii in the local construction, an output support exponent \(a \geqslant 0\), a length reserve \(\varepsilon > 0\) and a tree reserve \(\delta > 0\). Write

\(\displaystyle H = a + 2 \varepsilon + \delta .\)

As in the common purchases, choose the reserves before \(L\), with \(4 \varepsilon\) strictly below the incoming support exponent and \(8 \varepsilon\) strictly below both adjacent path widths. Choose \(L\) large enough for the cover conversions in Subsection 8.24 at exponent \(H\). Fix \(r \in (- 1 / 2, 0)\).

Use the disjoint union of the original forced labels, logarithmic labels and warning addresses. Equal supports do not identify labels. For a forced label \(u\), its support \(X_u\) is its fine support projected and enlarged by the prescribed \(\ell B\) halo, and its extra charge \(d_u\) is the cover number of that original fine support at radius \(B (h)\). For a logarithmic label use its full enlarged read support and the charge assigned in the inherited or itinerary construction: the old cover, the true path length (including winding and repeated edges), or the correction order. The three short families have charge zero. For a warning use its full warning footprint and charge zero.

Proposition 13.28. (One common value-bound array) Retain the funded local estimates used above, including their seed energy, chart and normalization constructions. On each stored step there are nonnegative bounds \(v_u, w_z\) for the original forced and logarithmic labels, independent of forest, tags, sector and integration variables, such that the forced factor is bounded by \(v_u\), and both the logarithmic factor \(\ell_z\) and \(e^{\ell_z} - 1\) are bounded in absolute value by \(w_z\). With warning bound \(TE (x)\) from Lemma 13.26, their combined array \(f\) obeys

\(\displaystyle \sup_y \sum_{u : y \in X_u} f_u e^{2 \varepsilon d_u + Hc_B (X_u)} \leqslant h^r .\) (13.21)

For every fixed list of forced labels used in the contraction factors, the actual connected packet with seed set \(A\) obeys, uniformly in that list,

\(\displaystyle |\mathcal{P}_A | \leqslant p_A := \exp \{(Cd^5 - \beta \mu \tau^2) n (A)\}, \qquad d = 1 + B + (\log h)^2, \quad \tau = P / \sqrt{h},\) (13.22)

with one \(C \geqslant 0\) and the purchased \(\mu > 0\). For any fixed extra charge \(b \geqslant 0\) and seed mark \(\nu \geqslant 0\),

\(\displaystyle \sup_y \sum_{A : y \in X_A} p_A e^{(2 \varepsilon b + \nu) n (A) + Hc_B (X_A)} \leqslant e^{- \mu P^2 / 8} .\) (13.23)

The requests \(b, \nu, r\), halo and exponent reserves are fixed before the common schedule is purchased. All three estimates hold at one common threshold, which may depend on these requests and is chosen before the history, uniformly in the cutoff, period and admitted input member. Disconnected packets have factor and bound zero.

Proof. We first verify the inputs rather than selecting new activity arrays. Lemma 13.27 supplies the incoming coefficient bounds, path budgets, threshold, support exponent, radius and coupling window on precisely this tube. The exponential forced allowance has every negative upper exponent: with \(y = \log \log h\), \(P^{1 / 5} / \log h = \exp (y^2 / 5 - y) \to \infty\). The incoming regular allowance has upper exponent \(- 11 / 10\) and the loop allowance has upper exponent zero. The inherited support conversion therefore applies with its original labels and masks. Its radius condition \(B (h) \leqslant 2 B (x)\) holds eventually since \(h - x\) is fixed for this stage pair. The strict width and support reserves imposed above are exactly those needed for its factor \(e^{2 \varepsilon d_u}\). This includes the squared path tails, whose reserve is \(8 \varepsilon\), not \(4 \varepsilon\).

Choose \(s\) with \(- 1 / 2 < s < r\). The inherited construction gives the forced rooted bound and the six inherited logarithmic rooted bounds at upper exponent at most \(s\), with head exponent \(H\) and the stated extra charges. The three remote short families have upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) by (8.170) and its support count. Their sector indicators remove the packet neighbourhoods, leaving the clean read regions on which that estimate is proved; their values use the original tags. The correction itineraries have every negative upper exponent by (8.177), with their full read supports and correction-order charge. Its count sums ordered words, including repeated bonds. Together these are all the logarithmic labels. Take the disjoint union of these nonnegative bound arrays, including their finite channel multiplicities. Their rooted sum \(J (x)\) has upper exponent at most \(s\). No bounds are selected separately for different forests or configurations: the quoted bounds are uniform over these variables and depend only on the fixed input coefficients and geometric labels.

Every nonzero logarithmic label has nonempty support. Since all support and extra-cost exponents are nonnegative, its scalar bound \(b_z\) is at most \(J (x)\), by retaining its term in the rooted sum at any point of its support. Eventually \(J (x) \leqslant 1\). For \(| \ell_z | \leqslant b_z \leqslant 1\), the mean value theorem gives

\(\displaystyle |e^{\ell_z} - 1| \leqslant eb_z .\)

Thus \(w_z = eb_z\) pays both \(| \ell_z |\) and \(|e^{\ell_z} - 1|\). It multiplies the rooted sum by a fixed constant and preserves its upper exponent. A label with identically zero factor can be priced by zero. The forced bound needs no exponentiation.

The warning support is contained in a fixed-multiple-\(B\) ball and has bounded \(B\)-cover number. There are a fixed number of warning types per coarse site and at most a polynomial in \(B\) possible addresses whose footprints meet a given site. Consequently the warning rooted bound, including \(e^{Hc_B (X_u)}\), is at most a fixed constant times a polynomial in \(B\) times \(TE (x)\). It has every negative upper exponent by the Gaussian warning estimate. Summing the forced, logarithmic and warning arrays therefore gives a function of upper exponent at most \(s\). As \(s < r\), that sum is at most \(h^r\) eventually. This proves (13.21), including all three families and both reserves in one bound.

For packets, the seed-energy allocation of Subsection 8.10 gives the core gain \(\mu \tau^2 n (A)\). If a physical packet is nonzero, its retained masks enforce the hot-bond control and flag agreement needed in the \(200 B\) annular comparison; Subsection 8.11 gives the remainder of upper exponent \(- 3 / 2\). The normalization corrections are bounded by Subsection 8.13, and the protected chart logarithm is eventually at most one by Subsection 8.18. The finite tag probabilities, with their actual multiplicities, give the ticket count in Subsection 8.25. Its proof of (8.187) now gives (13.22). Vanishing packets satisfy the same bound. The contraction factors for any fixed forced list take values in \([0, 1]\); the proof drops them, so its bound is uniform in that list. This is a value estimate and imposes no global health condition on the packet neighbourhood.

Finally \(\beta \geqslant h / 2\) gives \(\beta \mu \tau^2 \geqslant \mu P^2 / 2\). In the absorption argument of Subsection 8.25 take \(u = 1 / 2\) and replace its seed expense \(Cd^5\) by \(Cd^5 + 2 \varepsilon b + \nu\). These additional fixed costs are absorbed by increasing the coefficient of \(d^5\), since \(d \geqslant 1\). Use the head exponent \(H\) and \(n_0 = 1\) there. Its bound \(e^{- \mu uP^2 / 4}\) is exactly (13.23). All constants are fixed before the final threshold in \(x\). The same finite-stage maximum and running-maximum purchase as in Lemma 13.26 makes every required threshold hold at the stored steps. The bounds are uniform over the members of the original input interval, so selecting its retained member preserves them.\(\Box\)

For clarity we spell out the resulting component input. Give a packet extra cost \(bn (A)\) and mark \(\nu n (A)\), and give an optional label its charge \(d_u\) and zero mark. Set

\(\displaystyle \Gamma = h^r + e^{- \mu P^2 / 8}, \qquad q_x = h^{r / 2}, \qquad h_B = (2 B + 1)^4 .\)

For sufficiently large \(x\),

\(\displaystyle \Gamma \leqslant 2 h^r, \qquad 4 h_B \Gamma / \delta \leqslant q_x < 1.\) (13.24)

Indeed the exponential packet bound is smaller than \(h^r\) eventually, and \(8 h_B h^{r / 2} / \delta \to 0\), since \(B = O ((\log h)^2)\) and \(r < 0\). The two rooted bounds above are precisely the vertex condition of Subsection 8.2, at head exponent \(a + 2 \varepsilon + \delta\). In particular the common-body estimate of Lemma 13.24 and the component summation apply to this actual value alphabet, with its original Gaussian and Haar measures.

For example, for connected sets of labels with at least \(m + 1\) labels, at least \(r_0\) seeds and total cover-plus-extra-cost at least \(D_0\), (8.11) gives the rooted sum of their value majorants, weighted by output support exponent \(a\) and polynomial order \(k_0\), at most

\(\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon, k_0} e^{- \varepsilon D_0 - \nu r_0} \frac{\Gamma q_x^m}{1 - q_x} .\) (13.25)

Use \(r_0 = 0\) unless the component is required to contain a packet. This is an estimate for the one actual value alphabet and a fixed forced list. Uniformity in that list is not permission to sum unpriced copies. If a later response assigns several analytic outputs to the same component, their majorants must first be summed over that component's fiber as required by Subsection 8.2. The differentiated entrance still requires healthy full supports and the stock-extension argument of Subsection 8.28. Neither that additional hypothesis nor the precursor and vapor identifications follows from (13.25) alone.

13.10.8Summing the actual component variants

We now supply the thin-component input left open in Subsubsection 3.13.1. Retain the local hypotheses of Proposition 13.28 and the offer, occupancy and shipment hypotheses of Lemma 13.22. They refer to the same stored representation. Write \(E\) for the available component exponent, \(\varepsilon > 0\) for its length and polynomial reserve, and \(\delta > 0\) for its tree reserve. Its head exponent is \(E + 2 \varepsilon + \delta\). Let \(r \in (- 1 / 2, 0)\) be the chosen rate. These requests precede the common schedule.

It is useful to retain a growing seed mark, rather than just the fixed marks in (13.23). Put

\(\displaystyle M = \mu P^2 / 4, \qquad \gamma_0 = h^r + e^{- M / 4}, \qquad R_{\text{menu}} = h^r e^{(E + 2 \varepsilon + \delta) c_{\ast}}, \qquad \gamma_1 = \gamma_0 + R_{\text{menu}}, \qquad \rho = h^{r / 2},\)

where \(c_{\ast}\) is the fixed birth-halo cover constant in the shipment conversion. The optional menu bound is at most \(h^r + R_{\text{menu}}\). The packet site estimate, with the additional seed weight \(e^{Mn (A)}\), is at most \(e^{- M / 4}\) eventually. Indeed \(\beta \mu \tau^2 \geqslant \mu P^2 / 2 = 2 M\). After extracting \(M\) per seed, at least \(M\) remains. The packet enumeration of Subsection 8.2 adds \(2 \log D_B\) per seed, the fixed support and length costs, and the expense \(Cd^5\). Their sum is \(o (M)\), since \(d = O ((\log h)^2)\) and \(P = \exp ((\log \log h)^2)\). Eventually the geometric ratio is at most \(e^{- M / 2}\). Its rooted sum is at most \(H_B e^{- M / 2} / (1 - e^{- M / 2}) \leqslant e^{- M / 4}\), because \(H_B\) grows polynomially in \(B\). This proves the asserted marked packet bound, without treating \(M\) as a fixed request.

Both \(\gamma_0\) and \(\gamma_1\) have upper exponent \(r\). Thus, after one further threshold increase,

\(\displaystyle \frac{4 (2 B + 1)^4 \gamma_0}{\delta} \leqslant \frac{1}{2}, \qquad \frac{4 (2 B + 1)^4 \gamma_1}{\delta} \leqslant \rho \leqslant \frac{1}{2} .\)

For an integer \(k \geqslant 0\) define the two positive component budgets

\(\displaystyle O_k = P_{\varepsilon, k} \frac{\gamma_0}{1 - 1 / 2}, \qquad U_k = P_{\varepsilon, k} \frac{\gamma_1}{1 - \rho}, \qquad P_{\varepsilon, k} = e^{\varepsilon} k! \varepsilon^{- k} .\) (13.26)

Here \(O_k\) pays the original alphabet and \(U_k\) the augmented one. Both have upper exponent \(r\).

Lemma 13.29. (Component fibers and the thin rooted bound) Let \(C\) be an actual connected finite set of elementary labels, with full support \(X_C\) and total cover-plus-length charge \(s_C\). Its menu replacement terms admit nonnegative majorants \(b_{C, T}\) such that

\(\displaystyle \sum_T b_{C, T} = \left( \prod_{A \in C \text{packet}} p_A \right) \prod_{u \in C \text{optional}} v_u^{\mathcal{I}} .\) (13.27)

The sum includes the distinct menu entries at a common address. The normalized forest and tag averages cost no additional factor. With

\(\displaystyle t_C = (1 + s_C)^k \sum_T b_{C, T},\)

the rooted sum over all such components is at most \(U_k\) at exponent \(E\). Restricting to thin components preserves this bound. Restricting instead to components containing a packet gives, for \(k = 0\), the stronger bound \(U_0 e^{- M}\). All these are bounds for the actual component labels and their summed replacement terms.

Proof. A replacement set \(T\) must occupy distinct optional addresses of \(C\). Give it the majorant consisting of its wages, the original bounds at unreplaced addresses and the unchanged packet bounds. The residual-body lemma bounds the absolute integral by this product on the one Gaussian and Haar probability space. This bound is uniform in the forest and finite tags. Their averages have nonnegative coefficients summing to one; applying the triangle inequality before averaging preserves the same product. These averaging indices are not extra unweighted labels in the component alphabet. The only sum over replacement choices is exactly the finite product identity (13.17), which proves (13.27).

Apply (8.11) to the augmented alphabet with site bound \(\gamma_1\), tail ratio \(\rho\), and mark \(M\) on every packet seed. For the unrestricted components take minimum seed count zero, minimum extra cost zero and minimum excess label count zero. This gives \(U_k\). A thin component is a subset of these distinct connected sets, not another copy of each set, so restriction decreases the nonnegative sum. If a component contains a packet, its total number of typed seeds is at least one. Taking minimum seed count one and \(k = 0\) gives \(U_0 e^{- M}\).

If a later output groups several terms with the same component or barcode, the norm of their sum is bounded by the sum of these already assigned majorants. The argument uses support containment when an output has smaller support. No estimate is made by multiplying the component bound by an unbounded number of copies. For absolute convergence on the finite torus, every nonzero component has nonempty support and \(E \geqslant 0\). Summing the rooted bound over all sites gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_C t_C \leqslant | \Lambda_c | U_k .\)

Consequently every subseries and every regrouping just used converges absolutely. The volume factor is used only for this assertion; it is not inserted into the rooted estimate.\(\Box\)

Fix a field derivative order \(j\). Choose the polynomial order \(k = K + j\) returned by the integrated derivative estimate below, and a nonnegative upper-exponent-zero function \(A_j (x)\) enlarged to dominate its constant. These choices precede the common schedule and are used in all the following pledges; a smaller arbitrary polynomial order would not pay the differentiated terms. Put

\(\displaystyle P_0 = (1 + 2^{j + 2} A_j) (O_k + 2 U_k), \qquad P_X = O_k + 2 U_0, \qquad V = 10 (P_0 + P_X) .\) (13.28)

Choose \(a \geqslant 0\) and \(e > 0\) with \(a + 2 e \leqslant E\). These are also fixed before the schedule. Since all three displayed bounds have upper exponent \(r < 0\), eventually

\(\displaystyle \frac{4 (2 B + 1)^4 P_0}{e} \leqslant \rho \leqslant \frac{1}{2}, \qquad \frac{4 (2 B + 1)^4 V}{e} \leqslant \frac{1}{2}, \qquad V \leqslant \frac{1}{2} .\) (13.29)

For example the first left side divided by \(\rho\) has upper exponent \(r / 2 < 0\); the other assertions follow directly from their negative upper exponent. All bounds are uniform in the finite menu.

In particular the actual thin weights \(t_C\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \sup_z \sum_{C : \hspace{0.17em} z \in X_C} t_C e^{(a + 2 e) c_B (X_C)} \leqslant P_0 .\)

Indeed Lemma 13.29 gives \(U_k\) at exponent \(E\), and \(U_k \leqslant P_0\). This supplies both the vertex input and the tree smallness required in Subsubsection 3.13.1. Its factorial-normalized cluster proof now gives the actual cluster fee bound \(P_0 / (1 - \rho)\) at exponent \(a + e\), and the subtracted response costs \(2 P_0 / (1 - \rho)\). Repeated component occurrences in a cluster retain their factorial normalization. This is not a new unpriced fiber. The separate packet-containing component budget is at most \(P_X e^{- M}\), since \(U_0 \leqslant P_X\). These statements construct the thin input; the reconstruction of the full forced response from these two populations still uses the separate cluster identities.

13.10.9The integrated derivative with an actual insertion

We next construct the marked integral majorant, rather than assume that the scalar estimate can be differentiated. Fix one original optional label \(u_{\ast}\), of fine crest cover \(c_f\), whose normalized insertion has pointwise amplitude \(w \geqslant 0\). It is a regular or forced insertion, not a warning. Its local construction in Subsection 8.33 multiplies the insertion by its larger protective cutoff. Use the compensated variation of Subsection 8.20, holding the original fine configuration \(U_{\ast}\) fixed. The inserted observable is then the constant \(h (U_{\ast})\). Each protective cutoff is a fixed smooth scalar function of a compensated increment divided by its threshold. The increment bounds in that section, divided by the threshold, have upper exponent zero; the fixed-order chain rule gives the same bound for each cutoff's derivative scale. The number of cutoff factors is bounded by a geometric function of upper exponent zero times the full cover plus length charge. The product rule adds these scales and retains amplitude \(w\). It therefore gives scale at most \(r_{\ast} (x) (1 + c_B (X_{u_{\ast}}) + d_{u_{\ast}})\), with \(r_{\ast}\) of upper exponent zero. This representation with the original observable fixed is used on the protective cutoff's nonzero germ. Outside its closed support the factor is a zero germ; the protected extension and the strict room in the compensated chart cover the boundary as in that construction. No derivatives of the arbitrary original fine observable are taken.

Choose the derivative order before the schedule, and retain the funded local jet hypotheses in Subsection 8.28, including the differentiated precision and compensation estimates. The variation is a map \(V\) from a finite-dimensional real normed parameter space, with \(V (y_0)\) equal to the base coarse field. Its link jets through order \(j\) obey the bounds of Subsection 8.20, with order-\(k\) bound \(C_G v (x)^k\), where \(v \geqslant 0\) has upper exponent \(- 1 / 2\) and the fixed convention for \(C_G\) is the one used there. The constants below are uniform over this class of variations, not over arbitrary rescalings of the parameter. These are stronger local inputs than the covariance norm used only for value estimates. Let \(C\) be an admitted connected component containing \(u_{\ast}\). At the base coarse field require its whole assigned union support \(X_C\) to be healthy. Thus every packet's full \(500 B\) neighbourhood and every other read support is healthy. Use the smooth stock extension for packets and the unmasked coarse-tail extension, as specified in that section. They agree in value with the physical factors on this domain; we do not differentiate a physical flag test.

Let \(a_u\) be the elementary jet amplitudes, with \(a_u = T\) for a warning, and let \(J_C\) be the joint reduced-margin warning event. Put \(b_C =\# \{ \text{warning labels in } C\}\) and \(s = s_C\). On the common Gaussian space let \(I\) be the full read-bond set and let \(F_I\) be the polynomial defined in Subsection 8.27. The product rule, including the density ratio and the compensation Jacobian, supplies fixed integers \(q, K\) and nonnegative upper-exponent-zero functions \(D_j, E_j\) such that

\(\displaystyle g_C (z) = w \hspace{-0.17em} \prod_{u \in C \setminus \{u_{\ast} \}} a_u \hspace{0.17em} D_j (x) (1 + s)^j \boldsymbol{1}_{J_C} (z) F_I (z)^{qj}\) (13.30)

bounds every derivative through order \(j\) of this transformed integrand at the base parameter. It is uniform in the frozen Haar variables. The original label's bound has been removed exactly once. Removing this label removes no warning, so \(J_C\) and \(b_C\) are unchanged.

For completeness, the scale bound follows by adding all factor scales. Every nonzero factor has nonempty support, so their sum is at most a fixed upper-exponent-zero function times \(1 + s\). Write \(r_{\text{jet}} (x)\) for the common factor scale. The density-ratio derivatives add a scale of the form \(DF_I^q\). Since \(F_I \geqslant 1\), the total scale is bounded by \((1 + D + 2 r_{\text{jet}} + r_{\ast}) (1 + s) F_I^q\). Raising this to order at most \(j\) gives (13.30), after increasing \(D_j\). Warnings outside \(J_C\) vanish as germs by the reduced-margin argument, so no derivative of its indicator is involved.

The read count is at most \((1 + s) (2 B + 1)^4 \rho_L\), where \(\rho_L\) is the fixed bond-density constant. The Gaussian moment estimate at order \(2 qj\) gives

\(\displaystyle (\mathbb{E}F_I^{2 qj})^{1 / 2} \leqslant E_j (x) (1 + s)^K .\)

Purchase the warning bound \(\mathbb{P} (J_C) \leqslant \eta^{2 b_C}\) with a fixed arbitrarily negative upper exponent for \(\eta\). Applying Cauchy–Schwarz on this one Gaussian space proves that \(g_C\) is integrable and that

\(\displaystyle \int g_C \leqslant A_j (x) \hspace{0.17em} w \prod_{u \in C \setminus \{u_{\ast} \}} \tilde{a}_u \hspace{0.17em} (1 + s)^{K + j}, \qquad \tilde{a}_u = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} T \eta, & u \text{ a warning},\\ a_u, & \text{otherwise} . \end{array} \right.\) (13.31)

Haar integration costs one. The local common-neighbourhood dominator of Subsection 8.26 justifies differentiating each component integral. The estimate above is a base-point derivative bound; it does not infer uniform differentiability of an infinite sum from bounds only at that point.

There is an exact way to pay the marked factor before summing components. With \(g = h^r\) and incoming support exponent \(\nu_f\), set

\(\displaystyle j_{\ast} = ge^{- (\nu_f - \varepsilon) c_f}, \qquad D_{\ast} = \frac{we^{(\nu_f - \varepsilon) c_f}}{g} .\)

Then \(D_{\ast} \geqslant 0\) and \(D_{\ast} j_{\ast} = w\), including \(w = 0\). The marked-footprint calculation in Subsection 8.2 gives the extra rooted vertex bound at most \(ge^{(E + 2 \varepsilon + \delta) c_{\ast}} = R_{\text{menu}}\). Replace the bound at \(u_{\ast}\) by \(j_{\ast}\), retaining the same support and length charge. The array is bounded by the old array plus this one new entry, so its site bound is at most \(\gamma_1\); replacement is not assumed to increase the old bound. Equation (13.31) is now \(D_{\ast} A_j\) times the product bound of that array, multiplied by \((1 + s)^{K + j}\). The differentiated packet amplitudes have the same marked seed estimate used above: their added expense \(3 jd^5\) is still \(o (M)\). The differentiated optional bounds retain exponent \(r\), by choosing the intermediate exponent strictly below \(r\) before absorbing the fixed derivative constants.

Consequently the sum of the integrated majorants for all admitted healthy components through a site, at exponent \(E\), is at most

\(\displaystyle D_{\ast} A_j (x) U_{K + j} .\) (13.32)

This statement concerns the sum of the base-point integral majorants. For a fixed sector and forest, Tonelli's theorem applies to the nonnegative witnesses on its probability space. When the actual component construction assigns different sectors, use the disjoint union of the component probability spaces with counting measure; Tonelli then gives the same sum of integrals. On the finite torus the total integral is at most \(| \Lambda_c | D_{\ast} A_j U_{K + j}\). Restricting to any actual subfamily or averaging the forest and tags preserves the bound. The unmarked version is obtained by retaining the original factor instead of replacing it; its bound is \(A_j O_{K + j}\). For a physical thin term with empty flag inventory, health on its support identifies this local extension with the physical value; outside that domain one uses its prescribed mask. No smoothness of that mask across the boundary is claimed. Thus the derivative input has the correct domain and a common integrable witness on the selected tube, including the directly stored step at index zero.

13.10.10The precursor and the two local correction arrays

The preceding bounds can now be used before either of the numerical response payments. We describe the actual arrays being paid. Retain the local return and its exact splitting and reset hypotheses from Sections 12.9 and 12.10; in particular the truncation, folding, path and clipping data are those of the stored return. The small-field comparison and reset estimates in those sections are still local analytic premises. The argument here supplies the component, insertion and summation inputs and applies those estimates to their two distinct correction arrays.

The hard precursor.
The thin-component cluster fees have rooted bound \(P_0 / (1 - \rho)\) at exponent \(a + e\), and the packet-containing components have rooted bound \(P_X e^{- M}\) there. A precursor label consists of a connected collection of the latter components decorated by regular cluster occurrences, with their actual positive integer multiplicities. Its bound is the product of the component bounds and the regular cluster fees divided by the occurrence factorials. The original compatibility and eligibility tests are retained in the physical array. For estimating its bound, they may be dropped.

Here is the multiplicity estimate with the present constants. On the alphabet of packet-containing components and regular cluster labels, write \(w_i\) for these bounds, and give a component the mark \(m_i = M\) and a regular cluster the mark zero. Inflate to \(v_i = 2 w_i e^{m_i}\). The rooted bound of \(2 v_i\) at exponent \(a + e\) is at most

\(\displaystyle 4 P_X + \frac{4 P_0}{1 - \rho} \leqslant 4 P_X + 8 P_0 \leqslant V.\)

By (13.29), \(V \leqslant 1 / 2\) and the distinct connected-set tree ratio for these bounds, with reserve \(e\), is at most \(1 / 2\). At a site in the nonempty support of \(i\) the individual rooted summand gives \(v_i' = v_i e^{ac_B (X_i)} \leqslant 1 / 2\). Thus, for each fixed distinct set of labels \(S\),

\(\displaystyle \prod_{i \in S} \sum_{n \geqslant 1} \frac{(v_i')^n}{n!} \leqslant \prod_{i \in S} 2 v_i' .\)

The rooted connected-set sum is therefore at most \(2 V\). Every actual precursor label contains a packet-containing component, so its inflated product has gained at least \(e^M\). It has also gained \(2^m\) if it has at least \(m\) occurrences. Extracting these factors proves the rooted precursor bound

\(\displaystyle 2^{- m} e^{- M} \hspace{0.17em} 2 V \quad \text{at support exponent } a.\) (13.33)

This is the bound of the actual decorated labels, including repeated regular clusters, not a bound for a single decoration. The exact flattening identity of Subsection 8.31 applies: connected components and flattening are inverse, and preserve the sign, inventory and both kinds of factorial. It identifies the priced array with the precursor gas. Its fixed flag gates are bounded by one and hence preserve every payment majorant. Since \(V \leqslant 1 / 2\), the case \(m = 0\) is at most \(e^{- M} = e^{- \mu P^2 / 4}\), the hard input of Lemma 13.23.

The same construction applies to the finite menu: replace each component bound by the summed ticket bound from Lemma 13.29. The score-product formula in Subsubsection 3.13.1 bounds each finite product by the product of its payment bounds. Division by the two occurrence factorials and the nonnegative summation above therefore give the same rooted payment for all its switching scores. This supplies the paid precursor, rather than merely a bound on each switched value.

The scalar insertion interval.
The inserted and uninserted component values, say \(b_C\) and \(a_C\), obey \(|a_C | \leqslant A_C\) and \(|b_C | \leqslant D_{\ast} B_C\), with the original and single-replacement product bounds respectively. These are the value cases of the residual-body and marked estimates. For every \(| \theta | D_{\ast} \leqslant 1\), the affine term \(a_C + \theta b_C\) has the joint value-and-derivative bound \(A_C + 2 B_C\) of Subsection 9.1. The witnesses are \((A_C + B_C, B_C)\), so the sum of the two witnesses is exactly this bound. The product rule for that joint norm incurs no arity factor. Thus the thin cluster series and the decorated precursor series have common summable majorants on the open interval \((- D_{\ast}^{- 1}, D_{\ast}^{- 1})\) if \(D_{\ast} > 0\), and on all of \(\mathbb{R}\) if \(D_{\ast} = 0\). In the latter case \(b_C = 0\) for every \(C\). The budgets for \(A_C + 2 B_C\) are bounded by \(O_k + 2 U_k\) and the corresponding marked-seed bound already included in (13.28). Nonempty supports turn their rooted bounds into absolute sums on each finite torus. Equation (9.2) now justifies differentiation of those scalar series. This interval argument is separate from the coarse-field jet argument at a healthy base point above. The latter supplies the regular local jet bounds for the resulting insertion; it is not used as a substitute for uniformity on the scalar interval.

The first soft correction.
Write \(D_o (U)\) for the discrepancy at anchor \(o\) in (12.61). This is the difference between the allocated finite short density, its constant value and its folded local replacement, on exactly the physical and auxiliary data of the stored return. That section proves its locality on the doubled neighbourhood \(N_{2 z} (o)\) and

\(\displaystyle D_o (U) = 0 \quad \text{if } N_{2 z} (o) \text{ is healthy}, \qquad |D_o (U) | \leqslant q,\)

where

\(\displaystyle q = 2 h_{\text{val}} \left( 1 + \sum_{i = 0}^4 |a_i | \hspace{0.17em} | \Lambda_i | \right), \qquad h_{\text{val}} = 2 \text{ballCost} (r_0) C_{\text{turn}} h^{- 7 / 16} .\)

Here \(r_0\) is the local panel radius, not the negative rate \(r\). Let \(M_2\) be the fixed cover bound of \(N_{2 z} (o)\) at radius \(B\). At a given site at most \(\text{ballCost} (2 z)\) anchors contribute. The ordinary correction array \(- D_o\), including its normalized symmetry copies, consequently has soft bound

\(\displaystyle b_0 = \text{ballCost} (2 z) qe^{(b_c + 2) M_2} .\)

The raised correction uses the same discrepancy and radius and adds one unit of support exponent, so its bound is

\(\displaystyle b_+ = e^{M_2} b_0 .\)

This equality follows termwise from the cover bound; if the actual cover is smaller, it is an inequality in the favourable direction. The displayed expressions are the chosen uniform allowances. All factors except \(h^{- 7 / 16}\) have upper exponent zero, while the exponential cover factors are fixed stage constants. Let \(C_{\text{cov}} = e^{\chi_0}\) be the cover allowance already fixed in Lemma 13.23. The entry threshold can be chosen so that

\(\displaystyle b_0 \leqslant \min (1, C_{\text{cov}} e^{- M_2}) h^{- 1 / 8}, \qquad b_+ \leqslant C_{\text{cov}} h^{- 1 / 8} .\)

Indeed \(b_0 h^{1 / 8}\) tends to zero, and both constants in the minimum are strictly positive and fixed before the threshold. This explicitly matches the first payment even if the convenient cover bound \(M_2\) differs from the purchased ticket \(\chi_0\). The vanishing on healthy doubled supports is exactly the eligibility condition needed to absorb this logarithmic array. The signs follow from (12.61): it is \(- D_o\) that is absorbed. These are the ordinary and raised precursor soft bounds; neither is the bound of the final reset correction.

The final local correction.
At the base budget point \(p\) retain the actual path, clip \(S\), width \(\sigma\), path count \(C\), reset \(\alpha\) and regular allowance \(H\). The reset decomposition of Subsection 12.10 gives two disjoint logarithmic families. The first is the local Taylor-subtraction discrepancy, whose amplitude is bounded by the reset-loss constant times the original regular majorant. The second is the low-path mask discrepancy, bounded by the boundary-hit coefficient times its original absolute path coefficient and relaxation weight. Both vanish on their assigned healthy supports, as proved by the reset identities. They are measurable, gauge invariant and local on those supports. Thus their nonnegative pointwise majorants, not only the sums of their values, are available for the payment.

For the ordinary row use the ordinary footprint payments of that section, giving

\(\displaystyle f_0 = \text{softNeed} (p, S, \sigma, C, \alpha, H) .\)

For the raised row repeat the support estimate at exponent \(p. \text{supportExponent} + 2\). The reset halo has population radius \(4 B + 2\) and cover increment \(81\); use width reserve \(1 / 2\). The low-path support has population radius \(B\), its original length charge and cover increment \(C_{\text{low}}\); use width reserve \(\sigma / 8\). The path-width hypothesis implies \((p. \text{supportExponent} + 2) / B \leqslant \sigma / 2\), leaving this positive reserve in the original path sum. Applying the footprint sum separately to the two families gives exactly

\(\displaystyle f_+ = F (H) + N (p, \alpha, H) Q (p, S, \sigma, C)\)

with \(F, Q, N\) as in (3.83). The scalar boundary-hit bound \(N\) follows from the same reset coefficient estimate and regular allowance \(H\); it is uniform for \(| \alpha | \leqslant A_j\). This is a rooted bound on the actual reset and path arrays. Reindexing into the final dictionary is injective, with zero values at unused addresses, and preserves the bound. It is attached to the raised budget, whose radius is still \(B\), although the displayed formula is evaluated at the base point \(p\).

Finally apply the first numerical payment to (13.33) and \(b_0\) or \(b_+\), respectively. The compatible-family identity and payment argument in Subsection 8.32 produce hard bound \(2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 8}\). Widening the dictionary sends old labels injectively to their unchanged supports and assigns zero elsewhere; it preserves both the scores and their majorants. Apply the second payment to that hard bound and the corresponding final soft array, \(f_0\) or \(f_+\). The result is the bound \(2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 16}\) on that row's actual output, with the two different support exponents. No output or rate from the ordinary row is substituted for the raised one. All component sums, local corrections and both payments use the same selected representation, including at index zero. The local funding, jet, short-panel and reset hypotheses explicitly retained above remain necessary; the argument does not establish them merely by increasing a threshold.

13.10.11Retention on the public run

We now remove the two hypotheses of Proposition 13.20 that concern the fixed interfaces. The first, preservation of the reserved slots, is Lemma 13.21. The second asks for the arrival, ordinary and raised-response receipts of Subsection 3.16 on the retained representations and interface. Its content, made explicit in Sections 3.13 and 3.16, consists of six items:

    (A1)

    the shipment of every admitted offer into rooted majorants on the component alphabet of the fixed interface;

    (A2)

    the vertex input and tree smallness of Subsubsection 3.13.1, with budgets \(P_0, P_1\), ratio \(\rho \leqslant 1 / 2\), marking exponent \(E_0\) and total pledge \(V_Q\) obeying (3.97), at the earned exponent;

    (A3)

    the hard precursor of Subsubsection 3.13.2, of bound \(2 V_Q e^{- E_0}\);

    (A4)

    the soft precursor bounds of the ordinary and raised paths, at most \(y^{- 1 / 8}\) and \(e^{\chi_0} y^{- 1 / 8}\), together with the two final soft demands \(f\) and \(f_+\) as rooted bounds on the actual reset and path arrays;

    (A5)

    the two rate payments (3.84) and (3.85), for the ordinary and the raised path;

    (A6)

    the tuning law, that is, differentiability of the response in the menu parameter with summable value-and-derivative majorants.

Here \(y = \hat{\beta}_{i + 1}\) is the fine coupling of the stored step.

The proofs of these items in Sections 13.10.3–13.10.10 use local analytic estimates on the stored step. We name them explicitly. On each stored step \(i < N\), with its stored tube, incoming representation \(R_{N, p, i + 1}\) and local return:

    (L1)

    the funded path, shell, guide and local precision bounds of Sections 8.5, 8.12, 8.13 and 8.14;

    (L2)

    the seed-energy, chart, annulus and normalization estimates entering the packet value bound of Subsection 8.25;

    (L3)

    the differentiated local estimates of Sections 8.28 and 8.20, including the differentiated precision and compensation bounds;

    (L4)

    the finite short-panel localization and folding estimates of Sections 12.9 and 12.4, which give (12.61) and its discrepancy bound;

    (L5)

    the reset decomposition, its loss and boundary-hit estimates, and the path footprint sums of Subsection 12.10.

These are not new hypotheses on the selected history. They are the local nonlinear estimates whose thresholds the common delays of Subsection 13.1 pay before the scale realization: the sector integrals with their Gaussian prefactors and shell bounds (L1), the marked and unmarked local bounds (L2), (L3), the small-field localization and parameter comparison (L4), and the scalar reset (L5). Subsection 13.1 applies them to the same tube, paths, budget points and local return to construct the selected history itself, and the uniform derivative bank of Subsection 13.4 is the one used in (L3). They are needed here at exactly the same data, as the next proof shows.

Theorem 13.30. (Retention estimates on the public run) Keep the selected history of this section, with the common purchases of Sections 13.1 and 13.4 and the local analytic estimates (L1)–(L5) which those purchases pay. Include in the common delays the numerical requests of Lemmas 13.26 and 13.23, Proposition 13.28, the two smallness conditions (13.29), the thresholds of Sections 13.10.9 and 13.10.10, and the tariff and large-coupling thresholds named in Proposition 13.20. Make the fixed requests of those results with earned exponent \(a_i = \alpha_i + 3\) at target level \(i\), any fixed reserve \(e > 0\), component exponent \(E = a_i + 2 e\), a rate \(r \in (- 1 / 2, 0)\) and a fixed derivative order \(j \geqslant 1\).

Then for every cutoff \(N\), period \(p\) and step \(0 \leqslant i < N\), the stored interface carries the receipts (A1)–(A6) on the retained representations \(R_{N, p, i + 1}\), \(R_{N, p, i}\). Consequently every conclusion of Proposition 13.20 holds on the selected history without its arrival, raised-response and slot-preservation hypotheses: the paid response of the fixed interface to every admitted finite priced offer, its ordinary and flagged families with rooted bounds \(1\) and \(w_i = 2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 16}\) at exponents \(\alpha_i + 1 / 2\) and \(\alpha_i + 1\), the quiet and singleton identities, the exact push, soundness and tuning, retention of the upper contact addresses, the displayed inequalities for \(\delta_i, T_i, c_i\), and the terminal endowment at every \(N \geqslant 0\). All thresholds are fixed before the history is selected and are uniform in \(N\), \(p\), the finite menu, the forest, tags and sectors, and the retained member of the shooting interval.

Proof. Order of the choices. Every request listed in the theorem is a fixed constant chosen with the profiles and before the block lengths, or a lower threshold for the coupling at a fixed pair of stages. The cluster reserve \(e\) is distinct from the length and polynomial reserve \(\varepsilon\) of Subsubsection 13.10.8. Choose \(\varepsilon > 0\) with \(4 \varepsilon\) strictly below the incoming support exponent and \(8 \varepsilon\) strictly below both adjacent path widths, and choose the component tree reserve \(\delta > 0\). These choices are possible because those exponents and widths are positive, and they precede \(L\). The cover conversions at the component exponent \(E\) and head exponent \(E + 2 \varepsilon + \delta\) are those of Subsection 8.24, made before \(L\) as in Proposition 13.28. Each threshold result above holds on a tail of couplings. At a stage there are finitely many permitted target stages. As in the proofs of Lemmas 13.26 and 13.23, replace the current delay at each stage by its maximum with these thresholds and then by its running maximum. This is one more step of the successive enlargement in Subsection 13.1, before the scale realization. It preserves every earlier purchase. The selected history is constructed afterwards from these delays, so all the threshold conclusions hold at \(x = \hat{\beta}_i\) for every stored step, including \(i = 0\). Nothing is chosen after a finite menu, forest or tag is fixed.

The local estimates are those of the stored step. By Lemma 13.27 the representation at \(i + 1\) is an admissible input of the local estimates: its path and high clip are those of the stored tube, its threshold, radius, coupling band and activity allowances are the purchased ones, and its loop allowance is at most \(2 P\). By Lemma 13.25 the stored tube has adjacent quadratic compatibility. The local return of the stored step is the return constructed in Subsection 13.1 on that tube at the retained interval point, by the stored identities recalled in the proof of Proposition 13.20. Hence (L1)–(L5) are applied to the same input, tube, return and budget points at which they were purchased. No other representation, horizon or return appears in the following steps.

(A1). The offer bound is at most \(y_i = x_{i + 1}^{- 1}\) at exponent \(\alpha_{i + 1} - 4\), with fine sites in the crests and parent occupancy at most \(r_i\). Lemma 13.22 applies to the stored interface's mixed alphabet. Its extra site bound (13.16) is the shipment conversion of Theorem 3.33, which uses only the offer, the occupancy bound, the birth geometry and the tariff. Its common mixed-body hypothesis is Lemma 13.24, whose warning inputs are Lemma 13.26 under (L1). This gives the menu-augmented alphabet and the ticket product (13.17) on the actual labels.

(A2). Proposition 13.28, under (L1) and (L2), gives the common value bounds and the packet bound. Lemma 13.29 sums the actual replacement fibers, and (13.28) defines the pledges \(P_0\) and \(P_X\). With \(a = a_i\) and the reserve \(e\), the actual thin weights obey the vertex inequality of Subsubsection 3.13.1 with budget \(P_0\) at exponent \(a + 2 e \leqslant E\), and (13.29) gives its tree condition with \(\rho = h^{r / 2} \leqslant 1 / 2\). That section admits any ratio \(\rho < 1\) satisfying these two inequalities, and \(h = \beta_F\) is its fine coupling; we use this one. Set

\(\displaystyle E_0 = M = \mu P^2 / 4, \qquad E' = P_X, \qquad P_1 = P_X e^{- M}, \qquad V_Q = V.\)

The packet-containing budget of Lemma 13.29 is \(P_1\). Since \(\rho \leqslant 1 / 2\),

\(\displaystyle 4 e^{E_0} P_1 + \frac{4 P_0}{1 - \rho} \leqslant 4 P_X + 8 P_0 \leqslant 10 (P_0 + P_X) = V \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2},\)

which is (3.97). Both \(P_0\) and \(P_X\) have upper exponent \(r < 0\), as that section requires. By (3.80) the logarithmic response has rooted bound \(2 P_0 / (1 - \rho) \leqslant 4 P_0 \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2}\) at exponent \(a + e\). Since \(\alpha_i + 1 / 2 \leqslant a + e\), it has rooted bound at most one at exponent \(\alpha_i + 1 / 2\), which is the ordinary output budget of Proposition 13.20.

(A3). The decorated precursor bound (13.33) with \(m = 0\) is \(2 Ve^{- M} = 2 V_Q e^{- E_0}\). By the flattening identity cited there it is the bound of the actual precursor gas. Since \(V \leqslant 1 / 2\), it is at most \(e^{- \mu P^2 / 4}\), the hard input of Lemma 13.23. The same holds for the finite-menu version, with the summed ticket bounds.

(A4). By Subsubsection 13.10.10, under (L4), the first soft correction arrays have bounds \(b_0\) and \(b_+\), with \(b_0 \leqslant h^{- 1 / 8}\) and \(b_+ \leqslant C_{\text{cov}} h^{- 1 / 8}\), where \(C_{\text{cov}} = e^{\chi_0}\). Under (L5) the final correction arrays have rooted bounds \(f_0\) and \(f_+\), the demands (3.82) and (3.83) at the base budget point, the latter attached to the raised budget. These are the four soft receipts of Subsubsection 3.13.2.

(A5). Apply Lemma 13.23 with \(a = 2 V_Q e^{- E_0}\), and with \(b = b_0\) on the ordinary path and \(b = b_+\) on the raised path. Its first conclusion (13.18) and the hard/soft fusion of Subsection 8.32 give the hard bound \(H_1\) after widening. Its second conclusion (13.19), with \(f_{\epsilon} = f_0\) or \(f_+\), gives the capital and quote inequalities (3.84) and (3.85) with \(\kappa_2 = \mu P^2 / 16\), and the output bound \(H_2 = 2 e^{- \mu P^2 / 16}\). The raised path supplies the flagged ledger at exponent \(\alpha_i + 1\) with this bound, as stated at the end of Subsubsection 3.13.2. This is the flagged allowance \(w_i\). The ordinary path supplies the gas payment of the usable certificate. Neither path is deduced from the other.

(A6). Subsubsection 13.10.9, under (L3), gives for every inserted original label a common integrable majorant of the marked integrand and its jets through order \(j\) at a healthy base point, with the rooted bound (13.32). Its scalar-interval paragraph in Subsubsection 13.10.10 gives summable joint value-and-derivative majorants for the thin cluster and decorated precursor series on the interval \(| \theta | D_{\ast} < 1\), and (9.2) justifies termwise differentiation. These are the derivative data which fix the tuning law in Subsection 3.16. The polynomial order \(K + j\) used there is the one included in \(P_0\), so the differentiated terms are paid by the same pledge.

Conclusion. The fixed interface is the one-step transport at the retained interval point, and the receipts (A1)–(A6) concern that very interface and the pair \(R_{N, p, i + 1}\), \(R_{N, p, i}\). Lemma 13.21 supplies the slot-preservation hypothesis at every nonterminal entry, including the level-\(m\) dictionaries and flag predicates. Thus both hypotheses of Proposition 13.20 that concern the fixed interfaces hold. Its remaining hypotheses are the fixed profile and the delays, which include the tariff and large-coupling thresholds by the first step. Proposition 13.20 therefore gives all the asserted conclusions. Its terminal endowment uses only the schedule.

Index zero and uniformity. Every lemma used above requires only \(i < N\), so the direct stored step at \(i = 0\) is included; no default interface is used. The per-marked lifting service projection restricts the same conclusions to \(1 \leqslant k \leqslant i < N\). The value bounds, component sums and warning bounds are uniform in the forest, tags and sectors, and the forest and tag averages are probability averages. Selecting a member of the shooting interval keeps the representations, paths and budget points on which all the bounds were proved.\(\Box\)

The theorem does not prove the local estimates (L1)–(L5) themselves. They are the incoming local interface of the whole selected history and are proved, or recorded as inputs, in their own sections. It also does not supply the auxiliary-history transition hypothesis used for the fixed-level limits at the beginning of this section, which concerns different histories. The consumers in Subsection 3.16 still prepare their own finite lists and prove their slice integrability; the theorem supplies the paid response to any such list which is an admitted offer.

13.10.12The service at every reference level

Fix a grade \(d\), a period \(p\), a cutoff \(N\), and \(t_d \leqslant k \leqslant N\); in particular \(k \geqslant 1\). We verify the inputs of Theorem 4.15 for this family.

Theorem 13.30 supplies the adjacent retention and terminal endowment on the selected replay, by removing the interface hypotheses of Proposition 13.20. Separately, Proposition 13.15 constructs the marked density and its terminal forced-profile identity on the startup representation when the reserved addresses, lawfulness, sound section and paid entrance fee are available. The numerical endowment does not depend on that label construction.

The room and bank constants are those chosen for the local coefficient maps. The scale inequalities in Subsection 4.6 give their common marker bounds

\(\displaystyle \zeta_{d, m} = \hat{\beta}_m^{- (\deg F_d + 4)}, \qquad \phi_m = \hat{\beta}_{m + 1}^{- 1}, \qquad \delta_m = \hat{\beta}_{m + 1}^{- 2}, \qquad \eta_m = \tfrac{1}{4} \hat{\beta}_m^{- 1 / 2} .\)

Here \(F_d\) is the finite coefficient frame. Their strict positivity, local coefficient bounds, adjacent-bound growth and support inequalities are proved there. Since \(d \leqslant J (k) \leqslant J (m)\), the finite-grade inequalities purchased at stage \(J (m)\) apply at every \(m \geqslant k\). The occupancy bound at level \(m\) is \(N_{J (m + 1)}\), which is nondecreasing because both maps are nondecreasing.

The same scale estimates give

\(\displaystyle B_m \geqslant 8, \qquad \delta_m \phi_m e^{B_m / 128} \geqslant 1, \qquad B_{m + 1}^2 \leqslant L_m B_m^2 .\)

The stain/contact bound pays the simultaneous forced support requirement. These inequalities continue to hold after restriction to any reference level \(k\); no constant is selected from \(N\). For the local-section norm use its fixed camera constant \(C_d\). The fitted stencil has cover at most one and its values are bounded by \(C_d\), uniformly in the cutoff and period. Its paid coefficient bound is precisely the bank bound in the scale estimates. The cover bound just stated is the footprint-cover clause of the height record, item (iii) of Theorem 4.15; the support-size inequalities of its item (ii) are among the scale estimates displayed above. Subsection 4.4 is not used for either: it supplies the occupancy, migration and separation of the label sites, which enter only in the moment bound below.

The backward transfer data supply the remaining finite transfer record. Its coefficient and activity bounds are the two separate ledgers in Subsection 4.3; its adjacent identities use the selected maps, and its terminal forced-profile identity is the singleton hook of Proposition 13.31, retained by Proposition 13.35. The third identity in (10.45) instead compares adjacent profiles; it does not supply the terminal hook. The latter is essential because the scalar integral identity alone would not give the terminal marked polynomial. All these conclusions hold for every \((d, k, N, p)\) in the stated range, with the already fixed reference levels. These are the remaining service fields under the stated local purchases, finite backward construction and selected-replay hypotheses.

We may therefore apply Theorem 4.15 and the terminal birth calculation. At a reference level \(k\), a finite label list with paid weights and separated origins has normalized moment bound

\(\displaystyle \exp \{| \Lambda_k |\mathcal{T}_k + 6|\mathcal{V}|\} .\)

Let \(m \leqslant k\) now denote the root level opened at \(k\), as in Subsection 4.4, rather than a running level. Take one label at each site of \(\Lambda_m\), so that \(M = |\mathcal{V}| = | \Lambda_m |\), placed by the aligned embeddings of Subsection 4.4. With \(n_j\) the side of the periodic lattice \(\Lambda_j\) and \(q_k = a_m / a_k\), that section records \(n_k = q_k n_m\); since \(| \Lambda_j | = n_j^4\), this gives the site identity \(| \Lambda_k | = M (a_m / a_k)^4\). The normalized \(M\)th root is consequently bounded by

\(\displaystyle \exp \{(a_m / a_k)^4 \mathcal{T}_k + 6\} / w.\)

This is the centered estimate for the same family, simultaneously at all suitable periods. The fixed-level convergence just retained and the removal of higher marker terms in Subsection 4.8 then give convergence along the full mesh sequence at every fixed suitable period. The finite-difference bounds of Subsection 4.11 apply to the same transfer data, so its limiting correlations have the smoothness proved in Subsection 4.10.

The further calibration of an arbitrary marked section and the rank and ultraviolet comparisons must still be made on this family. They will use the same reference levels and transfers; the centered construction above is already fixed before those comparisons or a volume subsequence are chosen.

Relation to the paid selected-history record.
Subsection 13.9 consumes a record (R1)–(R7) on the same family and the same transition records. Its parameters are those of this section: the stage function \(W\) is the purchased worth, \(u_i = \eta_i\), \(\phi_i\), \(\delta_i\) and \(\zeta_{d, i}\) are the marker bounds displayed above, \(r_i = N_{J (i + 1)}\), the interfaces are the stored transition records, and \(\psi_i\) is the common transfer bound (10.46) at level \(i + 1\). With these parameters, the present section assembles the following items, each from the inputs named in its place above. The room and bank inequalities (R1) are those of the purchased budget. The scale bounds (R2) are the positivity of the marker bounds and the three displayed inequalities. The monotonicity in (R3) is that of the occupancy bound. Proposition 13.20 gives (R4) under its stated hypotheses. Of (R5), this section supplies \(\phi_i \leqslant 1\), \(\psi_i \geqslant 0\), the second, third and fourth displayed inequalities, the bound \(X = C_d\), the cover number one, and the terminal inequality for \(u_N\). The backward transfer data, with their interface-by-interface comparison at bound \(\psi_i\) and their terminal forced-profile identity, are (R6). Proposition 13.31 below identifies the terminal coefficient family of (R6) on the stored top representation, with its singleton hook. Proposition 13.35 attaches the adjacent ledgers and finite-support comparisons to one transfer sequence on that coefficient family; its one-step input is proved in Proposition 13.34. Three items are not part of the centered service and are not derived in this section: \(r_i \geqslant 1\) in (R3); the positivity \(\zeta_{d, i} > 0\) and the first inequality of (R5), which is (13.5) for the bounds above; and the paid one-step input (R7). They belong to the stronger form of the funded continuation described in the proof of Proposition 13.1: \(r_i \geqslant 1\) is the prescribed lower bound of the occupancy schedule, positivity is that of \(\hat{\beta}_i\), the first inequality of (R5) is the tail clause of the delay purchased in Subsection 13.4, and (R7) is the local transition estimate on the fixed interfaces. For (R7), Proposition 13.36 below proves the tuning inequality from the grade-zero floor of Subsection 13.4, together with the signs. It reduces the zero-coordinate transfer record to the regular and forced insertion estimates, soundness of the fixed interface, and the countable Transfer record merger. Under the common local-purchase hypotheses, the first two are attached to the fixed interface in the ticket verification of Proposition 13.34, and the last is Lemma 13.33. The proof of Proposition 13.1 names this estimate but does not derive it. For none of the items has this section a self-contained proof: each rests on the cited producers.

13.10.13Terminal coefficient families and transfer sequences

A coefficient family assigns a marked coefficient to every label at one level. A transfer sequence collects these families across levels. The precise admissibility conditions are given below. This subsubsection treats item (R6) of Subsection 13.9, and the corresponding clause of the service record consumed in Theorem 4.15. Two things are needed. The top of the backward transfer data must be the marked coefficient family of Proposition 13.15 on the stored top representation \(R_{N, p, N}\). Every adjacent comparison must then be attached to one and the same family of coefficient families, whose transfer entries are the stored transfer entries. The profile-difference identity in (10.45) concerns one transition and an arbitrary response \(\psi\). It does not identify the terminal forced profile. That identification is assertion (4) of Proposition 13.15, which we now place on the stored representation.

Fix a grade \(d\), a period \(p\) and a cutoff \(N\) with \(t_d \leqslant N\). Then \(d + 3 \leqslant J (t_d) \leqslant J (N)\). Write \(R^{\top} = R_{N, p, N}\) for the stored top representation, \(S\) for the grade-\(d\) local section with its fixed camera constant \(C_d \geqslant 1\), and \(w^{\text{f}}_N\) for the forced allowance of level \(N\). A coefficient family at level \(m\) is a complex-linear family \(v \mapsto V (v)\) of coefficient fields on \(R_{N, p, m}\), together with a coefficient index \(v \mapsto x (v)\), which is a complex-linear isomorphism of \(F\), and a charge \(c (v) \geqslant 0\). We say that a coefficient family is admissible at \(m\) if all of the following hold for every \(v\):

  • the lead, the regular part and the forced part of \(V (v)\) are complex-linear in \(v\), and their supports do not depend on \(v\);

  • \(x (a_N^d e_0) = a_m^d e_0\), where \(e_0\) is the vacuum coordinate;

  • \(\text{dens} \hspace{0.17em} V (v)\) is complex-linear in \(v\), and for every signed lattice motion \(g\), with or without complex conjugation, the density of \(V (v)\) transported by \(g\) is \(\text{dens} \hspace{0.17em} V (g \cdot v)\);

  • \(V (v)\) has the terminal format of Subsubsection 13.10.14 at the origin of \(R_{N, p, m}\), and remainder bound \(c (v)\) at weight \(\sqrt{w^{\text{f}}_m}\);

  • its coordinate vector is \(x (v)\);

  • \(c (v) \leqslant s_m \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} x (v)\|_1\), where \(s_m \geqslant 1\) is the column count at level \(m\);

  • \(c (u + v) \leqslant c (u) + c (v)\);

  • the index commutes with the signed lattice motions.

A coefficient family satisfies the profile covariance at \(m\) if, for every motion \(g\), transporting the forced profile of \(V (v)\) by \(g\) gives the forced profile of \(V (g \cdot v)\). A transfer sequence is a choice of one coefficient family at every level \(m \leqslant N\).

Proposition 13.31. (The stored terminal coefficient family) Keep the selected history and the purchases of Subsection 13.4. Let \(d, p, N\) be as above. Then the four hypotheses of Proposition 13.15 hold for \(R^{\top}\), its local section \(S\) with constant \(C_d\), and \(w = w^{\text{f}}_N\). The marked coefficient family \(\mathcal{V}^{\top} (v) =\mathcal{V} (v)\) of that proposition is therefore defined on the stored top representation. Its index is the identity, \(x^{\top} (v) = v\), and its charge is \(c^{\top} (v) = \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\). For every \(v \in F\):

  1. \(\mathcal{V}^{\top} (v)\) has lead \(v\) and zero regular part;

  2. the density identity (13.10) holds, \(\text{dens} \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{V}^{\top} (v) = a_N^d R^{\top} \hspace{0.17em} \text{initial}_{N, p} (v)\);

  3. the forced profile of \(\mathcal{V}^{\top} (v)\) on the activities of \(R^{\top}\) equals the attachment of the singleton response \(f_v\) on the footprint \(Y\) (the singleton hook);

  4. \(\mathcal{V}^{\top}\) is admissible at \(N\) and satisfies the profile covariance at \(N\).

Proof. The stored top representation. By the choice of the history at the beginning of this section, \(R^{\top}\) is the member \(F_{\beta}\) of the exact Wilson startup family of Proposition 12.6, at the coupling of the retained parameter. That proposition applies at level \(N\) under the purchased schedule, and \(F_{\beta}\) satisfies the retained-history conditions at level \(N\).

Hypothesis (1). The forced arrays of \(F_{\beta}\) have the locality, measurability and gauge invariance required of the dictionary. Among the retained-history conditions of Proposition 12.6 (second part) is the forced stage bound at the allowance \(\delta_K\) of level \(N\). That bound is the rooted support sum \(\sup_x \sum_{i : \hspace{0.17em} x \in X_i} e^{E \text{cov}_B (X_i)} k_i \leqslant \delta_K\) of a majorant family \(k_i \geqslant |K_i |\), where \(E\) and \(B\) are the support exponent and cover radius of level \(N\). This is (13.8) with \(w = \delta_K\). Moreover \(\delta_K = \exp \{- M^{1 / 5} \}\) with \(M = \exp ((\log \log \hat{\beta}_N)^2)\), which is the allowance \(w (\hat{\beta}_N)\) of Subsection 13.7. This is the forced allowance \(w^{\text{f}}_N\) of the history.

Hypothesis (2). Proposition 12.6 states that the fixed terminal dictionary contains, for every forced support \(X\) with nonempty inventory \(I\) and every natural number, a reserved forced label with support \(X\) and inventory \(I\). It also states that the forced coefficient of \(F_{\beta}\) on every reserved label is zero, and that the additional predicate of such a label holds exactly when \(X \cap \text{flags} (U) = I\). Hence hypothesis (2) holds. Selecting one reserved label for each pair \((X, I)\) gives an injective assignment of an address with tag \((X, I)\), eligible whenever \(X \cap \text{flags} (U) = I\). This is the reserved-slot assignment of \(R^{\top}\), and it is the property recorded in the nesting receipt of the run.

Hypothesis (3). The grade-\(d\) local section is the fixed sound section of the frame. It is the section used in the selected root-test estimate of Subsection 4.7, whose hypotheses include its soundness. Its axis values are bounded by the fixed camera constant \(C_d \geqslant 1\) at every configuration, as recorded in the service at every reference level above.

Hypothesis (4). Since \(d \leqslant J (N)\), the final sentence of Proposition 13.15 applies at the admitted terminal level \(N\). The fee thresholds are among the entrance delays of Subsection 13.4, as stated in the third subsection of Subsection 13.7.

Conclusions. Assertions (1)–(3) are the definition of \(\mathcal{V} (v)\) together with assertions (3) and (4) of Proposition 13.15. For (4), the format clauses are assertion (1) there together with the zero regular part. The origin lies in every footprint support \(T_b \supseteq Y\). The remainder bound with \(a_{\text{r}} = 0\) and \(a_{\text{f}} = \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\) at weight \(\sqrt{w^{\text{f}}_N}\) is assertion (2) there. Indeed \(\text{mass} (T_b, e) = M (T_b) e\), so the forced mass sum is at most \(\sqrt{w^{\text{f}}_N} \hspace{0.17em} a_{\text{f}}\). The cone bound is \(\| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1 \leqslant s_N \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} v\|_1\), since \(s_N \geqslant 1\). Subadditivity is the triangle inequality for the core norm. Covariance of the index is linearity of the motion action. The index \(x = \text{id}\) is an isomorphism and satisfies \(x (a_N^d e_0) = a_N^d e_0\). The lead, regular and forced entries are complex-linear in \(v\) by the first conclusion of Proposition 13.15. The regular part is zero, and the forced entry at the address \(s (b)\) has support \(T_b\) for every \(v\), so no support depends on \(v\). Density linearity follows from (13.10), and density covariance is assertion (5) there. Profile covariance at \(N\) follows from assertion (4) there: the singleton response satisfies \(f_{g \cdot v} = g \cdot f_v\), and its attachment on \(Y\) is transported by the motion together with the footprint, as in the covariance paragraph of the second subsection of Subsection 13.7.\(\Box\)

For the adjacent steps we use the following one-step input. It is the quantitative content of a step of Lemma 4.8 on the fixed interface, in the form in which the finite-support comparison is exported. For \(m < N\), let \(s_{m + 1}\) be the column count at level \(m + 1\), \(L_m\) the block side and \(\psi_m\) the common transfer bound (10.46). A transfer witness across the fixed interface \(D_m\) between coefficient families \(V'\) at \(m + 1\) and \(V\) at \(m\) consists of two things. The first is a finite set of insertion types \(W\). The second is, for every \(v\), a response decomposition on \(W\) across \(D_m\), in the sense of Subsubsection 13.10.14. This witness relates the upper input data of \(V' (v)\) to the lower forced profile of \(V (v)\):

  • the exponential entry and the forced profile on \(R_{N, p, m + 1}\);

  • the vacuum (\(e_0\)) coordinate of \(x' (v)\);

  • the physical factor \(L_m^d\);

  • input bound \(C_{\text{orb}} \|x' (v)\|_1 \hspace{0.17em} (\psi_m + 2 s_{m + 1})\) and output bound \(c (v)\).

    (RS)

    One-step transfer on the fixed interface. Let \(m\) satisfy \(t_d \leqslant m < N\). Suppose \(V'\) is a coefficient family at \(m + 1\) that is admissible and satisfies the profile covariance there. Then there is a coefficient family \(V\) at \(m\) with four properties:

    • \(V\) is admissible at \(m\) and satisfies the profile covariance at \(m\);

    • for every \(v\), \(\text{dens} \hspace{0.17em} V (v)\) is the push of \(\text{dens} \hspace{0.17em} V' (v)\) through the stored transition of step \(m\), as in Lemma 4.8;

    • the coefficient indices and charges satisfy the paid adjacent cone and charge inequalities of Subsection 4.3;

    • there is a transfer witness across \(D_m\) between \(V'\) and \(V\).

The input (RS) is what the paid ticket of the fixed interface is to supply. That ticket consists of:

  • a finite set of insertion types containing the reserved-slot assignment keys of \(R_{N, p, m + 1}\);

  • a heading;

  • the estimates of Subsection 10.10;

  • soundness of \(D_m\);

  • \(\psi_m \geqslant 0\);

  • \(\sqrt{w^{\text{f}}_{m + 1}} \leqslant 1\);

  • the room and bank bounds.

We now verify this ticket and its summation on the selected replay.

Summing the component comparisons

We first supply the countable summation step used in the finite-column construction. All the coefficients in this paragraph belong to one lawful representation, with the same anchor, background holonomy and local section, at one positive forced weight \(\kappa\). Each regular atom satisfies the prescribed regularity package and ordered-derivative bounds in the spatially weighted directions of Subsection 7.9; retaining an atom separately retains these properties. The mass convention is the one recalled in Subsubsection 13.10.14.

Lemma 13.32. (Countable addition of coefficients) Let \(P_n\) be formatted coefficient fields with remainder bounds \(r_n\), where \(\sum_n r_n < \infty\), and suppose \(\sum_n \|P_n .x\|_1 < \infty\). There is a formatted coefficient \(P\) whose lead is \(\sum_n P_n .x\), whose regular atoms are the separate atoms of all the \(P_n\), and whose forced coefficient at an address \(a\) is \(\sum_n P_n . \text{forced}_a\). It has remainder bound \(\sum_n r_n\). Its exponential entry, forced profile and density are the respective sums of those of the \(P_n\). The assertion also holds for the turn-weighted remainder bounds used in a transfer record, with their same turn constant.

Proof. For each \(n\) retain its nonnegative regular and forced allocations \(r_n^{\text{r}}, r_n^{\text{f}}\), with \(r_n^{\text{r}} + r_n^{\text{f}} \leqslant r_n\), and their atom majorants \(b_{n, j}, d_{n, a}\). Their mass sums obey

\(\displaystyle \sum_j \text{mass} (X_{n, j}, b_{n, j}) \leqslant r_n^{\text{r}}, \qquad \sum_a \text{mass} (S_a, d_{n, a}) \leqslant \kappa r_n^{\text{f}} .\)

Enumerate the pairs \((n, j)\) by one countable regular dictionary. Every regular function keeps its original support and all its format and regularity properties; no derivative of a newly summed regular function is required. At a forced address use the majorant \(d_a = \sum_n d_{n, a}\). Tonelli and linearity of mass on a fixed support give

\(\displaystyle \sum_{n, j} \text{mass} (X_{n, j}, b_{n, j}) \leqslant \sum_n r_n^{\text{r}}, \qquad \sum_a \text{mass} (S_a, d_a) \leqslant \kappa \sum_n r_n^{\text{f}} .\)

Since mass dominates the value majorant, all the forced sums converge absolutely and uniformly. They preserve measurability, gauge invariance and dependence on \(S_a\). Outside the eligibility set every summand vanishes; if the sum is nonzero, its common support contains the anchor. Thus the forced format is preserved. These two displayed bounds prove the remainder assertion. A turn-weighted bound is proved by retaining the separate allocations and summing \(C_{\text{turn}} r_n^{\text{r}} + r_n^{\text{f}}\), rather than replacing the turn constant by a larger one.

The finite-dimensional lead sum converges absolutely. The local section is linear and bounded in this norm on the finite-volume configuration space. Hence its placement commutes with the lead sum. The masked regular terms are bounded by \(b_{n, j}\), so their double series also converges absolutely. This proves the exponential-entry identity. For each tag the forced-profile identity follows by interchanging its address sum with the \(n\) sum, using \(\sum_{n, a} d_{n, a} < \infty\).

For densities use the dressed-field bound (13.34), applied at the present level. It is uniform in the address and configuration. The lawful representation's exponential and evaluation have the finite-volume envelopes used in the same proof. Consequently the density series has a constant majorant times

\(\displaystyle \sum_n \|P_n .x\|_1 + \sum_{n, j} b_{n, j} + \sum_{n, a} d_{n, a} < \infty .\)

The density formula in Subsubsection 13.10.14 can therefore be summed term by term. This proves its identity as well as the required measurable domination. These envelopes may depend on the finite volume; no such factor is added to the remainder bounds.\(\Box\)
