Lemma 13.33. (Addition of the comparison witnesses) Consider countably many transfer records on the same finite set of insertion types of the same sound fixed interface. Let their predicted leads be \(v_n\), their lead-error bounds \(c_n\), their remainder bounds \(r_n\), and their input bounds \(u_n\). Suppose these three nonnegative bound sequences and \(\|v_n \|_1\) are summable. Suppose also that their exponential entries and forced profiles have the stated pointwise sums, their unit values are summable, and their input densities have a measurable dominated sum. Then they add to a transfer record for the summed data, with predicted lead \(\sum_n v_n\) and bounds \((\sum_n c_n, \sum_n r_n, \sum_n u_n)\). In particular its response decomposition has input bound \(\sum_n u_n\) and output bound \(\sum_n r_n\).

Proof. Choose the coefficient \(P_n\) and the comparison witnesses of each Transfer record. Its lead estimate gives

\(\displaystyle \|P_n .x\|_1 \leqslant \|v_n \|_1 + c_n / C_{\text{orb}} .\)

Thus the actual leads are summable, a requirement separate from remainder summability. Lemma 13.32 constructs the summed coefficient. The triangle inequality gives its lead-error bound \(\sum_n c_n\) and its turn-weighted remainder bound is \(\sum_n r_n\). The selected transition has a bounded measurable kernel on this finite volume. Dominated convergence therefore passes the input density sum through each transition slice. Together with the density identity of the preceding lemma this proves the exact pushed-density identity, retaining the single physical factor \(L_m^d\).

For completeness the two costs of the comparison witness must also be added. A response decomposition consists of a countable priced list of real elementary insertions with complex coefficients, all of whose keys lie in the specified insertion support, and a local response. The list represents the input exponential entry after subtracting its unit value and represents its forced profile. The output forced profile is the transported list, multiplied by \(L_m^d\), plus the hooked local response. Its input cost is the sum of the absolute coefficient-weighted insertion masses; its output cost is the reserved response mass. More explicitly, if the leaves are \(M_j\), their complex weights are \(z_j\), the response is \(\Psi\), the input entry and profile are \(\alpha, \omega\), the output profile is \(\beta\), and the unit is \(\delta\), these identities are

\(\displaystyle \sum_j z_j \text{prose} (M_j) = \alpha - \delta, \qquad \sum_j z_j \text{entry} (M_j) = \omega, \qquad \sum_j L_m^d z_j \text{quoted} (D_m, M_j) = \beta - \text{hook} (\Psi) .\)

Here prose is the ordinary insertion on a regular leaf and zero on a forced leaf; entry is the insertion in its tagged forced coordinate and zero on a regular leaf. The quoted profile is the forced response of that leaf under the fixed interface. These are the three identities (10.45), with the unit kept separately. Each response slot depends only on the links in its prescribed support, is gauge invariant and Borel measurable, and vanishes when that support is healthy. A nonzero slot has support containing the anchor. No derivative condition is imposed on these response slots.

Take the disjoint union of these lists over \(n\), retaining each insertion and its coefficient. Every key is still in the original insertion support. Tonelli bounds its purchase mass by \(\sum_n u_n\). For the responses, retain the majorants \(b_{n, X}\) of the slot \(\Psi_n (X, \cdot)\), and define

\(\displaystyle b_X = \sum_n b_{n, X}, \qquad \Psi (X, U) = \sum_n \Psi_n (X, U) .\)

Reserved mass is linear in the amplitude on a fixed support and dominates that amplitude. Tonelli therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_X \text{reservedMass} (X, b_X) \leqslant \sum_n r_n, \qquad | \Psi (X, U) | \leqslant b_X .\)

Every slot sum is absolutely and uniformly convergent. Dependence on its support, gauge invariance and healthy vanishing pass termwise to the sum, and pointwise limits preserve Borel measurability. If a summed slot is nonzero, some summand is nonzero, so its support contains the anchor. This proves the response bound with its exact sum of output bounds.

Each input leaf's ordinary entry and tagged forced entry are bounded by its weight, hence by its purchased mass. Thus the input identities can be summed absolutely and their iterated sums give the stated inputs, at every configuration and every profile tag. For the output identity use the volume bound in soundness of this same interface: the forced mass of the response to a leaf \(M\) is at most \(w \text{load} (M)\) for a fixed \(w\). Consequently the double sum of quoted forced profiles is absolutely bounded by \(w\) times the total purchased list mass (with the fixed factor \(L_m^d\)). For the hooked responses use (7.64) and (7.66): their total absolute forced mass is bounded by a fixed multiple of their reserved response mass. Hence both sides of the output-profile identity are absolutely summable. Sum the component identities and interchange the list, response and address sums. The result is exactly the response decomposition for the merged coefficient, with the two costs asserted in the statement. This also proves addition for a finite collection, multiplication by a scalar with costs multiplied by its modulus, and monotonicity in all three bounds. Zero fields and empty lists give the zero Transfer record at any nonnegative bounds.\(\Box\)

The one-step transfer at an actual selected step

Proposition 13.34. (The one-step transfer is available) Under the selected-history and common-purchase hypotheses of this section, (RS) holds at every \(t_d \leqslant m < N\). Its finite set of insertion types is fixed before the coefficient vector. The lower coefficient family, its charge, its index and its comparison witness are constructed together on the stored transition.

Proof. The local input and its ticket. Use the stored interval witness at \(m\) and its retained parameter. Lemma 13.27 identifies its incoming representation, paths, clipping and coupling band with those of the funded interpolation. The purchases of Sections 13.1 and 13.4 include the primary, regular and forced insertion estimates at every grade through the permitted stage. Here \(d + 3 \leqslant J (t_d) \leqslant J (m)\), so the section, derivative and kernel orders required in Subsection 10.10 are available. The local section and the sound marked derivative map are those of that same step. Soundness also follows from Theorem 13.30.

Lemma 13.21 gives the upper Keeper, including its exact eligibility predicate. On the finite fine torus choose one ordinary key for each anchored support and the reserved-slot assignment key for each support–inventory tag. There are only finitely many tags. Their union is an insertion support \(W_0\), independent of the incoming coefficient. Subsection 10.10 supplies on this insertion support the three component transfer records with a common heading \(T = B \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L_m}\) and bounds \(C, \eta, t\), where

\(\displaystyle \eta = L_m^{- 2 / 5}, \qquad t = \psi_m, \qquad \kappa_f = \sqrt{w^{\text{f}}_{m + 1}} \in (0, 1] .\)

Its primary bound uses the same local-section bound as \(\psi_m\); the forced remainder is divided by \(\kappa_f\), whereas its input bound is not. All bound choices precede the history. The purchased inequalities (4.40) hold on this step.

The covariance cancellation. The evaluation of every stored representation is strictly positive. At the terminal scale it is the positive Wilson density. At an adjacent step the blocking noise is a continuous strictly positive function on the compact pair of configuration spaces, hence has a positive minimum. The transition density is its mixture with nonnegative likelihood weights summing to one, and has the same positive lower bound. The exact retained density identity expresses the lower evaluation as its integral against the positive upper evaluation, up to the positive scalar normalization of the reset. The lawful envelope makes this integral finite; probability Haar measure and pointwise positivity make it positive. Downward induction proves the assertion at every level.

For the given upper coefficient family write \(I = x'\) for its index. Its admissibility clauses supply (H1)–(H6) of Subsection 4.13, and its assumed profile covariance supplies the remaining profile hypothesis there. The sound local section gives the coefficient law under motions. To see why the exponential entry also transforms correctly, subtract the covariant dressed forced term from the covariant density in its defining formula. The dressed term is determined by the forced profile, by the tag argument and absolute domination in Subsubsection 13.10.14. Divide by the nonzero background evaluation just proved. This establishes the needed exponential-entry covariance without imposing covariance of the raw address arrays.

The basis transfer records. For each frame axis \(e_a\) set \(v_a = I^{- 1} e_a\). Repack its forced array into Keeper addresses, by the five-part repacking argument of Proposition 13.36; that argument preserves any lead, not just zero. It preserves the exponential entry, forced profile, density and remainder allocations. Split the result into its primary coordinate, regular atoms and forced atoms. Apply the three ticket clauses to this decomposition, using the exact vacuum transfer record for \(a = 0\). If its regular and forced remainder allocations are \(a_{\text{r}}, a_{\text{f}}\), then their mass sums are at most \(a_{\text{r}}\) and \(\kappa_f a_{\text{f}}\). The lead and remainder error sums are bounded by \(\eta (a_{\text{r}} + a_{\text{f}})\); the input cost is at most \(2 a_{\text{r}} + 2 \kappa_f a_{\text{f}} \leqslant 2 (a_{\text{r}} + a_{\text{f}})\). The predicted leads consist of one primary lead and zeros, so are summable. The input densities have the dominated decomposition of the same repacking proof. Lemma 13.33 therefore applies, including to the two response decomposition costs.

Put \(s = s_{m + 1}\), \(R = C_{\text{orb}}\), \(D (z) = \| \textsf{D}^{\text{gr}}_{d, L_m} (\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} z)\|_1\), \(c = C + \eta s\) and \(\epsilon = \eta (1 + s)\). The upper charge bound is \(c' (v_a) \leqslant s \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a \|_1\). The resulting basis transfer record has predicted lead \(Te_a\), lead error at most \(\epsilon \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} e_a \|_1\) after any permitted motion, remainder bound \(cD (e_a)\), and input bound \(t + 2 s\). Here the motion estimate uses the ticket's turn-weighted remainder and orbit-weighted lead estimate; motion is not assumed to preserve the coordinate norm.

One lower coefficient family for all vectors. Apply the finite construction of Proposition 4.43 to these basis Transfer records. Explicitly take their moved coefficients \(Z_{g, a}\) and put

\(\displaystyle V (v) = \sum_{g, a} \chi_{g, a} (Iv) Z_{g, a}, \qquad x (v) = V (v) .x, \qquad c_- (v) = cRD (Iv) .\)

The chips \(\chi\) include the independent conjugation flag as in (4.41), so the coefficients of this finite sum are complex-linear in \(v\). Its regular supports and forced dictionary are fixed before \(v\). The finite reconstruction identity and upper density linearity give the exact grade-scaled density push. In this formula the physical factor \(L_m^d\) is included once, as in \(\mathcal{T}_m\) of that proposition.

The same proposition gives the format, coarse-weight remainder, motion covariance and two-cost response decomposition of this coefficient. Its readout is an isomorphism by the cone estimate: a zero lower readout forces a zero upper one. Here its room and bank hypotheses follow from Proposition 13.3: monotonicity and \(J (m + 1) \leqslant J (m) + 1\) give \(W (J (m + 1)) \leqslant W (J (m) + 1)\) and \(W (J (m + 1)) \leqslant W (J (m + 1) + 1)\). Thus both room bounds dominate the heading constant required in the interpolation, and the bank bound is stronger than its required one. In particular

\(\displaystyle c_- (v) \leqslant s_m \| \text{core} \hspace{0.17em} x (v)\|_1, \qquad \|Iv\|_1 \leqslant s_{m + 1} \|x (v)\|_1, \qquad \|x (v)\|_1 \leqslant s_{m + 1} L_m^d \|Iv\|_1 .\)

The full direction and charge estimates are those of Lemma 4.42, with the same heading. The charge is nonnegative and subadditive because \(cR \geqslant 0\) and \(D \circ I\) is a seminorm. At the physical unit its error and charge vanish, and \(T (a_{m + 1}^d e_0) = a_m^d e_0\). This proves the unit clause exactly. The density is linear and motion/reality covariant by the finite reconstruction and covariance of the stored kernel. These verify all the admissible clauses, not merely the remainder bound.

The profile of the forced finite sum is its summed profile, by Lemma 13.32. Reindexing the finite motion average shows that it has the required profile covariance. Let \(W\) be the union of all motion images of \(W_0\). It is finite and independent of \(v\). Transport each basis response decomposition to this insertion support, multiply it by its chip, and add. The input entries, profiles and vacuum coordinates recombine to those of the original \(V' (v)\) by the covariance cancellation above and the frame identity. The output profiles recombine to those of \(V (v)\). The weighted chip estimates give the two bounds

\(\displaystyle \sum_{g, a} | \chi_{g, a} (Iv) | (t + 2 s) \leqslant R \|Iv\|_1 (t + 2 s), \qquad \sum_{g, a} | \chi_{g, a} (Iv) | cD (e_a) \leqslant c_- (v) .\)

These are precisely the transfer witness input bound and output charge in (RS). Thus its density, cone, charge, covariance and common-support statements hold for the same lower coefficient family. No history or interface is reselected.\(\Box\)

Proposition 13.35. (One transfer sequence from the terminal coefficient family) Let \(d, p, N\) be as above. Then there is a transfer sequence \((V_m)_{t_d \leqslant m \leqslant N}\) with the following properties:

  1. \(V_N =\mathcal{V}^{\top}\), the coefficient family of Proposition 13.31;

  2. every \(V_m\) is admissible at \(m\) and satisfies the profile covariance at \(m\);

  3. at every fixed interface \(D_m\), \(t_d \leqslant m < N\), the density of \(V_m (v)\) is the push of that of \(V_{m + 1} (v)\), the adjacent cone and charge inequalities hold, and there is a transfer witness between \(V_{m + 1}\) and \(V_m\) with input bound \(C_{\text{orb}} \|x_{m + 1} (v)\|_1 (\psi_m + 2 s_{m + 1})\).

The finite transfer data of Lemma 4.8 may be taken to be the coefficient maps of this transfer sequence, normalized by its reference readout. In particular their terminal forced profile is the singleton hook of Proposition 13.31.

Proof. We use downward induction on \(k\) from \(N\) to \(t_d\). The claim at stage \(k\) is that there is a transfer sequence with \(V_N =\mathcal{V}^{\top}\) such that (2) holds at every \(m \in [k, N]\) and (3) holds at every \(m \in [k, N)\).

For \(k = N\), put \(V_N =\mathcal{V}^{\top}\), and fill the levels below \(N\) with the zero coefficient family. Assertion (4) of Proposition 13.31 gives (2) at \(N\); (3) is vacuous.

Suppose the claim holds at stage \(k + 1\), with \(t_d \leqslant k < N\), and let \((V_m)\) be the transfer sequence it provides. Its coefficient family \(V_{k + 1}\) is admissible and satisfies the profile covariance at \(k + 1\). Apply Proposition 13.34 at \(m = k\) with \(V' = V_{k + 1}\), and replace the coefficient family at level \(k\) by the resulting \(V\). The coefficient families at levels above \(k\) are unchanged, so (1), and (2) and (3) above \(k\), persist. At level \(k\), (2) holds and the interface \(D_k\) satisfies (3) by (RS). This is the claim at stage \(k\). After \(N - t_d\) steps we obtain the transfer sequence.

The construction is finite. Each step modifies one level, and only after the levels above it have been fixed; no coefficient family is chosen after testing a lower one. The density identities, the cone inequalities and the terminal isomorphism \(x_N = \text{id}\) are those used in Lemma 4.8 and its normalization. The coefficient maps of this transfer sequence are therefore a valid choice of the finite transfer data, and their top is \(\mathcal{V}^{\top}\).\(\Box\)

What these propositions supply.
Proposition 13.31 is unconditional relative to the cited results. It discharges, on the stored top representation, the four hypotheses of Proposition 13.15: lawfulness, reserved addresses, the sound section and the paid fee. It exports the lead, the zero regular part, the core charge, (13.10), the admissible clauses and the singleton hook. The reserved addresses are thus not an independent receipt of the run. They follow from the reserved labels of the exact startup family. Proposition 13.35 together with Proposition 13.34 supplies the remaining part of (R6). It places all the adjacent ledgers and finite-support comparisons on one transfer sequence whose top is that coefficient family, under the common selected-history and local-purchase hypotheses of this section.

13.10.14Transfer across a fixed interface with zero coefficient

This subsection treats item (R7) of Subsection 13.9. It proves the tuning inequality from the purchase and the bound bookkeeping outright. The transfer record itself is reduced to three inputs on the same fixed interface: the regular and forced insertion estimates, soundness, and the countable merger of transfer records.

Fix a grade \(d\), a cutoff \(N\), a period \(p\) and a level \(i\) with \(t_d \leqslant i < N\). Then \(d + 3 \leqslant J (t_d) \leqslant J (i)\). Write \(R^+ = R_{N, p, i + 1}\) and \(R^- = R_{N, p, i}\) for the stored upper and lower representations, and \(D = D_{N, p, i}\) for the fixed interface between them. The block side of this step is \(L_i\), the block length chosen at the entrance stage \(J (i + 1)\). Write \(C_i = \text{columns} (i)\) for the column count of the profile at the stage \(J (i)\), and \(\kappa = \sqrt{w^{\text{f}}_{i + 1}}\), so that \(0 < \kappa \leqslant 1\). Put

\(\displaystyle \varrho_i = \text{rate} (L_i) = L_i^{- 2 / 5}, \qquad \text{stepSmall} (L_i) = L_i^{- 3 / 8},\)

and let \(\psi_i = 2 e^{\alpha_{i + 1}} \tau_{i + 1}\) be the common transfer bound (10.46) at the fine level \(i + 1\); here \(\tau_{i + 1} \geqslant 0\) is the fine local-section derivative bound. The number \(\varrho_i\) is the number called \(\eta_i\) in (R7). It is not the marker bound \(\eta_m = \tfrac{1}{4} \hat{\beta}_m^{- 1 / 2}\) displayed earlier in this section, which is the allowance \(u_m\) of (R5). Let \(W_i\) be the finite insertion support chosen above for this step. It contains the principal-mode keys of the supports containing the origin and the keeper key of every support–inventory tag.

Coefficients and their densities.
We recall the data entering a transfer record. A coefficient field \(Q\) on \(R^+\) consists of a coordinate vector \(Q.x \in F\), regular atoms \((X_j, f_j)_{j \geqslant 0}\) with supports \(X_j\) and local functions \(f_j\), and a forced array \((k_a)_{a \geqslant 0}\) indexed by the addresses of the dictionary of \(R^+\). Address \(a\) carries a support \(S_a\), a nonempty inventory \(I_a \subseteq S_a\) and an activity \(K_a\); its tag is \(\text{tag} (a) = (S_a, I_a)\). The exponential entry of \(Q\) is

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{E} (Q) (U) = Q.x_0 + \ell (U, Q.x) + \sum_j \chi_{X_j} f_j (U),\)

where \(Q.x_0\) is the unit coordinate, \(\ell (U, \cdot)\) is the masked placement of \(\text{core} \hspace{0.17em} Q.x\) by the local section at the origin, and \(\chi_{X_j} f_j\) is the masked regular term. The forced profile \(\mathcal{P} (Q)\) is the function on tags

\(\displaystyle \mathcal{P} (Q) (X, I) = \sum_{a : \text{tag} (a) = (X, I)} k_a .\)

A finite set \(b\) of addresses is admissible at \(U\) if the supports \(S_c\), \(c \in b\), are pairwise disjoint and the inventories \(I_c\), \(c \in b\), together cover exactly the flags of \(U\). This is the admissibility condition of the gas sum of the activity expansion, stated in Subsection 7.9 (subsection “Putting the response into the existing gas”); the additional eligibility predicates of the addresses do not enter it. Two consequences are used below. First, admissibility of \(b\) at \(U\) depends on the addresses of \(b\) only through their tags. Second, two distinct addresses of one admissible set have disjoint supports. The dressed field at an address \(a\) is

\(\displaystyle \text{Dr}_a (U) = \sum_{b \ni a \text{admissible at } U} \prod_{c \in b \setminus \{a\}} K_c (U) .\)

Absolute bound on the dressed fields. The stored representation \(R^+\) is lawful. Indeed the selected history satisfies all its local activity bounds at every level (Subsection 12.13). These are the retained-history conditions spelled out in the proof of Proposition 12.6. In particular the forced activities of \(R^+\) have the forced stage bound at the forced allowance \(w^{\text{f}}_{i + 1}\) of level \(i + 1\). That bound is the rooted support sum (13.8) of Subsection 13.7, at the support exponent \(\alpha_{i + 1}\): there are majorants \(\bar{K}_c \geqslant 0\) with \(|K_c | \leqslant \bar{K}_c\) and

\(\displaystyle \sum_{c : x \in S_c} \text{mass} (S_c, \bar{K}_c) \leqslant w^{\text{f}}_{i + 1} \qquad \text{for every site $x$ of the fine torus $\Lambda_{i + 1}$,}\)

where \(\text{mass}\) is the fine mass recalled below. Since \(\text{mass} (S, e) \geqslant e\) for \(e \geqslant 0\) (see the remainder convention below) and every support is nonempty, summing over \(x \in \Lambda_{i + 1}\) gives \(\sum_c \bar{K}_c \leqslant | \Lambda_{i + 1} | \hspace{0.17em} w^{\text{f}}_{i + 1}\). Fix \(a\). The map \(b \mapsto b \setminus \{a\}\) is injective on the finite sets containing \(a\). Hence, for every \(U\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{b \ni a \text{admissible at } U} \prod_{c \in b \setminus \{a\}} |K_c (U) | \leqslant \sum_{T \text{finite}} \prod_{c \in T} \bar{K}_c = \sup_{F \text{finite}} \prod_{c \in F} (1 + \bar{K}_c) \leqslant \exp \left( | \Lambda_{i + 1} | \hspace{0.17em} w^{\text{f}}_{i + 1} \right) = : D_{i + 1} .\) (13.34)

The equality holds because every finite \(T\) lies in some finite \(F\), and \(\prod_{c \in F} (1 + \bar{K}_c)\) is the sum of \(\prod_{c \in T} \bar{K}_c\) over the subsets \(T \subseteq F\); the last inequality is \(1 + x \leqslant e^x\). So each dressed series converges absolutely, and \(| \text{Dr}_a (U) | \leqslant D_{i + 1}\) uniformly in \(a\) and \(U\).

The density of \(Q\) is

\(\displaystyle \text{dens} (Q) (U) = R^+ . \text{eval} (U) \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{E} (Q) (U) + e^{R^+ . \text{exponent} (U)} \sum_a k_a (U) \text{Dr}_a (U),\)

in which the forced series has been regrouped by its address. The regrouping is justified by (13.34) whenever the forced array is absolutely summable. For the fields \(Q\) with a remainder bound considered below this holds, since \(\sum_a |k_a (U) | \leqslant \sum_a e'_a < \infty\) by step (v) of the proof of Proposition 13.36.

The terminal format at the origin requires several things of \(Q\). Each regular atom is regular on its support, its support contains the origin, it vanishes at the identity configuration, and it passes the holonomy test. Each forced coefficient is gauge invariant, measurable and local to its support. Where \(k_a\) is nonzero, address \(a\) is eligible, that is \(S_a \cap \text{flags} (U) = I_a\); and \(k_a \ne 0\) forces the origin to lie in \(S_a\). A remainder bound \(q\) at weight \(\kappa\) means that there are \(a_{\text{r}}, a_{\text{f}} \geqslant 0\) with \(a_{\text{r}} + a_{\text{f}} \leqslant q\), together with ordered-derivative majorants \(\|f_j \|_{0, X_j} \leqslant e_j\) in the spatially weighted norm of Subsection 7.9, and \(e'_a \geqslant 0\) with \(|k_a | \leqslant e'_a\), such that

\(\displaystyle \sum_j \text{mass} (X_j, e_j) \leqslant a_{\text{r}}, \qquad \sum_a \text{mass} (S_a, e'_a) \leqslant \kappa \hspace{0.17em} a_{\text{f}} .\)

Here \(\text{mass} (X, e) = e^{\alpha_{i + 1} \text{coverNumber} (B_{i + 1}, X)} e\) is the fine mass. It is linear in \(e\) for a fixed support. The support exponent \(\alpha_{i + 1}\) of a budget is positive (Subsection 7.9) and the cover number is nonnegative, so \(\text{mass} (X, e) \geqslant e\) for every \(e \geqslant 0\). This is the normalized remainder convention of Subsection 7.9.

A transfer record across \(D\) on an insertion support \(W\) for input data \((\alpha, \rho, \delta, f)\) is defined as follows. Here \(\alpha\) is an exponential entry, \(\rho\) a forced profile, \(\delta\) a unit value and \(f\) an upper density. The record specifies a leading coefficient \(\ell_0 \in F\) and bounds \((c, r, u)\). It contains a lower coefficient field \(P\) on \(R^-\) with the format at \(R^-\), the exact push identity \(\text{dens} (P) = L_i^d \textsf{P}_i f\), the leading coefficient error bound \(C_{\text{orb}} \|P.x - \ell_0 \|_1 \leqslant c\), and a turn-weighted remainder bound at most \(r\). It also has a response decomposition on \(W\) across \(D\) relating \((\alpha, \rho, \delta)\) to the lower forced profile \(\mathcal{P} (P)\), with input bound \(u\) and output bound \(r\). This is the notion of Subsection 10.10: there the three elementary insertions are described by exactly these items, with bounds written as \(C_{\text{orb}} \|Q.x - \cdots \|_1 \leqslant \cdot\), \(r = \cdot\) and “input allowance”. It is also the description before Lemma 13.18, with \(\ell_0 = 0\).

Proposition 13.36. (Transfer across a fixed interface with zero coefficient) Keep the selected history, its fixed interfaces and the purchase of Subsection 13.4. Fix \(d, N, p\) and \(t_d \leqslant i < N\), with the notation above. Assume the following three inputs on the fixed interface \(D\).

    (K)

    Insertion estimates. The upper representation \(R^+\) has a Keeper, an injective assignment \((X, I) \mapsto \textsf{k} (X, I)\) of an address with tag \((X, I)\) to every pair \(\varnothing \ne I \subseteq X\), eligible whenever \(X \cap \text{flags} (U) = I\). Its keys lie in \(W_i\). On the insertion support \(W_i\) the regular and forced estimates of Subsection 10.10 hold on \(D\) with \(\eta = \varrho_i\), in the following form, with lead \(0\).

    • Let \((X, f)\) be a regular atom with \(0 \in X\) that passes the format tests, and let \(e\) be an ordered-derivative majorant of \(f\), \(\|f\|_{0, X} \leqslant e\) in the spatially weighted norm of the remainder convention above. Put \(m = \text{mass} (X, e)\). Then the input data

      \(\displaystyle (\chi_X f, \hspace{0.17em} 0, \hspace{0.17em} 0, \hspace{0.17em} R^+ . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} \chi_X f)\)

      have a transfer record with bounds \((\varrho_i m, \varrho_i m, 2 m)\).

    • Let \(a\) be an address whose key lies in \(W_i\), and let \(f \ne 0\) be a forced insertion at \(a\) that fits its slot, with \(|f| \leqslant e\). Put \(m = \text{mass} (S_a, e)\). Then the input data

      \(\displaystyle (0, \hspace{0.17em} f\boldsymbol{1}_{\text{tag} (a)}, \hspace{0.17em} 0, \hspace{0.17em} e^{R^+ . \text{exponent}} f \text{Dr}_a)\)

      have a transfer record with bounds \((\varrho_i m / \kappa, \varrho_i m / \kappa, 2 m)\).

    (S)

    Soundness. The interface \(D\) is sound for the transition operator of the step.

    (M)

    Merger. transfer records across \(D\) on one insertion support are monotone in their three bounds. A countable family of them merges into a transfer record whose input data, lead and bounds are the sums, provided the following hold: the entries and the profiles converge pointwise; the unit values, the three bounds and the norms of the leads are summable; and the densities are measurable and dominated. This is the merger of Lemma 13.33, including the response decomposition witnesses. Finally, zero input data have a transfer record with lead \(0\) and any nonnegative bounds.

Then (R7) holds at \(D\) with \(\eta_i = \varrho_i\):

\(\displaystyle (1 + 2 C_i) \varrho_i \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (L_i), \qquad D \text{ is sound}, \qquad \psi_i \geqslant 0, \qquad \varrho_i > 0.\)

Moreover, let \(Q\) be any upper coefficient field on \(R^+\) with \(Q.x = 0\), the terminal format at the origin and remainder bound \(q\) at weight \(\kappa\). Then its input data \((\mathcal{E}(Q), \mathcal{P}(Q), Q.x_0, \text{dens} (Q))\) have a Transfer record across \(D\) on \(W_i\) with lead \(0\) and bounds \((\varrho_i q, \varrho_i q, 2 q)\). The insertion support \(W_i\) is finite and does not depend on \(Q\).

Proof. Tuning. Subsection 13.6 chooses, for every stage \(j\), a threshold \(v_j\) with \((1 + 2 c_j) \text{rate} (L) \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (L)\) for all \(L \geqslant v_j\); here \(c_j\) is the column count of the profile at stage \(j\). Indeed the ratio of the two sides is \(\text{rate} (L) / \text{stepSmall} (L) = L^{- 2 / 5 + 3 / 8} = L^{- 1 / 40}\). So the inequality is equivalent to \(L^{1 / 40} \geqslant 1 + 2 c_j\), and \(v_j = \lceil (1 + 2 c_j)^{40} \rceil\) suffices. The stage threshold \(n_k\) there is the maximum of these \(v_j\) over \(j \leqslant k\), together with the credit thresholds. By Subsection 13.4, the purchase includes \(n_k\) among the block floors of stage \(k\). Every block length chosen at stage \(k\) is at least that floor. Take \(k = J (i + 1)\). The block side of the present step is chosen at that stage, so \(L_i \geqslant n_{J (i + 1)}\). Since \(J (i) \leqslant J (i + 1)\), the threshold \(v_{J (i)}\) is one of the terms of \(n_{J (i + 1)}\). Hence \(L_i \geqslant v_{J (i)}\). The column count at level \(i\) is that of the profile at stage \(J (i)\), so \(C_i = c_{J (i)}\). Therefore \((1 + 2 C_i) \varrho_i \leqslant \text{stepSmall} (L_i)\). This is the grade-zero clause of the purchase: the inequality involves no section, and the threshold \(v_j\) is the same for every grade.

Signs and soundness. We have \(\varrho_i = L_i^{- 2 / 5} > 0\). Also \(\psi_i = 2 e^{\alpha_{i + 1}} \tau_{i + 1} \geqslant 0\), since \(\tau_{i + 1} \geqslant 0\). Soundness is (S).

Repacking the forced array. Let \(Q\) be as in the statement. Define \(Q'\) to have the same coordinate vector and regular atoms as \(Q\). Its forced array is

\(\displaystyle k'_{\textsf{k} (X, I)} =\mathcal{P} (Q) (X, I), \qquad k'_a = 0 \text{ if $a$ is not a reserved address} .\)

The tag of every address has a nonempty inventory contained in its support, so it lies in the domain of the Keeper. We check five properties of \(Q'\).

(i) \(\mathcal{E} (Q') =\mathcal{E} (Q)\), because the exponential entry does not involve the forced array.

(ii) \(\mathcal{P} (Q') =\mathcal{P} (Q)\). Fix a tag \((X, I)\). The only address with this tag at which \(k'\) can be nonzero is \(\textsf{k} (X, I)\), since \(\textsf{k}\) is injective and \(\text{tag} (\textsf{k} (X, I)) = (X, I)\). Hence \(\mathcal{P} (Q') (X, I) = k'_{\textsf{k} (X, I)} =\mathcal{P} (Q) (X, I)\).

(iii) \(\text{dens} (Q') = \text{dens} (Q)\). The dressed field depends on the address only through its tag. To see this, let \(\text{tag} (a) = \text{tag} (a')\) with \(a \ne a'\), and let \(\sigma\) be the transposition of \(a\) and \(a'\). Then \(\sigma\) preserves tags, so it maps admissible sets to admissible sets. No admissible set contains both \(a\) and \(a'\), because their supports coincide and are nonempty, while distinct members of an admissible set have disjoint supports. Thus \(b \mapsto \sigma (b)\) is a bijection from the admissible sets containing \(a\) to those containing \(a'\), and \(\sigma (b) \setminus \{a' \} = b \setminus \{a\}\). Comparing the two series term by term gives \(\text{Dr}_a = \text{Dr}_{a'}\); both series converge absolutely by (13.34). Write \(\text{Dr}_{(X, I)}\) for the common value. The forced series converges absolutely, since \(|k_a (U) \text{Dr}_a (U) | \leqslant D_{i + 1} e'_a\) and \(\sum_a e'_a < \infty\) by (v). Grouping it by tags therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_a k_a (U) \text{Dr}_a (U) = \sum_{(X, I)} \mathcal{P} (Q) (X, I) (U) \text{Dr}_{(X, I)} (U),\)

and the same identity holds for \(Q'\). By (i) and (ii) the two densities coincide.

(iv) \(Q'\) has the terminal format. The regular clauses are unchanged. Fix a tag \((X, I)\) and put \(g = k'_{\textsf{k} (X, I)} = \sum_{\text{tag} (a) = (X, I)} k_a\). This sum converges absolutely, by the majorants \(e'_a\) and the summability below. A countable absolutely convergent sum of gauge-invariant, measurable functions local to \(X\) has the same three properties. If \(g (U) \ne 0\), then some \(k_a (U) \ne 0\) with \(\text{tag} (a) = (X, I)\). Eligibility of \(a\) at \(U\) gives \(X \cap \text{flags} (U) = I\), so the reserved-slot assignment address \(\textsf{k} (X, I)\) is eligible at \(U\). Similarly \(g \ne 0\) forces some \(k_a \ne 0\), hence \(0 \in S_a = X\).

(v) \(Q'\) has remainder bound \(q\) at weight \(\kappa\), with the same \(a_{\text{r}}, a_{\text{f}}\). The regular part is unchanged. For the forced part, put \(e''_{\textsf{k} (X, I)} = \sum_{\text{tag} (a) = (X, I)} e'_a\) and \(e'' = 0\) elsewhere. Then \(|k' | \leqslant e''\) by the triangle inequality. Masses on the common support \(X\) add, so \(\sum_a \text{mass} (S_a, e''_a) = \sum_a \text{mass} (S_a, e'_a) \leqslant \kappa a_{\text{f}}\). Since \(\alpha_{i + 1} > 0\) and the cover number is nonnegative, \(\text{mass} (S_a, e'_a) \geqslant e'_a \geqslant 0\), so \(\sum_a e'_a \leqslant \kappa a_{\text{f}} < \infty\). This is the summability used in (iii) and (iv).

Consequently \(Q\) and \(Q'\) have the same input data \((\mathcal{E}, \mathcal{P}, Q.x_0, \text{dens})\), and it suffices to produce the transfer record for \(Q'\).

Decomposition. Since \(Q' .x = 0\), also \(Q' .x_0 = 0\), and the placement term \(\ell (U, 0)\) vanishes. Thus

\(\displaystyle \begin{aligned} \mathcal{E} (Q') & = \sum_j \chi_{X_j} f_j,\\ \mathcal{P} (Q') & = \sum_a k'_a \boldsymbol{1}_{\text{tag} (a)},\\ \text{dens} (Q') & = \sum_j R^+ . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} \chi_{X_j} f_j + \sum_a e^{R^+ . \text{exponent}} k'_a \text{Dr}_a . \end{aligned}\)

All three series converge absolutely and pointwise. For the density the regular terms are dominated by the coefficient envelope of the lawful representation and the remainder bound. The forced terms are dominated by \(e^{R^+ . \text{exponent} (U)} D_{i + 1} e''_a\), by (13.34), and \(\sum_a e''_a = \sum_a e'_a < \infty\) by (v). Tonelli's theorem applied to these nonnegative majorants gives a dominated, measurable expansion, as in Subsection 4.13. Put \(m_j = \text{mass} (X_j, e_j)\) and \(m'_a = \text{mass} (S_a, e''_a)\). We attach a transfer record to each term.

  • Regular atom \(j\). Its input data are \((\chi_{X_j} f_j, 0, 0, R^+ . \text{eval} \hspace{0.17em} \chi_{X_j} f_j)\). The atom has \(0 \in X_j\), passes the format tests and has majorant \(e_j\). By (K) it has a transfer record with bounds \((\varrho_i m_j, \varrho_i m_j, 2 m_j)\).

  • Forced address \(a\) with \(k'_a \ne 0\). Then \(a = \textsf{k} (X, I)\) is a Keeper address, so its key lies in \(W_i\). Its coefficient fits the slot by (iv), and \(|k'_a | \leqslant e''_a\). By (K) it has a transfer record with bounds \((\varrho_i m'_a / \kappa, \varrho_i m'_a / \kappa, 2 m'_a)\). Since \(\kappa \leqslant 1\), monotonicity (M) gives one with bounds

    \(\displaystyle (\varrho_i m'_a / \kappa, \varrho_i m'_a / \kappa, 2 m'_a / \kappa) .\)

  • Forced address \(a\) with \(k'_a = 0\). Its input data are zero, and by (M) they have a transfer record with bounds

    \(\displaystyle (\varrho_i m'_a / \kappa, \varrho_i m'_a / \kappa, 2 m'_a / \kappa) .\)

Summation. All leads are \(0\), and so are all unit values. The entries, profiles and densities of the terms sum to those of \(Q'\), by the decomposition. The bound series are summable:

\(\displaystyle \sum_j \varrho_i m_j + \sum_a \frac{\varrho_i m'_a}{\kappa} \leqslant \varrho_i (a_{\text{r}} + a_{\text{f}}) \leqslant \varrho_i q, \qquad \sum_j 2 m_j + \sum_a \frac{2 m'_a}{\kappa} \leqslant 2 q.\)

Here we used \(\sum_j m_j \leqslant a_{\text{r}}\) and \(\sum_a m'_a \leqslant \kappa a_{\text{f}}\) from (v). By (M) the terms merge into one transfer record on \(W_i\) for the input data of \(Q'\), with lead \(0\). Its bounds are the three sums, and by monotonicity they may be replaced by \((\varrho_i q, \varrho_i q, 2 q)\). By the repacking step these are also the input data of \(Q\).

The insertion support \(W_i\) was chosen before \(Q\), from the finite fine torus and its reserved-slot assignment. It is finite because the torus has finitely many supports and tags.\(\Box\)

What the proposition supplies.
Of (R7), the proposition proves the tuning inequality, the signs, and the reduction of the transfer record for an arbitrary zero-coordinate coefficient to single insertions. The tuning inequality uses only the grade-zero threshold of the purchase. That threshold pays tuning; the Transfer record itself is paid at the grade \(d\) of the frame, through (K). The three inputs are not proved here. The admissibility facts and the absolute bound (13.34) used in the repacking are not further inputs: they follow from the definition of the gas sum in Subsection 7.9 and from the lawfulness of the stored representations, as shown before the proposition.

  • The regular and forced estimates (K) are those of Subsection 10.10. They hold relative to the retained funded interpolation and its slot realization, with \(\eta = L^{- 2 / 5}\) there equal to \(\varrho_i\). They are attached to the fixed interface through the same history identifications. Their derivation uses the retained path construction of Subsection 11.21, under its stated hypotheses, and the common-history attachment used in Proposition 13.34.

  • Soundness (S) of the fixed interface is asserted in Subsection 10.10 for the retained interpolating point.

  • The merger (M) is Lemma 13.33, including the response decompositiones.

The Keeper of \(R^+\) in (K) is part of the retained slot realization. At the terminal level it is the reserved-slot assignment of Proposition 13.15.

13.11Bounded local approximations on the original phase grid

The comparison argument requires bounded real observables which approximate whole replicated fields under the original finite-volume law. A bound on a single expectation would not suffice. We construct these observables using one insertion at the terminal scale and a second insertion at an intermediate scale. Their difference is controlled by the marked estimate, before taking any limit or choosing a phase.

Fix the selected family \(C\) and the extraction order already used for its centered estimates. Write \(a_i\) for the physical mesh, \(\sigma_i\) for the running scale, \(c_i\) for the column count, and \(\ell_i\) for the accumulated column logarithm. Thus \(a_i \to 0\) whereas \(\sigma_i \to + \infty\). At grade \(d\) the finite coefficient space has coordinate norm \(\| \cdot \|_1\), coordinate basis \((e_s)\), and reference depth \(r_d\). The finite-volume observable associated with \(L\) is denoted by \(O_{N, p} (L)\), with its base point at the origin. All expectations below use the original Wilson probability measure \(\mu_{N, p}\).

We use the paid selected-history data of Sections 13.9 and 13.10: the coefficient transport on this history, its adjacent density and forced-profile identities, and its height, transition, occupancy and support estimates. Precisely, the input is the record (R1)–(R7) of Subsection 13.9, for every grade, together with the opening schedule of Subsection 4.4. In particular the positive heights \(\zeta_{d, i}\) and weights \(\phi_i\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle 0 < \phi_i \leqslant 1, \qquad \phi_i L_i^{d + 1} \zeta_{d, i} \leqslant \zeta_{d, i + 1}, \qquad L_i = a_i / a_{i + 1} .\)

These data are used with the same local sections and transition kernels at every occurrence below. The availability of this entire paid history is an input here; it is not inferred merely from the existence of an unmarked Wilson measure.

13.11.1Transporting a bounded local observable

For \(k \leqslant N\), let \(\textsf{B}_{k, N}\) be the iterated averaging operator which carries a scale-\(k\) observable to a function of the scale-\(N\) configuration. At the step from \(i\) to \(i + 1\) it uses the transition kernel of the selected history, with that history's coupling at \(i + 1\) and its fixed terminal indices \((N, p)\). Its adjoint \(\textsf{P}_{k, N}\) pushes densities from \(N\) to \(k\), relative to product Haar measure. Normalization of each transition kernel, followed by induction, gives

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rl} \| \textsf{B}_{k, N} F\|_{\infty} & \leqslant \|F\|_{\infty},\\ \textsf{B}_{k, N} \bar{F} & = \overline{\textsf{B}_{k, N} F},\\ \int (\textsf{P}_{k, N} g) F \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_k & = \int g \hspace{0.17em} \textsf{B}_{k, N} F \hspace{0.17em} d \nu_N . \end{array}\)

Here \(\nu_i\) is product Haar measure at scale \(i\), \(F\) is bounded measurable, and \(g\) is integrable. The last identity follows first at one step by integration against the kernel, and then by iteration. The same argument with \(F = 1\) proves preservation of the density integral.

The local geometry supplies a useful stronger identity. Fix a coarse depth \(J \leqslant k\) and a window radius \(R\). Choose \(k\) large enough that the propagated radius-\(R\) windows, based at distinct \(J\)-cell centers, remain inside their respective chambers at every intervening depth. This is the room condition. To verify it, let \(h_i\) be the calendar index at depth \(i\), so \(h_{i + 1} \leqslant h_i + 1\), and recall \(L_i \geqslant 3\). Starting with \(R_k = R\), the kernel read set has propagated radius

\(\displaystyle R_{i + 1} = L_i R_i + 10 (h_{i + 1} + 3) L_i .\)

Put \(T_i = 30 a_i (h_i + 5)\). Since \(a_{i + 1} = a_i / L_i\), \(T_{i + 1} \leqslant (2 / 3) T_i\) and \(a_{i + 1} R_{i + 1} - a_i R_i \leqslant T_i / 3\). Therefore

\(\displaystyle a_i R_i + T_i \leqslant a_k R + T_k \qquad (i \geqslant k) .\)

Both \(a_k R\) and \(T_k\) tend to zero. Choose \(k\) so that \(2 (a_k R + T_k) < a_J\). Then \(2 a_i R_i < a_J\) at every later depth, which places the window inside the centered \(J\)-chamber, using the odd-subdivision chamber convention. This proves the required room condition uniformly in terminal depth, period and number of cells. If \(F_i\) depends only on the window at cell \(i\), then

\(\displaystyle \textsf{B}_{k, N} \left( \prod_{i \in A} F_i \right) = \prod_{i \in A} \textsf{B}_{k, N} F_i\) (13.35)

for every finite set \(A\) of distinct cells. Indeed, at one transition the kernel variables read by different chambers are disjoint. Integrating their product therefore factors. The propagated-window condition permits this argument at every subsequent transition. Induction over the depth proves the formula. Covariance of the kernels under signed lattice motions, and their compatibility with conjugation, also show that transport commutes with the centered copy maps. These are identities of the kernels; no factorization of the Wilson probability measure is asserted.

At scale \(k\), define \(F_{k, L}\) to be the physically rescaled exponential entry of the primary coefficient \(L\). More explicitly it is

\(\displaystyle F_{k, L} (U) = a_k^{- d} L_0 +\boldsymbol{1}_{\text{healthy} (k)} (U) \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{Q}_{k, L} (U),\)

where \(\mathcal{Q}_{k, L}\) is the continuous gauge-invariant candidate of the local section including its physical factor \(a_k^{- d}\), and the indicator tests the section's footprint. The candidate estimate gives a bound on \(|\mathcal{Q}_{k, L} |\) independent of \(N \geqslant k\) and \(p\). The indicator has values zero and one; thus \(F_{k, L}\) is bounded and measurable uniformly in these indices. Its complete read set, including the mask's halo, lies in the window of radius \(R_d + 4\), where \(R_d\) is the stencil radius. Consequently there is \(A_k \geqslant 0\) such that

\(\displaystyle \sup |F_{k, e_s} | \leqslant A_k \qquad \text{for every basis index } s,\)

uniformly in \(N, p\). For example one may sum the finitely many individual bounds. Continuity of the masked field is not needed.

13.11.2The marked discrepancy estimate

Fix an opened pair of depths \(J \leqslant m\), with \(m \geqslant r_d\), and choose \(k \geqslant m\) satisfying the room condition above. Put

\(\displaystyle E_k = 4 (1 + c_k), \quad G (k, i) = \exp ((\sigma_k - \sigma_i) / 8), \quad S_{J, m} = \exp ((a_J / a_m)^4 \mathcal{T}_m + 5),\)

where \(\mathcal{T}_m\) is the vacuum treasury. Choose \(w > 0\) so that

\(\displaystyle wa_m^{- d} \frac{E_k}{G (k, m)} \leqslant \frac{\zeta_{d, m}}{2} .\) (13.36)

By Lemma 4.9, applied to \(J\) opened at \(m\), the opening supplies migrating centers for the full \(J\)-cell census, separated at every depth between \(m\) and \(N\). Their occupancy is one and therefore fits the nondecreasing paid occupancy schedule, whose entries are at least one.

Lemma 13.37. For every basis vector \(e_s\) and every \(N > k\), there is a coefficient \(b_s\) in the same history's coefficient transport such that

\(\displaystyle \|e_s - I_k b_s \|_1 \leqslant \tfrac{1}{4}, \qquad \rho_{J ; N, p} (\textsf{B}_{k, N} F_{k, e_s} - O_{N, p} (T_N b_s)) \leqslant S_{J, m} / w.\)

Here \(I_k\) is the coefficient identification at \(k\), \(T_N\) is the terminal identification, and \(\rho_{J ; N, p}\) is the centered full-family root test for \(\mu_{N, p}\).

Proof. Apply the backward coefficient construction of Lemma 13.18 to the primary coefficient with lead \(e_s\) at depth \(k\): in the notation of that lemma the top level is \(k\), the bottom level is the reference depth \(l = r_d \leqslant m\), the coefficient is the primary of \(e_s\), and \(w = 1\). This is legitimate because the initial remainder and forced profile of a primary vanish, its format is the primary format, and its coefficient norm is one. The transfer sequence is the one of the record (R6) at \((N, p)\), and the interfaces are those of the record. Recenter the resulting backward coefficients by the negative of their bottom primary \(b_s\), the primary of the state at \(r_d\). Writing the resulting coefficient family as \(Z_i\) and its remainder bound as \(g_i\), the third item of the lemma gives, for \(r_d \leqslant i \leqslant k\) and in particular for \(m \leqslant i \leqslant k\),

\(\displaystyle g_i + \|Z_i .x\|_1 \leqslant E_k / G (k, i), \qquad Z_k .x = e_s - I_k b_s .\)

The last item of the lemma gives the terminal cone estimate \(32 (1 + c_k)^2 \|Z_k .x\|_1 \leqslant g_k \leqslant 2 (1 + c_k)\). Since \(c_k \geqslant 1\), it implies the stated quarter bound.

Let \(\tau_i = w \prod_{j = m}^{i - 1} \phi_j\). The physical rescaling changes \(a_i^{- d}\) by \(L_i^d\) at the next step, and \(G (k, i)^{- 1}\) changes by at most \(L_i\). Thus the height recurrence and (13.36) imply

\(\displaystyle \tau_i a_i^{- d} E_k / G (k, i) \leqslant \zeta_{d, i} / 2 \qquad (m \leqslant i \leqslant k) .\)

Moreover \(\|I_k b_s \|_1 \leqslant 1 + \|Z_k .x\|_1 \leqslant 2 E_k\). It follows that the negative played coefficient at \(k\) costs at most \(\zeta_{d, k}\) with weight \(\tau_k\). This is the input needed for the selected insertion service above \(k\).

The matching at \(k\) is exact, not just a norm comparison. Linearity of the played exponential entry and of its forced-profile map gives

\(\displaystyle Z_k^{\text{exp}} = \text{primary} (e_s)^{\text{exp}} + \text{played} (- b_s)^{\text{exp}}, \qquad Z_k^{\text{forced}} = \text{played} (- b_s)^{\text{forced}} .\)

The second identity uses the zero forced profile of the primary. Hence we can first insert the negative terminal observable \(- O_{N, p} (T_N b_s)\), transport its marked density down to \(k\), and then multiply by the positive local factors \(F_{k, e_s}\). The selected service of Subsection 13.10 applies above \(k\) with the just verified played-coefficient bound. Adding the local factors changes its exponential entries to those of \(Z_k\) and leaves its forced profiles unchanged.

Below \(k\) the backward coefficients supply the adjacent density identities and finite-support forced-profile comparisons. We verify both transition bounds. Put \(D_i = \text{orbitCost}_d (\psi_i + 2 c_{i + 1})\), where \(\psi_i\) is the adjacent comparison bound. The exact comparison bounds its next-level weighted remainder by \(D_i (g_{i + 1} +\|Z_{i + 1} .x\|_1)\); its lower-level remainder is \(g_i\). The paid room and coin inequalities are \(2 D_i \zeta_{d, i + 1} \leqslant 1\) and \(c_i \zeta_{d, i} \leqslant 1\). The marched height estimate, with the harmless factor \(1 / 2\) discarded, therefore gives

\(\displaystyle 2 \tau_{i + 1} a_{i + 1}^{- d} D_i (g_{i + 1} +\|Z_{i + 1} .x\|_1) \leqslant 2 D_i \zeta_{d, i + 1} \leqslant 1,\)

and, since \(\tau_{i + 1} \leqslant \tau_i\) and \(c_i \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \tau_{i + 1} a_i^{- d} g_i \leqslant \tau_i a_i^{- d} g_i \leqslant \zeta_{d, i} \leqslant 1.\)

The finite-support comparison is transported through the actual sound gauge interface and physically rescaled by the exact adjacent scale identity. Its profiles consequently have precisely these two bounds. The selected transition estimate now descends by induction from \(k\) to \(m\). At each step the centers migrate along the same embeddings, the occupancy and separation estimates persist, and the pushed singleton coefficients equal the prescribed local densities. Thus the marked estimate (4.10) of Subsection 4.2 applies at \(m\). The full polynomial representation through this two-stage descent, including the intermediate positive insertion and its mixed modes, is constructed in Lemma 16.30 below. The two bounds verified above are its adjacent numerical inputs. Its local entry bound is at most \(2\), its forced bound at most \(1\), and its action allowance is \(1\), giving total bound \(2 + 1 + 2 = 5\) per cell. These are the same bounds derived in the finite-census argument of Proposition 13.19; the new feature here is the intermediate positive insertion and the exact matching just proved.

For clarity we identify the coefficient being bounded. Let \(\mathcal{I}\) be the full set of \(J\)-cells and \(M = |\mathcal{I}| > 0\). Put \(U = O_{N, p} (T_N b_s)\) and \(F = F_{k, e_s}\). For a subset \(A \subseteq \mathcal{I}\), the coefficient after the insertion at \(k\) is

\(\displaystyle \sum_{B \subseteq A} \textsf{P}_{k, N} \left( e^{- S_N} \prod_{i \in A \setminus B} (-\mathcal{C}_i^N U) \right) \prod_{i \in B} \mathcal{C}_i^k F.\)

Here \(e^{- S_N}\) denotes the unnormalized Wilson density and \(\mathcal{C}_i^j\) the centered copy at depth \(j\), including its conjugation when prescribed by the tiling. Duality, (13.35), and commutation with copies show that its integral is

\(\displaystyle Z_{N, p} \hspace{0.17em} \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_{i \in A} \mathcal{C}_i^N (\textsf{B}_{k, N} F - U) .\)

This follows by expanding the product over all subsets \(B\); the negative old factors are retained together inside their pushed density. All integrals exist because the terminal fields are continuous on compact configuration spaces and \(F\) is bounded measurable. Further density transport to \(m\) preserves this integral, coefficient by coefficient.

For \(A =\mathcal{I}\), denote the last expectation by \(\Omega\). Applying the marked estimate and dividing by the positive original partition function \(Z_{N, p}\) gives

\(\displaystyle | \Omega | w^M \leqslant \exp (| \text{Site} (m, p) |\mathcal{T}_m + 5 M) .\)

The aligned four-dimensional count is \(| \text{Site} (m, p) | = M (a_J / a_m)^4\). Taking the \(M\)th root proves \(| \Omega |^{1 / M} \leqslant S_{J, m} / w\), which is precisely the asserted root test. No change of law or extraction order has entered the calculation.\(\Box\)

13.11.3Arbitrary real labels and a common coarse depth

For a label \(L\) of grade \(d\), put \(K_k (L) = 2 e^{\ell_k} \|L\|_1\). The vectors \(I_k b_s\) in Lemma 13.37 form a basis by Lemma 4.33. If \(t = T_N^{- 1} L\), solve \(\sum_s v_s I_k b_s = I_k t\). Then

\(\displaystyle L = \sum_s v_s T_N b_s, \qquad \sum_s |v_s | \leqslant 2 \|I_k t\|_1 \leqslant K_k (L) .\)

The last inequality is the backward coefficient budget of the same selected history. Define \(B^{\mathbb{C}}_{N, p} = \sum_s v_s \textsf{B}_{k, N} F_{k, e_s}\). Its supremum norm is at most \(K_k (L) A_k\). The room condition puts every summand in the origin chamber of the \(J\)-tiling; eventually the physical observables of this fixed grade also lie there. The rooted triangle inequality on chamber-interior fields therefore gives

\(\displaystyle \rho_{J ; N, p} (B^{\mathbb{C}}_{N, p} - O_{N, p} (L)) \leqslant K_k (L) S_{J, m} / w.\)

The coefficients \(v_s\) may depend on \(N, p\); their common bound makes the supremum estimate uniform in these indices.

Choose

\(\displaystyle w_k = \frac{\zeta_{d, m} G (k, m)}{2 a_m^{- d} E_k} .\)

Then (13.36) is an equality. The column estimates \(\ell_k = o (\sigma_k)\) and \(\log (1 + c_k) = o (\sigma_k)\) imply, by Lemma 4.38,

\(\displaystyle K_k (L) \hspace{0.17em} S_{J, m} / w_k \longrightarrow 0 \qquad (k \to \infty) .\)

In this limit \(J, m, d, L\) are fixed. Thus we can choose \(k\) to meet both the room condition and any prescribed error \(\varepsilon > 0\), and subsequently take \(N > k\) on the original late set.

If \(L\) is star-real, the exact reality identity makes \(O_{N, p} (L)\) real. Take \(B_{N, p} = \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} B^{\mathbb{C}}_{N, p}\). It has the same supremum bound and chamber support. Since the root test is invariant under conjugation and subadditive on these fields,

\(\displaystyle \rho_{J ; N, p} (B_{N, p} - O_{N, p} (L)) \leqslant \tfrac{1}{2} \rho_{J ; N, p} (B^{\mathbb{C}}_{N, p} - O_{N, p} (L)) + \tfrac{1}{2} \rho_{J ; N, p} (\overline{B^{\mathbb{C}}_{N, p} - O_{N, p} (L)}) \leqslant \varepsilon .\)

Here real fields are identified with their complex-valued inclusions.

For a finite grade ceiling \(D\) and a requested coarse depth \(J_0\), choose

\(\displaystyle J = \max \{J_0, r_0, \ldots, r_D \} .\)

Use the opening schedule once to select \(m \geqslant J\) with the required opening. The preceding construction then applies to every star-real label of grade at most \(D\) and each accuracy \(\varepsilon_q = (q + 1)^{- 1}\). Its approximation depth and supremum bound may depend on the label and \(q\), but its coarse depth \(J\) and extraction order do not. This is the coherent family of origin approximations needed for the comparison grid.

13.11.4From origin approximations to whole grid words

The same origin approximations must be used for all translated and reflected copies. For a real label \(v\) and a copy of spin frame \(s\), first apply the inverse spin frame to \(v\), then copy its origin approximation. The exact covariance identity turns the corresponding copied physical field into the field labelled by \(v\) at the desired site. A conjugated copy of a real approximant has the same value as its ordinary real pullback. All transformed labels remain real. Since the spin-frame set is finite, summing their supremum bounds gives a bound \(M_{v, q}\) valid for every cell at accuracy \(q\).

There is also an accuracy-independent bound \(A_v\) on the root tests of the physical origin fields in these finitely many frames. This is the one place where the centered one-field estimate enters: it is the uniform centered power bound of Theorem 4.7, used here as an input on the same family and extraction order, together with the reflection-positive transfer structure of the finite-volume laws. To obtain \(A_v\), choose a finer depth \(j \geqslant J\) with \(9 a_j \leqslant a_J\) and use the centered one-field estimate at that depth. The origin is in the margin of width \(3 a_j\) of its \(J\)-chamber. The centered comparison estimate embeds the fine test into this chamber and bounds its root test there. Sum these bounds over the finite spin frames. For each fixed \(q\), intersect their finitely many late sets with those for the origin approximations. We obtain, for every cell, chamber-interior fields \(X_v, Y_v\) satisfying

\(\displaystyle \rho (X_v) \leqslant A_v, \qquad \rho (Y_v - X_v) \leqslant \varepsilon_q, \qquad |\mathcal{C}_i Y_v | \leqslant M_{v, q} .\)

The copies of \(X_v\) are exactly the physical fields. The prescription for \(Y_v\) depends only on the label, accuracy and cell frame, and thus works simultaneously for arbitrary finite layouts.

For a word \(v_1, \ldots, v_n\), set \(M = 1 + \sum_i A_{v_i}\). Since \(\varepsilon_q \leqslant 1\), both \(\rho (X_{v_i})\) and \(\rho (Y_{v_i})\) are at most \(M\). For any injective layout in the actual finite torus, Lemma 4.36 gives

\(\displaystyle \left| \mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_i \mathcal{C}_{x_i} Y_{v_i} -\mathbb{E}_{N, p} \prod_i \mathcal{C}_{x_i} X_{v_i} \right| \leqslant n \varepsilon_q M^{n - 1} .\)

The new word is real, measurable and bounded by \(\prod_i M_{v_i, q}\). This bound holds even for colliding layouts; only the error estimate needs injectivity. An empty word has error zero.

Finally consider two fixed injective integer-grid layouts. Translate their copies numbered \(i < j\) by \(ti (1, 1, 1, 1)\) and \(tj (1, 1, 1, 1)\). For all sufficiently large \(t\), their first coordinates separate, so their union is injective. For each such fixed \(t\), eventual growth of the period makes the union injective on the torus as well. Apply the preceding word estimate to the concatenated labels. Taking real parts gives the required comparison with the real part of the whole complex product, since \(| \text{Re} \hspace{0.17em} z| \leqslant |z|\) and integration commutes with real parts. The order of choices is accuracy, pair of copy numbers, sufficiently large separation, then the original late cutoff/period limit.

Apply this construction to each of the four pairs of plain and reflected words. The difference between their two bounded real approximants has supremum bound equal to the sum of their individual bounds. Expanding the product of two such differences gives four terms, with total error at most \(\varepsilon_q\) times the sum of the four accuracy-independent word constants. This tends to zero. Theorem 4.40 therefore applies on the same extraction order and the same coarse grid. This supplies its local approximation and separated-pair inputs from the paid selected history, rather than assuming those approximations as an additional phase hypothesis.

13.12A fixed-coupling history for the rank comparison

The rank tests compare the interacting fields with a free field over a short interval of scales. They require an auxiliary integration history. The coupling is held fixed on that interval, and the local additive remainder is controlled in value. We give the density construction and its bounds here. The calibrated history constructed earlier supplies the endpoint and the kernels; it is still the history defining the fields.

13.12.1The additive remainder and its exact density factor

On a finite torus \(\Lambda\), write a representation's density as

\(\displaystyle R (U) = \exp \{v| \Lambda | - A (U) - E (U)\} \hspace{0.17em} G (U), \qquad E (U) = \sum_{\alpha} E_{\alpha} (U) .\)

Here \(A\) is its path action, \(G\) its forced gas with the prescribed supports, inventories and eligibility tests, and \(E_{\alpha}\) are real, measurable, gauge-invariant local functions: their values agree when the local links and the relevant final flag data agree. Each \(E_{\alpha}\) vanishes unless its support \(X_{\alpha}\) is healthy at the representation's threshold. Give \(X_{\alpha}\) an anchor \(a_{\alpha} \in X_{\alpha}\). Suppose \(b_{\alpha} \geqslant 0\) majorizes \(|E_{\alpha} |\) and, for the representation's support weight \(M (X) \geqslant 1\),

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\alpha : a_{\alpha} = x} M (X_{\alpha}) b_{\alpha} \leqslant q \quad (x \in \Lambda) .\) (13.37)

The sums in this inequality are assumed summable. Since the anchor set is finite, summing it gives

\(\displaystyle \sum_{\alpha} |E_{\alpha} (U) | \leqslant | \Lambda | q.\)

Thus the sum defining \(E\) converges absolutely, is measurable and gauge invariant, and \(e^{- E}\) has the finite-volume bound \(e^{| \Lambda | q}\). Let \(R^0\) be obtained by deleting just the additive terms. Its paths, coupling, scalar, forced activities and their tests are unchanged. Then

\(\displaystyle R = e^{- E} R^0 .\) (13.38)

For any finite family of source variables, the same identity holds for its polynomial-valued density. Consequently the integrability of that density follows from the corresponding envelope for \(R^0\). The estimate \(e^{| \Lambda | q}\) is used only for finite-volume integrability; it is not used as a uniform bound in the volume limit.

13.12.2A local multiplier keeps the expansion on the actual density

Here is the local operation behind the construction. For a masked additive term \(E_{\alpha}\), insert the letter

\(\displaystyle h_{\alpha} = e^{- E_{\alpha}} - 1.\)

The identity \(\prod_{\alpha} (1 + h_{\alpha}) = e^{- E}\) is exact. Its infinite version follows from absolute summability, since \(|e^{- t} - 1| \leqslant e^{|t|} |t|\) and \(\sum_{\alpha} b_{\alpha} < \infty\). If the weighted footprint estimate for these letters has allowance \(\omega\), attach them to the same component alphabet as the fluctuation integral. The component estimate of Subsection 8.2 and the reassembly identity of Subsection 8.29 apply with this enlarged alphabet. The old forced labels and their eligibility conditions are retained. In particular the resulting identity integrates \(e^{- E} R^0 = R\). This construction cannot be replaced by integrating \(R^0\) alone.

For later use, denote by \(\delta\) the weighted allowance of the unmarked coarse letters, including the multiplier letters. The regular-gas estimate of Subsection 8.30, at the purchased support exponent, supplies a masked logarithmic family \(\ell_{\gamma}\) with anchored cost at most \(16 \delta\), and

\(\displaystyle G_{\text{reg}} (U) = \exp \{- \sum_{\gamma} \ell_{\gamma} (U)\} .\) (13.39)

The elementary numerical conditions used at this step include

\(\displaystyle 16 \delta \leqslant 1, \qquad e^{2 \delta} - 1 \leqslant 1 - e^{- 16 \delta}, \qquad 16 \delta \hspace{0.17em} \mathcal{R} (B) \leqslant 1.\)

Here \(\mathcal{R} (B)\) is the connected-support counting constant in the regular logarithm bound, and \(B\) is the coarse support radius. They are imposed on the same letter bounds, not on a different auxiliary normalization. If the integrand has holes around marked sites, the forced-exclusion identity gives the corresponding localized logarithms with those holes. The scalar identity without holes and these identities with holes therefore have the same normalization.

Choose the new coarse scalar from the positive fluctuation prefactor. If the prefactor is \(C > 0\), its contribution to the scalar per coarse site is \(| \Lambda_c |^{- 1} \log C\). Positivity follows from the positive coupling, the positive chart ratio, and the positive determinant of the flat precision. In particular this logarithm does not choose a branch. Let \(A_c\) be the retained coarse path action and \(\beta B_c\) the reference action used in the fluctuation formula. Its local correction is \(\beta B_c - A_c\). Add its masked local pieces to \(\ell_{\gamma}\). If the former have anchored cost \(\epsilon\), the combined family has cost

\(\displaystyle \epsilon + 16 \delta .\) (13.40)

Indeed the two nonnegative majorant sums add at every anchor. The identity for its exponential is exactly

\(\displaystyle e^{v_c | \Lambda_c | - \beta B_c} \hspace{0.17em} G_{\text{reg}} = e^{v_c | \Lambda_c | - A_c - E_c} .\)

Choose the coarse forced activities by the addresswise construction of Proposition 14.2: attach the hole and boundary factors to the unmarked forced components, sum at each full support and flag inventory, and store those sums as the output's archived activities. The regular/forced decomposition of the same expansion then gives

\(\displaystyle R_c =\mathcal{K}_{\beta} R_f\) (13.41)

on every coarse configuration, including configurations with flags. To check this last assertion, first keep the source polynomial in the finite expansion. Its coefficient of the empty inventory equals the unmarked scalar density. The reassembly and exclusion identities hold coefficientwise and preserve exact flag coverage. Setting the source family empty therefore identifies their scalar coefficients on every configuration. There is no division by a density or by a forced activity in this argument.

13.12.3Symmetrizing the additive family

The newly obtained sum \(E_c\) is invariant under signed lattice motions, but an individual choice of anchored pieces need not have a symmetric indexing. Let \(H\) be the finite group of these motions on the torus. Replace a piece \(f_{\alpha}\) by the family

\(\displaystyle |H|^{- 1} f_{\alpha} (gU), \qquad (g, \alpha) \in H \times \mathcal{I},\)

with the correspondingly transported support and anchor. For fixed \(g\), the anchored bound is the old bound at the inverse image of the anchor. Multiplication by \(|H|^{- 1}\) and summation over \(g\) preserve that bound. Absolute summability permits interchange of the finite group sum and the piece sum. Since the total was invariant, the new total is \(|H|^{- 1} \sum_g E_c (gU) = E_c (U)\). Left multiplication in \(H\) permutes the new indices, giving the required covariance of individual addresses. Reindex this countable family and, if necessary, add zero terms. The gas and all forced addresses are unchanged. This produces the locality, measurability and motion properties together with (13.41).

13.12.4The cost over a short interval

Fix a block ratio \(L > 1\) and a width parameter \(b \geqslant 0\). For large \(s\) put

\(\displaystyle y = \log \log s, \quad A_s = e^{y^3}, \quad q_n = s^{- 1 / 200} A_s^{4 n}, \quad \omega_n = q_n A_s, \quad \delta_n = q_n A_s^3 .\)

The two parts of the comparison interval have lengths \(h_1, h_2\). Their definitions are the ceilings

\(\displaystyle h_1 = \left\lceil \frac{(b + 2) y^2}{\log L} \right\rceil, \qquad h_2 = \left\lceil \frac{\log (M_s^{10})}{\log L} \right\rceil, \qquad h = h_1 + h_2,\)

where \(M_s = \lceil (\log s)^2 \rceil\); the support wall is \(M_s^2\). The support-radius estimates give \(\log (M_s^{10}) \leqslant y^2\) eventually. Thus \(h \leqslant C_{L, b} y^2 + 2 \leqslant y^4\) eventually. For every fixed integer \(r \geqslant 0\), uniformly for \(0 \leqslant n \leqslant h\),

\(\displaystyle q_n A_s^r \leqslant s^{- 1 / 400}\) (13.42)

for all sufficiently large \(s\). In fact its logarithm is at most \(- (\log s) / 200 + (4 y^4 + r) y^3\). For \(y \geqslant 1\) the positive term is at most \((4 + r) y^7\). Since \(y^7 e^{- y} \to 0\) and \(\log s = e^y\), it is at most \((\log s) / 400\) eventually, proving the claim.

The footprint bound of an additive input with cost \(q_n\) is at most

\(\displaystyle C_{L, a, t} (42 B + 1)^4 q_n,\)

where the support reserve \(t\) is strictly positive, \(a\) is the fixed support exponent, and \(B \leqslant M_s^2\). All factors except the displayed radius and \(q_n\) are fixed before \(s\) is increased. The wall estimates imply \(C_{L, a, t} (42 M_s^2 + 1)^4 \leqslant A_s\) eventually. Hence the multiplier letters cost at most \(\omega_n\). The remaining unmarked letters have their purchased local bounds; their combined logarithm is allowed cost \(16 \delta_n\). The path correction costs at most \(s^{- 1 / 200}\). These bounds close the recurrence because, as soon as \(A_s \geqslant 17\),

\(\displaystyle s^{- 1 / 200} + 16 \delta_n \leqslant q_{n + 1} .\) (13.43)

Indeed \(s^{- 1 / 200} \leqslant q_n \leqslant \delta_n\) and \(q_{n + 1} = A_s \delta_n\).

For clarity, the small exponential conditions also hold uniformly through this interval. If \(0 \leqslant t \leqslant 1\), then \(e^t - 1 \leqslant 2 t\). Consequently \(e^{2 \delta_n} - 1 \leqslant 4 \delta_n\). If \(0 \leqslant \delta_n \leqslant 1 / 16\), the inequalities \(e^{16 \delta_n} \geqslant 1 + 16 \delta_n\) and \((1 + 16 \delta_n)^{- 1} \leqslant 1 - 4 \delta_n\) give

\(\displaystyle e^{2 \delta_n} - 1 \leqslant 1 - e^{- 16 \delta_n} .\)

The second rational inequality follows after multiplying by its positive denominator: \(12 \delta_n - 64 \delta_n^2 \geqslant 0\). The factors \(\mathcal{R} (B)\), the insertion inflation and any fixed comparison polynomial have at most the purchased powers of \(e^{y^2}\); multiplying (13.42) by such factors still gives zero in the limit. The forced-gas allowance is smaller than every negative power of \(s\), uniformly for the local scale parameter in \([s / 2, s]\). It therefore pays the support-counting factors in the normalization-with-holes estimates. All these are eventual conditions after \(L, b\) and the finite menu have been fixed.

13.12.5Re-expressing the starting density

We first supply the stronger forced allowance at the starting level. This is a change of presentation on one torus, before applying a blocking kernel. Write \(c_{\text{lin}}\) and \(c_{\text{dir}}\) for the two positive coefficients of its clip; the clip condition includes \(c_{\text{lin}} < c_{\text{dir}} / 16\). Fix a positive path rate \(\sigma\), a path coefficient bound \(C\), and a support exponent \(a > 0\). All these parameters are fixed before the scale \(s\) tends to infinity. In particular, each quadratic path row has \(\sum_j |c_j | e^{\sigma \hspace{0.17em} \text{length}_j} \leqslant C\); each cubic and quartic atom family has the corresponding absolute true-length coefficient sum at most \(C\). The loop family has that bound with \(\Gamma_s\) in place of \(C\). The forced gas uses its full support and inventory addresses with the original eligibility and exclusion tests; its stated allowance is an absolute weighted site-sum bound. Put

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{c} H_s = \exp ((\log \log s)^2), \quad h_s = \exp ((\log \log s)^{3 / 2}),\\ t = s^{- 1 / 2} H_s, \quad \tau = s^{- 1 / 2} h_s, \quad M = \lceil (\log s)^2 \rceil, \quad B = M^2 . \end{array}\)

The old budget has radius \(M\) and threshold \(t\); the new one has radius \(B\) and threshold \(\tau\). Their support exponent and derivative order are unchanged. Denote the corresponding masked additive sums by \(E_t, E_{\tau}\) and path actions by \(A_t, A_{\tau}\).

Proposition 13.38. (Exact endpoint re-expression) Suppose the original endpoint has coupling \(s / 2 \leqslant \beta \leqslant 2 s\), the fixed path and clip bounds above, quadratic-loop coefficient bound \(\Gamma_s \leqslant 2 H_s\), additive allowance \(s^{- 11 / 10}\), and forced allowance \(w_s = \exp (- H_s^{1 / 5})\). Fix \(0 < a_0 \leqslant c_{\text{lin}} / 20\). For all sufficiently large \(s\), uniformly in the period and endpoint field, there is a presentation of exactly the same density with the same path, clip, coupling and scalar, budget \((B, \tau)\), additive allowance at most \(s^{- 11 / 10}\), and forced allowance

\(\displaystyle G (s) = e^{- a_0 h_s^2} .\)

It retains the original additive coefficients with their new masks. In particular its additive value allowance is at most \(q_0 = s^{- 1 / 200}\), and deleting those coefficients gives the neutral incoming law needed by the local compiler.

Proof. We construct all factors before grouping them. A flagged site at threshold \(\tau\) is a site touched by a plaquette whose chord exceeds \(\tau\). Write \(F_{\tau} (U)\) for this set. Eventually \(2 \tau \leqslant t\). For a plaquette chord \(r\) put

\(\displaystyle d_t (r) = \left\{ \begin{array}{ll} c_{\text{dir}} r^2 / 2, & r \leqslant t,\\ c_{\text{lin}} tr, & r > t, \end{array} \right. \qquad \Delta (r) = d_t (r) - d_{\tau} (r) .\)

Then \(\Delta \geqslant 0\), and \(\Delta (r) \geqslant c_{\text{lin}} \tau^2\) for \(r > \tau\). On \(\tau < r \leqslant t\) this follows from \(\Delta (r) = c_{\text{dir}} r^2 / 2 - c_{\text{lin}} \tau r \geqslant (c_{\text{dir}} / 2 - c_{\text{lin}}) r^2 \geqslant c_{\text{lin}} \tau^2\); on \(r > t\) use \(t - \tau \geqslant \tau\). Distribute \(\Delta (r)\) equally among the sites touched by its plaquette. There are at most five such sites, including on a wrapping torus. The resulting local charges \(d_x\) satisfy

\(\displaystyle \sum_{x \in F_{\tau}} d_x = \sum_{\text{plaquettes } p} \Delta (r_p), \qquad d_x \geqslant c_{\text{lin}} \tau^2 / 5 \quad (x \in F_{\tau}) .\)

A plaquette with nonzero charge has all its touched sites in \(F_{\tau}\), which proves the equality without discarding charges at healthy sites.

The other quadratic contribution to \(A_t - A_{\tau}\) is the squared path row on the event that the old kept test holds and the new one fails. Truncate the row at true path length \(B\), before squaring, and call its resulting nonnegative half-square \(b_x\) at anchor \(x\), including that event indicator and the signed-channel average. The kept test at threshold \(\tau\) reads only a radius-\(B\) neighborhood once \(2 \leqslant \tau \exp (B / c_{\text{relax}})\): beyond that neighborhood the chord bound two makes its test automatic. Thus \(b_x\) reads at most a radius-\(2 B\) neighborhood, and vanishes if the radius-\(B\) neighborhood is \(\tau\)-healthy. Distribute \(b_x\) equally among the sites of \(F_{\tau}\) in that neighborhood. If there are none, \(b_x = 0\) and no division is made. Summing these shares gives nonnegative charges \(b'_z\), with \(\sum_z b'_z = \sum_x b_x\), supported within radius \(4 B\) of \(z\). Perform the same operation for each signed channel with its original averaging weight.

Set \(u = a_0 h_s^2\) and, at a flagged site, set

\(\displaystyle X_z (U) = \exp [- \beta (d_z (U) + b'_z (U))] ;\)

set \(X_z = 0\) when the site is unflagged. Since \(\beta \tau^2 \geqslant h_s^2 / 2\) and \(a_0 \leqslant c_{\text{lin}} / 20\),

\(\displaystyle |X_z | \leqslant e^{- 2 u}, \qquad \prod_{z \in F_{\tau}} X_z = \exp [- \beta \sum_z (d_z + b'_z)] .\) (13.44)

In particular each selected site factor supplies one factor \(e^{- u}\) which can be reserved for its connected component.

Here are the remaining exponent changes and their bounds. Write

\(\displaystyle D = - (A_t - A_{\tau}) + \beta \sum_z (d_z + b'_z) + (E_{\tau} - E_t) .\)

This is the sum of the omitted quadratic row pairs, the differences of the cubic, quartic and loop masks, and the difference of the additive masks. Each summand has a nonempty full read support and vanishes when that support is \(\tau\)-healthy. Their absolute site bound at any fixed enlarged exponent \(a + 2\) is bounded by

\(\displaystyle \epsilon_s \leqslant C_1 B^{C_2} [\beta C^2 e^{- \sigma B / 4} + \Gamma_s t^2 + \beta C (t^3 + t^4) + s^{- 11 / 10}] = s^{- 1 / 2 + o (1)} .\) (13.45)

The constants depend only on the fixed profile and exponent. To verify this bound, expand the difference of a full square and its truncated square as ordered pairs of path labels, with at least one length exceeding \(B\). Extract \(e^{- \sigma B / 4}\) from the two absolute exponential row sums after paying their full path supports, as in (8.182). The kept-test support adds a radius-\(2 B\) halo, whose cover cost is fixed. The difference of two nested masks has absolute value at most the old masked atom bound; for an order-\(j\) atom this is its coefficient times \(t^j\). The full path support has \(B\)-cover at most \(1 + 2 j + \text{length} / B\), so \((a + 2) / B \leqslant \sigma / 2\) eventually leaves a positive length reserve. For an old additive support \(Y\), the connected-cover construction gives

\(\displaystyle c_B (\text{halo}_8 \hspace{0.17em} Y) \leqslant C_3 + C_3 (M / B) c_M (Y) .\)

Take \(s\) large enough that \(C_3 (a + 3) M / B \leqslant a\). This converts its old weighted bound to the new one. Converting anchors to site hits costs a polynomial in \(B\), by the shell sum in Subsection 8.24, with the extra unit of cover exponent reserved for this conversion. The same calculation bounds the halo-enlarged old forced labels by \(C_1 B^{C_2} w_s\). These arguments retain all label multiplicities and true path lengths. Finally \(H_s = s^{o (1)}\), giving the last equality in (13.45).

Coalesce the summands of \(D\) with the same full support, giving \(D_Y\). There are finitely many supports on this torus, and the coalescing is absolutely convergent by the site bound. For large \(s\), each \(|D_Y | \leqslant \epsilon_s \leqslant 1 / 4\). Put \(W_Y = e^{D_Y} - 1\). Then

\(\displaystyle e^D = \prod_Y (1 + W_Y), \qquad |W_Y | \leqslant 2 |D_Y |,\)

and \(W_Y = 0\) on a \(\tau\)-healthy support. Combining with (13.44) gives the exact identity

\(\displaystyle e^{- A_t - E_t} = e^{- A_{\tau} - E_{\tau}} \prod_Y (1 + W_Y) \prod_{z \in F_{\tau}} X_z .\) (13.46)

Multiply by the original forced gas and expand the optional factors. Enlarge old forced supports by eight sites to read their original eligibility tests; retain those tests and their original mutual exclusions inside every term. Form connected components using intersections of these enlarged supports, the \(Y\)'s and the radius-\(4 B\) supports of \(X_z\). Every nonzero component contains a selected \(X_z\): a nonzero \(W_Y\) meets a new flag, and an old forced label contains an old flag, which is also a new flag. The factor for that flag has intersecting support and is present in the product. Record as the new inventory exactly the selected flag sites in each component, requiring it to equal \(F_{\tau}\) on that component's support. Require also that the union of old inventories is exactly the set of old flags on this component's support, and retain each old label's eligibility test. The components then have disjoint supports and cover all new flags. Conversely their constituents recover the original compatible forced collection and optional subset uniquely. This proves equality of the two gas sums, first for finite label families. The old absolute site bound, the finite-volume sum of the optional bounds, and the connected majorants below permit passage to countable families by absolute convergence.

After reserving \(e^{- u}\) per selected site factor, the combined weighted site bound is at most

\(\displaystyle \rho_s = 2 \epsilon_s + C_1 B^{C_2} w_s + (8 B + 1)^4 e^{(a + 1) 9^4} e^{- u} .\)

Indeed a radius-\(4 B\) ball has \(B\)-cover at most \(9^4\), and at most \((8 B + 1)^4\) of these balls contain a given site. Here \(\rho_s = s^{- 1 / 2 + o (1)}\), so \((2 B + 1)^4 \rho_s \leqslant 1\) and \(e^{\rho_s} - 1 \leqslant 1\) eventually. The rooted connected-set estimate proved in Subsection 14.4, using one unit of support reserve, bounds the resulting forced site sum by \(e^{- u} (e^{\rho_s} - 1) \leqslant e^{- u}\). No enumeration of possible flag subsets costs an additional factor: the selected site labels already specify the inventory, and its equality test only removes terms. Sum the resulting majorants and values at each full support and inventory address to obtain the new forced activities.

All constructions preserve locality, measurability and gauge symmetry. Signed motions permute their labels; averaging the original channel convention, or reindexing each full orbit, preserves the bounds. The old regular coefficients need no replacement. Their derivative bounds restrict to the smaller healthy domain and smaller allowed coordinate directions, while increasing the cover radius decreases its weight. They therefore retain their old additive allowance. Equation 13.46 and the gas bijection prove exact equality of densities with unchanged scalar and coupling. Deleting the additive coefficients leaves the same path and forced bounds and zero additive cost, proving the final assertion.\(\Box\)

13.12.6Joining to the endpoint
